## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

* Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-references to annexes, including a table of contents, budget sheet, etc.
* Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the main body of the proposal. Any discrepancies will be noted and must be reflected in all relevant documents.
* The total length for each funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 50 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service level.
* The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals shall be considered public documents from submission onwards with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities are required to provide a summary disclosure statement at page 4.

### A.2.1 Executive Summary

#### Climate Change Problem

- Rice is Thailand’s most important food crop, accounting for roughly half of Thai agricultural land and employing 18 million smallholder farmers.
- Rice farmers are among the poorest occupational groups in Thailand.
- Rice farming is a temperature-sensitive activity conducted in a climate that is becoming increasingly characterized by longer dry seasons.

#### At the same time

- Rice farming is also a significant contributor to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, mainly through methane (CH4) generated in flooded paddy fields but also carbon dioxide (CO2) from agricultural burning and nitrous oxide (N2O).
- Rice production accounts for approximately 3.5% of total anthropogenic GHG emissions and 10% of agricultural emissions.
- Thailand is the world’s second-largest rice exporter, with a significant portion of its rice exports destined to Europe.

#### Rice farmers’ awareness

- About 80% of farmers think that rainfall patterns are changing and that rice yields are suffering as a result. Smallholder farmers have limited capacity to adapt given their lack of access to information, technology, credit, inputs, markets, and credit.
- Furthermore, women smallholder farmers face disproportionate challenges in adapting to climate change because they often do not participate in agriculture (for example, with regard to technology access) or are generally not recognized or addressed.

#### Aspects of low-emission and/or climate-resilient rice farming

- Low-emission and/or climate-resilient rice farming has been identified and selectively rolled out by the government and other stakeholders through a variety of projects in Thailand.
- These aspects include water management, soil fertility, and cross-cutting context. From the perspective of agricultural service providers, investment in climate-smart technologies and practices is often not financially viable for smallholder farmers (particularly women smallholders) and the uncertain market size demand for climate-smart services, particularly compared to existing well-established and well-understood rice care service provision sector.

#### Proposed Interventions

- The Thai Rice Project will enable rice farmers – including women farmers – in addition to adapting to a changing climate while simultaneously reducing GHG emissions.
- The project design follows a bottom-up logic: behavioral changes will be triggered at the level of the farmer, from which will emerge demand for and adoption of climate-smart technologies, which will be made available by service providers as a result of their project technical and financial support. The climate-smart rice that is produced will be marketed to the private sector, with the aim of increasing farmers’ income while straw residues will be monetized.
- The Thai Agricultural Rural for Sustainable Rice (TARS) will set up a Climate Smart Agriculture Scheme (CSAS) in collaboration with the corresponding international standards and systems. Building on an existing – albeit small – domestic policy framework, the CSAS will be designed to provide a market-based approach to climate-smart rice production.
- The CSAS will be designed and implemented to unlock additional revenue flows. Policy makers will be supported in developing a national policy framework for climate-smart agriculture that coordinates investments from the public and private sectors to achieve a strengthened and more joined-up institutional framework for climate smart rice.

#### Climate Results / Benefits

- The combination of these project interventions will lead to a paradigm shift in how rice farming is conducted: main environmental benefits are expected through reduced GHG emissions, increased productivity supported by capable, well-equipped service providers. As a result, yields and livelihoods will improve.
- The Rice Scheme will enable the rice sector to generate ongoing economic value from their emission reductions, while the women smallholder farmers in the region will benefit from the project’s experiences through knowledge sharing and peer-to-peer learning.

#### Project Expectations

- The project is expected to reduce 12.56 MTCO2e during its 15-year lifespan, at a mitigation cost to the GCF of Euro 3.12/CO2e, and to build the climate resilience of approximately 253,400 smallholder rice farmers.

B.1 Climate context

### B.1.1 Strategic context

#### Rice Production
Agriculture plays an important role in Thailand’s economy, contributing 8% of gross domestic product (GDP) and employing approximately one-third of the labor force. It occupies roughly half of Thai agricultural land and employs 18 million farmers. Roughly 40% of labour in Thai agriculture is female.

#### Rice as a Staple Food
Rice is Thailand’s main staple food and it is also an important export commodity. Of the rice that is consumed domestically, approximately one-third is used as inputs into other sectors, including animal feed, livestock production, and processed foods. The remaining two-thirds of domestic consumption is sold loose in traditional rice stores or pre-packed in bags.

#### Major Rice Seasons
The major crop (i.e., wet season rice) is grown from May to October, except in the South, where the growing season is from mid-June to February of the following year. Almost three-quarters of Thailand’s annual rice production comes from the wet season crop, typically grown in the dry season between November and April, and from March to mid-June in the South.

#### Rice Growing Regions
Rice is grown in all provinces of the country. Approximately three-quarters of Thai rice is rain-fed and one-quarter is irrigated. The majority of rain-fed rice is produced in the northern and northeastern regions—the so-called rice bowl of Thailand. The majority of farmers in the Central Plains have access to irrigation, but only a small proportion of this area is under irrigated conditions.

#### Rice Growing Conditions
Almost three-quarters of the dry season rice grown under irrigated conditions is located in the Central Plains. Most of the remaining dry season rice is rain-fed and produced in the Northeast.

#### Altitude and Irrigation
Situated at 60-200m above sea level, with undulating topography where irrigation is difficult, the North-East, in contrast, relies primarily upon rain-fed or rain-fed mixed rain-fed irrigated rice farming. The average size of farms in this region is smaller than those found elsewhere.

#### Rice Varieties
Eastern rice is Hom Mali (the brand name for Thai jasmine rice), which is exported to Europe and North America.

#### Rice Farming Characteristics
Rice farming in Thailand is characterized as high risk but low return. The sector is very fragmented. Most rice is grown on farms smaller than 2 hectares by smaller farmers with limited access to credit.

### 7. While there is a lack of systematically collected sex-disaggregated data and gender analysis on Thai rice farmers, it is believed that women account for only 27% of agrarian land holders in Thailand. The majority of these women are smallholder farmers who are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts as they play a large role in rural economies and are heavily involved in agricultural activities. Women’s limited access to resources and decision-making power within the household, coupled with their lack of education and training, makes them more susceptible to climate change adaptation challenges.

Yields of in-season and off-season rice - averaging between 461 kg/ha and 573 kg/ha respectively, significantly lower than those in Viet Nam (490 kg/ha) and Indonesia (34% higher). Rice yields are also negatively impacted by climate change as they play a large role in rural economies and are heavily involved in agricultural activities. Women’s limited access to resources and decision-making power within the household, coupled with their lack of education and training, makes them more susceptible to climate change adaptation challenges.

### 8. Smallholder farmers are typically organized into so-called mega farms - groups of approximately 30-150 farmers who have joined together in order to increase their production capacity and improve their livelihoods – most are less than 5 years old - that are being promoted by the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MAC). The model serves as a second pillar of the extension support infrastructure, focused primarily on providing farmers with access to new technologies and market linkages. This model serves to reduce production costs, improve yields and build the technical knowledge base of smallholder farmers.

Farmers are also grouped in Water Usage Organisations (WUOs) - farmer groups that are organised and supported by the government’s Royal Irrigation Department (RID). WUOs can take a number of forms:

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE 10

## B

### B.1.2 Baseline Scenario

**Rice Farming is Impacted by Climate Change and Contributes to Climate Change**

- Rice farming in Thailand, and associated agricultural livelihoods in rice-growing regions, is highly vulnerable and exposed to the impacts of climate change.
- At the same time, rice farming is a significant contributor to greenhouse gas emissions, including carbon dioxide (CO2) from agricultural burning practices and nitrous oxide (N2O) from nitrogenous fertilizers.

#### Climate Vulnerability of Rice Farming

1. Thailand is already experiencing the effects of climate change:
   - According to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), the mean land surface air temperature in South-East Asia has increased by approximately 0.5°C per decade since 1976.
   - The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events have increased, with more frequent heavy rainfall events during the wet season and a corresponding reduction in the number of cold days and cool nights.

2. Mean annual precipitation varies across the country and shows considerable short-term variability:
   - Correlated with the El Niño Southern Oscillation and the Pacific Decadal Oscillation.
   - A general trend towards increased precipitation has been observed, particularly in the north and northeast regions.
   - In contrast, there have been reductions in the east and south, accompanied by accelerated seasonality: wetter wet seasons and drier dry seasons.

3. The frequency of tropical cyclones:
   - Has declined from 50 to 75 per decade since 1982.
   - The number of tropical storms has decreased from 30 to 45.
   - Typhoons have become less frequent, with the number decreasing from 1.3 to 5.9 days per decade.

4. Average daily rainfall intensity has also increased by 0.24–0.73 mm/day over the past three decades.
   - This has led to more intense and longer-lasting rainfall events during the wet season and an increased occurrence of drought events during the dry season.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROGRAM V10.0 | PAGE 11

## 15. According to climate models developed locally[2], the following future changes in the climate are projected[3][4]:

- **Increase in maximum and minimum temperature**: Average temperatures are predicted to increase by 1.7°C under RCP 8.5 (by 2059 compared to the baseline of General Circulation Models [GCMs] (1976–2005)) under RCP 4.5, and by 3.8°C under RCP 8.5 by 2059 compared to the baseline of General Circulation Models (GCMs) (1976-2005) under RCP 4.5.
- **The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events**: The frequency of heavy precipitation will increase, with an average annual increase in rainfall between (RCP) 2.6 by 1.3 to 2.3°C under RCP 4.5 and by 2.3 to 7°C under RCP 8.5.
- **The frequency and severity of floods**: The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events will increase, with an average annual increase in rainfall between (RCP) 2.6 by 1.3 to 2.3°C under RCP 4.5 and by 2.3 to 7°C under RCP 8.5.
- **The frequency and severity of droughts**: The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events will increase, with an average annual increase in rainfall between (RCP) 2.6 by 1.3 to 2.3°C under RCP 4.5 and by 2.3 to 7°C under RCP 8.5.

## Conversely, the frequency and severity of droughts are also expected to increase[5].

### Figure 1: Areas Projected to Face Drought Hazards[1]

#### 16. Modeling commissioned for the preparation of this Funding Proposal (Annex 2c) comes to similar conclusions as those in the previous study[2]. The model simulations were conducted using data compiled from 11 weather stations in rice-producing project areas in Central, North and North-East Thailand. These include daily maximum temperature, minimum temperature, precipitation amount, heat wave frequency index (HWF), etc., and composite indicators i.e. the standard precipitation evapotranspiration ratio (SPEI) and standardized precipitation index (SPI). The model used is the RCM2 methodology, using ENSO-Land as a reference data set, was used to interpolate missing values. Analysis of the results showed that the model simulations were able to capture the patterns in dry seasons. The statistical significance assessment technique (CRU) and ERA5[3] demonstrated the results for each station were compared for validation.

#### 17. Figure 2 shows the comparison between two model simulations, CMIP6[4] and CORDEX[5]. Eight high-resolution Regional Climate Models (RCMs) from COREDX Core and 21 Global Climate Models (GCMs)[6] were used to generate climate projections for Thailand at three time horizons: near-term (2025-2049), mid-term (2030-2074) and long term (2075-2099) – based on 3 climate scenarios [Shared Socioeconomic Pathways (SSPs): SSP1-2.6, SSP4-2.6 and SSP5-8.5][7].

#### Key findings of the project modeling results and projections for future temperature show a clear upward trend (Figure 2).

## Impacts of Climate Change on Rice Yields

10. Rice yields have demonstrated considerable sensitivity to past climate change trends. Farmers consume 70% of the country's water supply and rice is especially water-intensive, consuming more energy than any other crop in Thailand. Each kilogramme of rice requires approximately 2-3 m³ of water per season and each kilogramme of paddy rice produced requires 2–3 m³ of irrigation water. In addition to this, the country's climate has become drier over time, with less rainfall available to fulfill the demands imposed by rice production. Even in a moderate drought, measured in terms of relative soil evaporation (RSE), yields can be reduced by up to 50%. Rice yield reductions are also associated with rice bioclimax by 25%, caused by stomatal closure which leaves the leaf CO₂/O₂ ratio, resulting in photosynthetic reduction and grain reductions in grain weight, panicle number per unit area, panicle length and grain number per plant.

20. In the drought of 2019, yields in the second growing season declined by 54%, leading to a fall in exports from Thailand. The government responded with an emergency rice import plan to protect domestic revenues. In the drought of 2020/21, the government effectively prohibited rice farming in some areas for not only water conservation but also to prevent further decline in export earnings. The Thai government also started to encourage rice farmers to diversify away from rice by planting non-rice crops, particularly in the north and northeast regions where there is more rainfall. The government's adaptation requirements for Thailand will include "the reduction of water usage and expansion of its storage capacity".

21. Rice yields are sensitive to the projected changes in climate parameters. As a result of speckled sterility and reduced accumulation of assimilates[36], rice grain yield declines when the average daily temperature exceeds 34°C for more than two consecutive days, which is expected under RCP 8.5 global level; each increase of one degree Celsius in mean temperature is expected to reduce the total rice yield by approximately 10%.[37] The Rice Yield and Climate Change Project[38] found that a temperature increase up of 2°C by 2059 under RCP 4.5 in the assessed area will reduce yields by about 6%.

22. In addition to sustained, incremental increases in temperature over the course of decades, rice yields are sensitive to transitory heatwaves. Heat exposure above ~34°C for as little as 1 hour can reduce grain yield by up to 7%.[39] Rice plants can recover from such heat stress within a few weeks even when water root uptake and transpiration are unaffected[40], and also applies to irrigated rice.

## Table B: Anticipated Impact of New-Term Climate Change on Rice Yields

23. Wetter wet seasons and drier dry seasons will have the effect of leaching mineralised nitrogen from the soil more rapidly, before the rice crop can utilise it; the efficacy of fertilizers will thereby be reduced. It is possible that this effect may be offset by increased rainfall in the wet season leading to higher soil moisture concentrations. However, this fertilization effect shows strong interaction with soil mineral nitrogen levels, with responses being greater where soil mineral nitrogen levels are low and/or where there has been a depletion of these levels due to changes in precipitation patterns; the CO2 fertilization effect is expected to weaken. Moreover, because of the increased rainfall during wet seasons, rice plants will be more likely to suffer from lodging and make use of the higher CO2 concentration. It is very probable, therefore, that positive effects on rice yields from higher CO2 concentrations will be less than offset by changes in other climate parameters, notably temperature and rainfall.

24. Modeling commissioned for the preparation of this funding proposal and undertaken by the International Rice Research Institute (IRRI) indicates that between 2015-2039, the effects of these climate change scenarios on rice yields will be negative in all three regions studied. Table B provides a summary of anticipated yield reductions, which average 1.0 tonnes per hectare in the dry season and 6.4 tonnes per hectare in the wet season.

## 25. Livelihoods of smallholder farmers are impacted by the impacts of climate change.

Changes in temperature, rainfall variability and intensity or frequency of extreme weather events directly affect farm yields, as well as their households' food security, income and well-being. Rice farmers' awareness of climate change is high, but they lack information on how to adapt. Smallholder farmers who are rice farmers are suffering as a result. But smallholder farmers have limited capacity to adapt, given their lower income levels, access to credit, and other financial constraints. They also face challenges in accessing markets and credit; and their often chronic dependence on external support[1]. Furthermore, women smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable because they lack the resources and skills needed for sustainable agriculture (for example, with regard to technology access) are generally not recognized or addressed.

## 26. The climate vulnerability assessment commissioned for the preparation of the Thai Rice Project estimates that smallholder rice farming households in Thailand's southern provinces are highly vulnerable according to the Climate Exposure Index (CEI)[2]. This vulnerability is expected to increase over time, particularly as a result of increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events.

### Figure 6: Small Administrative-Level Climate Exposure Index (CEI) in 24 Assessed Provinces Across Thailand[3]

## 27. Alternative livelihood options for smallholder farmers are limited. Rice farming is a water-intensive, temperature-sensitive activity in an environment that is projected to experience increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. As such, rice farming may become less viable over time; therefore, it would be simply discontinue rice farming. However, the scope for making such a switch, and the scale at which this could happen, are not clear. Rice farming remains an important livelihood in Thailand's economic and political fabric of Thailand[4]. Rice remains the dominant agricultural industry, accounting for 51% of the cultivated area and contributing 13% of agricultural GDP. Not only is rice still a vital food

One crop dominates but it is also a major export, second only to rubber in value. Some alternative crops are even more sensitive to climate change than rice, including sugarcane (and cassava) in Upland Thailand. This suggests that the rice sector will be particularly vulnerable to climate change, with significant capital and lack of knowledge about production and markets – that would need to be overcome.

26. Moreover, climate models are clear that the total volume of precipitation in Thailand is unlikely to decrease much over this century. However, there may well be more intense rainfall events, leading to increased flooding and increasing intensity of precipitation events – i.e., rains being more concentrated in time and less evenly distributed across space. This will have a significant impact on water availability for agriculture, as well as water storage, do, therefore, offer potential solutions to at least some of the projected water scarcity. These are not necessarily easy or cheap options but they are available. The rice sector is not the only proposition, but they do suggest that the rice sector can and will continue to play a prominent role in Thailand’s food security.

27. In fact, the rice sector has already been playing this role for some time now. It is also important for other rice-producing countries to generate a rice surplus. Thailand is well positioned, if climate change challenges are overcome, to be able to do so. The rice sector will continue to play an important role in Thailand’s food security and particularly important in the current context of rising global food prices and wheat shortages, and given that rice is more affordable for most people than has been the world’s population? and accounts for 10% of global calorie consumption.

**GHG Emissions from Rice Farming**

28. Globally, rice is the third-largest crop in terms of area harvested, after wheat and maize. Rice GHG production contributes approximately 2.6% to total anthropogenic GHG emissions and 19% of agricultural emissions. The rice sector generates ~30% of global agricultural methane emissions and ~15% of nitrous oxide (N₂O) emissions. Rice is the largest source of N₂O in Thailand, accounting for a global level, comparable to that of aviation, shipping or deforestation.

29. Despite its significance, the rice sector is often overlooked in GHG mitigation discussions*, even though it is one of the most important sectors in terms of land use and emissions from other agricultural sectors – its potential to offer immediate*, low-cost**, multi-gas* abatement opportunities that can simultaneously serve to

## The Global Methane Pledge (GMP), launched in November 2021, aims to catalyze action to reduce methane emissions.

### Agriculture accounts for approximately 15% of Thailand's total GHG emissions and is the second-largest emitter after energy. In 2022, rice cultivation produced 27 MTCO2e of methane emissions, which accounts for 51% of total GHG emissions from agriculture (Figure 2). Rice is Thailand’s fourth-largest agricultural export and a major food source not found in the world.

### In irrigated rice production, which is the predominant practice in the Central Plains of Thailand, flooding of paddy fields to provide optimal growing conditions for rice plants leads to the release of methane. These emissions are methanogenic bacteria. These bacteria perform well under anaerobic conditions and are

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 10.1.0 | PAGE 20

B

## Responsibilities for decomposing organic carbon and transforming it into methane through the process of methanogenesis. Methane is vertically transported to the atmosphere through three principal pathways: (i) diffusion from soil, (ii) emission from livestock manure management procedures, and (iii) plant transport by diffusion into the roots and conversion to gaseous methane in the rhizosphere. The magnitude of these emissions is exacerbated by established farming practices such as continuous irrigation and the addition of organic matter.

### 34. Besides CH₄, rice cultivation also emits nitrous oxide (N₂O). N₂O is emitted both by the soil and the rice plant, with the principal determinants of emissions being the nitrogen fertilizer application rate and the water regime.

#### 35. Finally, open-field burning of rice straw emits carbon dioxide (CO₂), CH₄, and N₂O and other air pollutants (such as particulate matter). Rice straw and stubble burning is a widespread practice among Thai farmers to clear land for subsequent crops or to prepare the soil for rice planting in preparation for disease management measures. The practice is not illegal; largely because of its prevalence, its negligible contribution to total emissions from agriculture has been overlooked by policy makers but the government has implemented a number of initiatives to try to reduce it. Approximately half of rice straw and stubble burning emissions are attributed to open-field burning of rice straw in Thailand (MICOxsys), as well as aerosol particles that contribute to the brown haze experienced in Thailand and Malaysia, which is associated with particulate matter and other air pollutants. These particles are potentially toxic, with possible links to the prevalence of asthma and the frequency of asthma attacks.

#### 36. Detailed measurement of GHG emissions from rice fields has been conducted by the Rice Department in the context of developing the Thai Rice Project (TRP). A detailed description is provided in Section B.1.4. As shown in Figure 8, the overwhelming source of GHG emissions from Thai rice farming is methane (CH₄), which accounts for approximately 90% of total emissions. Open-field burning (water pumping, harvesting machines, etc.) is the second largest source, followed by emissions from the manure management procedures – which are notably greater than the emissions generated by application of fertilizer to the rice plants.

#### 37. For the purpose of developing the Thai Rice Project, a detailed estimate of baseline GHG emissions in the context of the current practices and technologies is provided in Table B.14 (Section B.1.4). This estimate uses data collected from Thailand government agencies and public data sources, as well as local surveys conducted by the Rice Department. The estimates do not include direct on-farm (on-site) CO₂ emissions from biomass open burning in Thailand. 2018 Atmosphere: 9

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V10.0 | PAGE 22

## Thailand’s Updated Long-Term Low Greenhouse Gas Emission Development Strategy (LT-LEDS) (2020)

Thailand is committed to reducing its greenhouse gas emissions by 27% below business-as-usual (i.e., without policy interventions). The LT-LEDS projects that Thailand's GHG emissions will now be reduced by 31.5%. This strategy identifies the agricultural sector as a major source of emissions, after energy. In the agricultural sector, the LT-LEDS states that measures promoting more efficient water use and better land management are key to reducing emissions. Other important agricultural priorities are: manure management, agricultural waste management, improved rice cultivation and practices, and the introduction of alternative rice varieties – all of which are described below in Section 8.1.4, improved water efficiency, promotion of agroforestry systems, reduction of post-harvest losses, and better climate-resilient agricultural processing, climate insurance and early warning systems for planting planning (e.g., crop calendar) and other climate-related information services. The case study on rice production in Section 8.1.4 is presented as a showcase initiative. The LT-LEDS also states that the Government of Thailand is seeking to reduce its emissions by 27% below business-as-usual.

### Key Adaptation Sector: Water Resources Management

* **Guideline 2, Measure 9:** Develop support and compensation systems or insurance schemes for water-related disasters which are effective and easily accessible to the general public.

## Key Adaptation Sector: Agriculture and Food Security

- **Guideline 1 Measure 2:** Develop appropriate insurance schemes or risk insurance systems to cover climate-related risks, including crop failure, livestock disease exchange rates, and market risk management, such as facilitating access to financial resources and markets.
- **Guideline 1 Measure 3:** Promote the development of new agricultural practices in response to the changing climatic e.g. adjusting crop calendars to minimize damage from floods and droughts, and growing crops that are more resilient to climate change.
- **Guideline 1 Measure 5:** Promote the improvement of crop / livestock varieties to accommodate the changes in climate conditions, such as developing new crop varieties with higher yields or disease resistance, and breeding livestock varieties that are resilient to heat and drought, food diseases and pests, and salinity in soil and water.
- **Guideline 1 Measure 8:** Support network building for climate change adaptation in the agricultural sector by promoting the exchange of experiences on climate change adaptation among stakeholders including knowledge, technology, agronomic innovation, with linkages to the local context, by making connections between farmers, extension workers, researchers, policy makers and other relevant stakeholders, including academic, government agencies, local private companies, and small-scale farmers.
- **Guideline 1 Measure 9:** Enhance farmers' awareness and knowledge on climate change impacts on their livelihoods through training programs, workshops, seminars, and other activities that provide information and knowledge from academic research as well as local wisdom related to climate change adaptation. The program should also promote the exchange of experiences among stakeholders and the general public to enhance awareness; and by promoting network building by Type 1 and Type 2 projects, facilitating effective exchanges of experiences on climate change management and responses to climate change.
- **Guideline 1 Measure 10:** Promote the development of water resources management for agriculture in order to improve food security. The project should aim at improving flood-risk and drought-risk areas to be more effective, by planning water resources management for flood risk and drought risk.

### Thailand's STARFUND

#### Allocation under the eighth Global Environment Facility (GEF) funding cycle (GEF 8, 2020-2026) is US$ 19.1 million, of which the bulk of US$ 11.6 million or 57% is associated with the project on Sustainable Rice Landscapes in Thailand. The project will be implemented over a period of five years and aims to reduce land degradation. An important GEF-funding-based project: Inclusive Sustainable Rice Landscapes (ISRL) in Thailand, which was approved under the seventh funding cycle (GEF 7), with GIZ as an Executing Entity, was approved in the preceding funding cycle (GEF-7) and recently converted to a Type 1 project. For more information on Section B.3 for details. The Thai government is still planning any rice projects under GEF-8.

#### Thailand's Long-term Climate Change Strategy

The Thai Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MoNRE) has defined Thailand’s long-term climate change strategy in the National Adaptation Plan (2015-2040). The plan includes a five-year term policy framework focusing on climate adaptation, adaptation capacity building that informs the development of sectoral strategies. The plan also defines the criteria for defining extreme heat days (defined as having temperatures higher than 33°C) is expected to increase by 2.3 weeks per year, and the number of days with high precipitation will increase from an average of 105 days in 2014-2016 to 118 days in 2039-2041. The plan also defines criteria for defining extreme heat days (defined as having temperatures higher than 33°C) is expected to increase by 2.3 weeks per year, and the number of days with high precipitation will increase from an average of 105 days in 2014-2016 to 118 days in 2039-2041.

#### The Thailand V-TTS

The Thailand V-TTS is administered by the Thailand Greenhouse Gas Management Organisation (TGGMO) under the Department of Environment (DOE). The project was approved for funding (2015-2017), involving approximately 40 industrial facilities – was intended to pilot a Monitoring, Reporting and Verification (MRV) system in accordance with the Kyoto Protocol MRV guidelines. and design certain ETS features such as cap-setting and allowance allocation. Phase I of the project is now complete.

* The T-VER Scheme is a voluntary domestic GHG crediting mechanism introduced by TGO that covers CO₂, CH₄ and N₂O emissions. An associated scheme, Premium T-VERs was introduced in 2013 to provide an additional incentive for projects with high carbon intensity. The T-VER Scheme is aligned with the international Core Carbon Principles (CCPs) developed by the Integrity Council for the Voluntary Carbon Market (ICVCM). The T-VER Scheme uses a project-based approach, which means that of the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM) - development of a project design document (PDD) and submission to the CDM Executive Board. GHG emission reduction methodologies are based on those approved by the CDM. Like the CDM there is no requirement for projects to be registered with the T-VER Scheme. Instead, a number of small-scale mitigation interventions can be aggregated together as one project. However, T-VERs are only issued against verified emissions reductions from projects that have been registered through the T-VER Scheme which opened in September 2012. Through its Thailand Carbon Offsetting Programme (TCOP), TGO has developed a carbon offsetting mechanism for the aviation sector. The price of T-VERs varies across project types, with solar photovoltaics (PV) and hydropower T-VERs, for example, being priced at approximately 100 baht/CO₂e (~ €1.00/tonne CO₂e), while waste recovery T-VERs at approximately 40 baht/CO₂e (~ €0.40/tonne CO₂e).

* The Scheme is currently limited in size and scope, particularly in relation to agriculture. At the 24th regional meeting of the Parties to the Kyoto Protocol (KPM) in November 2013, Thailand announced further 12% focused on waste management. Only two projects are agricultural – fertilizer manufacturing and rice straw composting. The T-VER Scheme is expected to deliver annual emissions reductions of approximately 5 million tonnes CO₂e per year or around 4 million T-VERs, none of them from the agriculture sector.* An alternate wetting and drying (AWD) (see text box below) project in the rice sector could be a potential candidate for inclusion under methodology AMI III.0A. With appropriate market support measures – technical assistance, awareness raising and capacity building – it is estimated that the total emissions reductions of T-VER Scheme could potentially supply 27 million T-VERs by 2030.*

## B

### The Thai Rice Project is fully aligned with the draft Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MoAC) Climate Change Action Plan (CCAP) (2020-2037) and the National Adaptation Program of Actions (NAPA) relating to: adaptation (primarily, reducing yield losses due to climate change), mitigation (including increased carbon sequestration in soil and vegetation), increasing productivity (including through digital tools), farmers and communities, and operationalizing the Strategic Plan (including strengthening the capacity of the Department of Agriculture). The project is also aligned with the NDCs linked to the 2nd Updated NDC. The project is also aligned with MoAC’s Rice Department (RD) Policy in promoting sustainable rice production and the Thai Agricultural Standard (TAS) for the application of the Thai Agricultural Standard (TAS) certification (see below). The Thai Rice Strategy (2020-2037) is a comprehensive strategy to promote sustainable rice production. The strategy is divided into four sub-strategies, covering the international market, the domestic market, water resources and climate change adaptation. The Office of Natural Resources and Water Resources (ONWR) Master Plan on Water Management (2018-2037) specifically identifies climate change as a major challenge to Thailand’s water security and calls for increased efforts in water use efficiency in irrigated areas and constructing over 541,000 small dams to address future droughts.

### The Sustainable Rice Platform (SRP) Standard for Sustainable Rice Cultivation™ is the world's first voluntary sustainability standard for rice. The SRP Standard, together with its Assurance Scheme and Performance Indicators, provides a framework that enables farmers to demonstrate the impact of these practices in order to make public sustainable claims in the market, and monitoring of environmental performance. In 2019, the SRP launched a new initiative called the Sustainable Rice Organisations (SROs), are incentivizing 150,000 smallholders in 30 countries to shift to sustainable production practices. The SROs have been certified by the SRP and are now able to market their products through outlets in Europe beginning in 2021. The Standard has been successfully piloted in Thailand by GI in partnership with private companies. Documented and verified results include successfully helping 18,000 farmers increase their net income by more than 50% and reduce that GHG emissions from farming by 21%.

### The Sustainable Agriculture Initiative (SAI) Platform Farm Sustainability Assessment (FSA) enables farmers to demonstrate the impact of their practices in order to make public sustainable claims in the market, and monitoring of environmental performance. The FSA is used for more than 70 agricultural crops (including rice) in over 40 countries, empowering farmers with tools that help them improve their sustainability performance. In Thailand, SAI has partnered with private companies. Documented and verified results include successfully helping 18,000 farmers increase their net income by more than 50% and reduce GHG emissions from farming by 21%.

### The SRP Standard and the SAI Platform FSA apply to a wide range of rice cultivation systems, and therefore are suitable for all types of farms. However, they may not be appropriate in all locations or most suitable in local contexts. In order to further promote scaling-up and adoption of sustainable practices, the Thai Rice Project has developed the Thai Agricultural Standard (TAS) under the Agriculture Standards (ACS), under MoAC.

## Sustainable Rice that is broadly equivalent to these international standards. The TAS encompasses a range of sustainability elements, including water use, fertilizer use, rice straw, stable management, farmer welfare and biodiversity.

| Standard | Objective | Background | Scope | Differences |

| Sustainable Rice Platform (SRP) | To provide a framework that can serve a number of supporting sustainable initiatives in rice supply chains. | Originally co-conceived in 2011 by IRRI, UNEP and GIZ as an independent association. The Association serves the SRP through a Creative Commons License, effectively, a public-private partnership. | The SRP Standard applies to best practices for rice production, including post-harvest handling and storage under the farmer's control. The SRP Standard (Version 2) was developed in 2019 (pages 41 and 53) and includes 8 major themes: * Farm management (crop rotation, soil fertility, etc.) * Irrigation (heavy rainfall areas, rice species, leveling, etc.) * Water use (irrigation, groundwater extraction, etc.) * Nutrient management * Integrated pest management (weeds, diseases, birds, etc.) * Harvest and post-harvest handling (storage, stubble and straw, etc.) * Health and safety (for both workers and the environment) * Labour (forced labour, hazardous work, etc.) | The TAS is grounded in international standards frameworks - ACFS is a recognized third-party certification scheme that includes specific requirements and implementation requirements. ## The Thai Rice Insurance Scheme (TRIS)

### Internationally, there is demand for sustainable rice from a growing number of international rice sourcing companies. This demand is driven by the need to ensure traceability and to credibly distinguish sustainable rice from conventional rice.

The rice sector actively participates in the development of sustainability standards and recognizes that sustainability-focused agricultural standards can facilitate access to premium markets.

### The Thai Rice Insurance Scheme (TRIS), a national rice insurance scheme has been implemented in Thailand. TRIS is designed as an insurance-based risk management tool for farmers, which complements disaster relief assistance provided through the National Disaster Scheme. Under the National Disaster Scheme, compensation is paid out on a per hectare basis and is limited to 30 baht (€1.25) per hectare.

The main season rice area in a declared calamity area - defined by the occurrence of natural disasters (droughts, dry spells, floods, etc.) or disease infestations - is compensated at 99-288 baht per tonne (€4–0.38) depending on the location and type of disaster.

### There are two types of rice insurance policy under the TRIS: Basic Insurance Coverage (Tier 1) and Agricultural Credit Insurance (Tier 2). Tier 1 provides coverage for natural disasters and disease infestations at a rate of 99 baht per tonne (€0.43) in the event of natural disasters or 595 baht per tonne (€2.6) in the event of disease infestations. Tier 2 provides coverage for natural disasters and disease infestations at a rate of 100-288 baht per tonne (€4–0.38) depending on the location and type of disaster.

The premium rates are determined by the average total production cost per tonne, which is calculated as follows:

* Tier 1: The premium rate is based on value-added tax (VAT) and range varies from 99 to 288 baht per tonne (€0.43–0.38).
* Tier 2: The premium rate for BAIC loaned farmers is fixed at 595 baht per tonne (€2.6). For non-BAIC loaned farmers, premium rates depend on the location risks of planted areas at the district level.

The premium rates are determined by the average total production cost per tonne and are calculated as follows:

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE 26

## B

### Key Points:

- Tier 1: Non-BAAIC (This government subsidises 59.4 baht per rai ($0.3 Euros) of the insurance premium for non-BAAIC based Tier 1 farmers, accounting for 60% of the premium in low-risk zones and 80% in high-risk zones).
- For Tier 2 add-on insurance coverage, the premium rates are not subsidised and also vary by risk zone. The premium rate is 35 baht per rai ($0.1 Euros) for medium-risk zones and 110 baht per rai ($0.5 Euros) for high risk zones.

### Statistics:

- In 2021, 3.7 million rice farmers were insured under the TRIS – over 99% of them under Tier 1 only and an additional 486,000 under Tier 2.
- The number of farmers covered by the scheme has increased from 500,000 in 2013 to 7.5 million (80,000 farmers) in 2021 and 1.9 million in 2018.
- Penetration of the TRIS into rice farmers has now reached over 60% compared with only 40% in 2013.

### Financials:

- The scheme received premium payments of approximately 5.8 billion baht (Euro 7.9 million) and paid out insurance claims worth around 1.8 billion baht.
- The average loss ratio was 65%, with a net profit margin of -42%. The loss ratio does, however, vary from year to year; in 2019 it exceeded 200%, as high premium rates were paid out for the first time.

### Challenges:

- The TRIS has achieved considerable successes over its decade of implementation. However, it does face a number of challenges (Table 3). In particular: (i) the scheme is expensive – accounting for 6% of the government’s total agricultural insurance budget; (ii) there are issues with claims processing and stakeholder engagement – notably that of farmers and insurers – needs to be sustained; (iii) due to the ratio of assets under management, the scheme has not been able to attract private sector investment.
- The scheme is also facing challenges from: (a) a lack of demand for insurance among small local insurers have limited appetite to expand the scheme; and (b) climate change is introducing new uncertainties into the risk landscape.

### More Sophisticated Approaches:

More sophisticated approaches at assessing and pricing risk could positively contribute to all of these challenges.


## Table 3: SWOT Assessment of the Thai Rice Insurance Scheme

| Strengths | Weaknesses |
| --- | --- |
| Strong PPP collaboration | Delays in the declaration of calamity areas |
| 11 year track record of growth and increasing market share | Cases of non-declaration of calamity areas (basis for claims) |
| Covers a broad range of natural hazards | Poor quality of data on crop yields |
| High farmer participation | Poor quality of data on crop yields |
| Low cost to farmers | Poor pay-outs according to partial losses |
| No premium increase in 2019 | Inconsistent and unpredictable production costs – but top-up Tier 2 insurance is more expensive than the base Tier 1 scheme, increasing risk for insurers |
| Tier 3: Only for the wet season crop – despite the fact that (a) for irrigated farmers in the Central Plain, the dry season crop accounts for over half of their total cycle; and (b) drought, pests and disease are often a far greater threat to crops than floods. The scheme is also subject to the judgment of DUSA officials: pricing is not properly based on risk assessment and the scope of coverage is limited |
| Direct pay-outs (insurance companies rely upon DUSA loss reports, which are manually performed and lack accuracy) | TGA linked IT system |
| Limited understanding of farmers – the scheme does not affirmatively enrolment of farmers in low-risk zones. The premium for Tier 1 is subsidised and is hence considerably lower |
| High reliance upon government and BAAIC subsidies |

## Opportunities
- Expanded use of digital technologies – geospatial data, mobile apps, etc.
- Improved access to credit and rent could bring down costs, better design support to farmers and strengthen stakeholder participation.

## Threats
- Annual Cabinet approval of the subsidy budget introduces uncertainty.
- Loss of revenue from the fund (RfC), limiting the incentive of investors to participate.
- Climate change is introducing new weather extremes, periodicities and damage impacts.
- The scheme is subject to high transaction costs.
- High fiscal burden of the scheme and the need for a separate Act.
- Limitations in the implementation of the Microinsurance Act and the Public Debt Management Act.
- Limited capacity building and training constraints and hence opportunities to redesign – e.g., insurance products.

58. Given the challenges faced by the TRIS – which are widely recognized by all the key stakeholders, including the government, TMA members, BAAC and rice farmers – there has been considerable discussion on how to improve the scheme’s performance and sustainability.
Solutions have focused on: (i) technological innovation, including better use of big data, remote sensing and crop monitoring technologies; (ii) improving risk management tools to reduce fraud risk and improve communications with farmers; and (iii) transitioning away from, or automating, manual processes.

59. The government’s action plan to reform the TRIS is outlined in the 11th NEDSP (2023-2026), which notes that “the crop insurance system has not yet encouraged farmers to voluntarily purchase insurance.” Under Goal III of the NEDSP, Subgoal 5.4 aims for a more effective and efficient crop insurance scheme.
Indicator 2.1 refers to the need for more spatially refined assessment of climate risks. Strategy 3 of Goal III is to develop an integrated risk management system that includes: (i) the use of information systems for natural resources (water, soils, etc.), agriculture (optimized growing techniques), and disaster risk reduction; (ii) the development of a platform for data access and warehousing; and Sub-Strategy 9.2 on the development of platforms to facilitate data access and to support decision-making in the agricultural sector.
The inter-ministerial Committee on Disaster Risk Improvement (CDRI) has been established to oversee the TRIS reform.

60. A number of parallel initiatives have generated useful insights to support the work of the CDRI. For example, TMA has been running a pilot project in conjunction with the Department of Agriculture and the National Institute for Space Research and Technology (NIST) to develop an index-based crop insurance product. The project is supported by the Global Environment Facility (GEF), the Geospatial and Space Technology Development Center (GSTDC), and the Ministry of Science, Industry and Technology.
The CDRI has also been working on improving the quality and accuracy of satellite data sources (e.g., the correlation between different vegetation indices) and the use of big data. The TRIS has undertaken an assessment of index-based insurance products for rice in Thailand over the past two years and noted no damage data; the URC has undertaken an assessment of index-based insurance products for rubber.
The CDRI has also explored technical options for extending insurance to the off-season. BAAC has developed a business plan for the development of an index-based crop insurance product, including the Remote Sensing-based Information and Insurance for Crops in Emerging Economies (RICE) project, which is supported by the World Bank. The RICE project is being implemented with Axiata (a Swiss-based remote sensing company) and Allianz Re and subsequently Swiss Re.
The CDRI has also undertaken a review of the capacity building needs and independence of the TRIS, including index based scheme designs and remote sensing technology options, accompanied by national stakeholder consultations, capacity building and field trials.

## Market-Oriented Smallholder Value Chains (MSVC) project

### Project title: Market-Oriented Smallholder Value Chains (MSVC)

#### Implementing agency / donor: GIZ / German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ)

**Duration:** 2018 – 2022  
**Budget:** 3.5 million USD, including O&M and Crop Life International

The MSVC project was developed as a response to the fact that rice in Thailand is primarily produced in smallholder settings. A low degree of organisation and poor infrastructure mean that farmers occupy a weak position in the market. The project aims to improve the quality of agricultural services at the same time as creating regulatory and market requirements that relate to rice production, processing, and trade. In collaboration with Crop Life International, the MSVC project was formulated to build long-term relationships between non-farm enterprises and smallholder farmers, particularly those in the North-East region, through mega-farms and community rice centres (CRCs) as means of delivering support to, and enhancing the market position of, smallholder farmers. The project has five pillars:

- Improving production and post-harvest practices
- Strengthening farmer organisations
- Creating market opportunities
- Supporting public-private sector policy dialogue

An additional, cross-cutting, workstream of the MSVC project supported the adoption of the SRP Standard and EAM for rice in Thailand.

Approximately 19,000 Thai rice farmers in the North-East benefited from MSVC support (3,000 more than initially targeted); on average, they experienced a 20% increase incomes and were able to reduce GHG emissions by 27%. Over 8,500 farmers received training and technical support on sustainability standard compliance.

Based in Thailand, this global project— a joint initiative of GIZ, MARS, Ebro Foods and Olam International — aims to strengthen tools and systems to scale-up the production and global marketing of sustainable rice. The project will focus on developing and promoting best practices for the production of high-quality sustainable rice and its relevant tools such as the SSRP standard (in Thailand), its domestic equivalent, the TAO standard, and other international standards, which we help members increase the value of rice and expand the market for the product.

### Climate-Smart Technologies and Practices Supported by the Foundation Projects

#### 6.7 Laser land levelling (LLL): Laser land levelling is a precision agriculture practice for field levelling that is commonly used in the production of high-quality rice, such as in the Mekong Delta region of Vietnam. LLL is a quick and effective means of ensuring that an agricultural field has a level top flat surface (with a slope between 0-2%) to allow for efficient water application and drainage. It can be applied using a laser-guided tractor or by hand, such as with a waterlogging. Application of LLL can increase water use efficiency by 12-40% and fertilizer use efficiency by up to 35%. The amount of irrigation water required per hectare for rice production can be reduced by 20%-30%. Laser land levelling considerably lowers irrigation time from six to four 78 hours per hectare. This results in a reduction of the number of times that farmers need to irrigate their fields, which maximizes water savings.

Laser land levelling technology requires the use of a tractor with an external hydraulic system and a laser receiver, a control box and a hydraulic valve. A 40-horsepower four-wheel drive tractor (WDT) or 20-30 horsepower three-wheel drive tractor is required to operate the laser. The field must be levelled within 1.5 m of the desired slope.

## Alternate wetting and drying (AWD)

AWD is a water-saving technology that farmers can apply to reduce methane emissions from rice fields while maintaining their yields. Empirical testing of AWD in the Central Plains of Thailand indicates that AWD reduces total methane emissions by up to 70% compared with continuous flooding. The high potential for water saving is underlined by other studies, which estimate that AWD could save as much as 50% of irrigation water while maintaining rice yields.

According to the 2019 Refinement to the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories, an empirical study in Thailand found that methane emissions from paddy fields can be reduced by up to 70% as a result of AWD implementation. The reduction is achieved through multiple drainage periods during the cropping season of irrigated rice.

Empirical studies conducted in the Central Plains of Thailand indicate that methane emissions from paddy fields can be reduced by up to 63%, with no impact on rice yields, when applying climate-smart water management practices such as alternate wetting and drying (AWD). The reduction is achieved through multiple drainage periods during the cropping season.

In AWD, irrigation water is applied a few days after the disappearance of the ponded water. Hence, the field is alternately flooded and non-flooded. The number of days of non-flood soil between irrigations can vary from 1 to 7 days depending on crop growth stage. A practical way to implement AWD is by using a field water level to monitor the water depth in the field. When the water level drops below 5 cm, irrigation should be applied to re-flood the field to a depth of at least 8-10 cm and maintained for up to 3 days as needed.

Site-specific nutrient management (SSNM): SSNMs help maintain or enhance crop yields while saving fertilizer through more efficient use. It represents an effective adaptation strategy to declining water availability in rice production systems, especially under climate change scenarios. SSNMs can identify localized areas for targeted fertilizer application through differences in leaf coloration and apply email, geographically precise amounts of fertilizer while also reducing farm worker exposure to agrochemicals.

### Straw / stubble management (SSTM)

If they have access to irrigation systems, then rice farmers seek to grow 2 crop cycles per year. This leaves only a short time period in which to prepare the land for planting the second crop. The most common method of preparing this land is burning the straw and stubble that remains after harvest. Post-harvest burning of straw and stubble provides services to farmers if straw and stubble have not been removed from the field beforehand. Post-harvest burning also releases nutrients in the form of ash, which can be used as a fertilizer. Burning straw and stubble also helps to reduce the amount of organic matter in the soil, thus facilitating land preparation, as well as a pest and disease management tool (1). This practice of burning straw and stubble is common throughout Southeast Asia and contributes significantly to air pollution. According to IPPC good practice guidelines, CO2 emissions from biomass burning are generally considered to be neutral because they are offset by the carbon dioxide released during the combustion cycle that started with primary production. However CH4 and NOx emissions from biomass burning are considered to be significant contributors to climate change (1). Burning straw and stubble also releases SO2, leading to anaerobic decomposition of the stubble and further methane production.

To minimize GHG emissions, removal of the straw by baling or using it as a substrate for fertilizer are more sustainable options. The use of a baler is an option that can be considered in areas where there is access to a pick-up real mechanism and a compacting unit to make a dense mass of square or round baled straw. This bale can then be used as a substrate for the application of fertilizers, such as urea, as well as for production feedstock for bioenergy, pulp / paper production through cellulose extraction, biochar or as a fuel for energy generation (2).

### Integrated Pest Management (IPM)

Thailand ranks fourth in the world in its annual use of pesticides. Of the five most commonly used pesticides in Thailand, four have been banned in the European Union. The Pesticide Action Network (PAN) has reported that 10% of farmers in Thailand are using banned pesticides. Foundation projects support a combination of IPM techniques, including conservation of natural enemies through habitat creation and use of biopesticides. In addition to this, there is also an emphasis on cultural practices (3). DAE is currently supporting farming communities to establish County Pest Management Committees (CPMCs) in each province (one per district), with each one supporting approximately 30 farmers to implement IPM techniques. Such technologies are being implemented by the government and private sector in countries such as Indonesia, Malaysia and Viet Nam; for instance, in 2010 Thailand implemented one of the world's largest integrated pest management projects.

most successful biological control programmes, the control of the cassava mealy bug [174]. However, due to a range of knowledge, behavioural and economic barriers, IPM is not being systematically applied.[174] As a single example, in Thailand, only 20% of rice farmers are using IPM. A recent study[175] analysing the positive impact on rice yields of biological pest control, notably the role of damselflies, demonstrated that IPM can increase rice yield by up to 30%. However, other studies[176][177] suggest that rice yields in insecticide-treated fields are often lower than those in untreated fields.[178]

### 75. IPM offers considerable adaptation benefits: by avoiding or reducing climate-induced outbreaks of pests, pathogens and weeds; by increasing the resilience of crops to climate change; and by improving soil health. It already confront a number of pests, notably delusifiers such as Chrysopogonid nematodes and Myrmicaria disperses.[179] However, there is currently no evidence that IPM can reduce yield losses due to drought or flooding. There is some evidence that climate change – particularly drought, which results in water stress and hence nutrient stress[180][181] – may increase the risk of pest outbreaks. In addition, IPM offers considerable adaptation benefits: (i) by reducing available yield loss; IPM reduces the GHG emission intensity per unit of food produced compared to conventional agriculture; (ii) by increasing soil health and stubble burning practices with mimicking the risk of corresponding pest or disease outbreaks.

### 76. Rice variety diversification, crop diversification and crop rotation: Protecting local genetic rice varieties is essential for ensuring long-term resilience against climate change. In addition, some rice varieties in certain geographies (e.g., drought-resistant and heat-resistant) offer potential climate adaptation benefits.[182] For example, the Thai jasmine rice variety ‘Suphan Buri’ (SBR1) has a high yield under white rice (typically comprising of modern, high-yielding varieties and generally sold at the lowest prices), particularly in dry seasons. The SBR1 is also more resistant to drought than other rice varieties; it is less expensive than white rice[183]. Hoi Muei rice (another Thai jasmine rice with premium quality and price, often used for export) has a high yield under white rice but is more susceptible to drought in the Northeast East.[184]

### 77. Within these categories, there are at least 99 local rice varieties found in Thailand, each with unique characteristics relating to: inter alia, disease resistance, adaptability to environmental conditions and production costs.[185] The Thai jasmine rice San-pah-tong I (SPTI), New Phra II (NPII), RSB6 and Skon Tawan[186] are all high-yielding varieties. However, the SBR1 is more heat-resistant than other varieties; Suphan Buri (SBR1) is relatively more tolerant to heat.[187] The range of varieties planned has, however, reduced significantly in recent years due to a combination of factors including: (i) the increasing use of modern traditional farming methods; to modern, commercially-oriented rice agriculture; government support

## For irrigated rice areas where rice is typically cultivated two or three times a year, crop diversification can save water and reduce fertilizer use. Rice varieties that are more drought-tolerant than the current varieties (e.g., as potato, maize, vegetables or beans) that use less water during a drying drought situation or that offer higher economic returns per unit of water used.

### Irrigated areas in the North offer the potential to grow rice in the wet season, followed by a post-rainy crop such as sorghum, millet, or groundnuts. Such intercropping can be grown under moisture-stress conditions and which help conserve moisture and enrich soil through nitrogen fixation. This would also reduce water use for irrigation of the second crop.

### A perennial rice variety cultivated around perennial plants, trees and ponds does not require intensive care once fully grown. Moreover, the products can be obtained all year round if a range of different varieties are used.

### Crop rotation also improves the ability of rice farmers to suppress pest disease outbreaks and reduce soil-borne diseases. It is recommended that farmers should rotate their crops every 3 years in order to avoid an outbreak of root blight, a fungal disease caused by Magnaporthe oryzae, can cause yield losses of up to 50%. Crop rotation also helps to improve the physical structure of the soil and facilitates faster recovery from biotic stresses once the stress has been removed.

However, farmers lack crop rotation experience and expertise, and also timely market price information for better decision-making. In this context, if subsistence farming and/or food security is a consideration, then the promotion of crops such as sorghum or millet would be appropriate, whereas nitrogen fixing crops (for example) could be promoted for commercial farmers.

## Dry direct-seeded rice (DSD). Dry direct seeding is a crop establishment method wherein rice seeds are sown directly into paddy fields. This method has been widely adopted in the Philippines and Indonesia where it is known as "padi dibasah" or "padi direng". The seedlings are raised in a nursery and then transplanting them into flooded fields. Globally, DSR is practiced on approximately 32% of rice area (FAO, 2015). It has been shown that dry direct seeding can include higher seeding rates, lodging and risk of weed and nematode infestation. DSD also works most effectively when undertaken in conjunction with early-morning, short-duration (ESD) adapted rice varieties.

But DSR also offers significant benefits: plants are not subjected to stresses, such as being pulled from the soil and having to re-establish roots; (ii) reduced fertilizer use because of the higher nutrient content in the direct-seeded rice variety; (iii) reduced methane emissions because of the less saturated / more aerobic conditions associated with direct seeding compared to transplanting; and (iv) reduced emissions from the shorter-duration rice varieties often used in conjunction with DSR. The extent of these benefits will vary depending upon the baseline and the specific hydrological and nutrient regime used with direct seeding.

## 80. Farm-level water management (FWM)

The amount of water available for rice farming at the beginning of the growing season is determined by rainfall, irrigation infrastructure, and its subsequent storage. Construction / enlargement of hydropower infrastructure, such as dams and monkey check dams, can increase the availability of water in a river basin. The Royal Thai Government has developed a Master Plan on Water Resource Management, which envisages the construction of over 514,000 small dams to store water for irrigation purposes.

FWM is an important component of sustainable rice farming that should be implemented by individual farmers without the high capital costs and planning / coordination challenges associated with large-scale infrastructure projects. The most common FWM practices are: (i) construction and installation on-farm water tanks for rainwater collection. Such tanks are small - typically 2-4 cubic metres in size and provide a storage capacity of up to 10,000 litres; (ii) the use of earthen dams or check dams to store rainwater in earthen walls. They can supply water to rice fields via ditches made by ploughing or pipes / via a pre-existing system of irrigation channels; and (iii) the construction of small weirs which tanks are most effectively employed in conjunction with water-efficient farming methods such as AWD.

## 81. Agro-meteorological advisory services

Providing farmers with up-to-date, accurate weather forecasts, agricultural information, and advice on appropriate responses (e.g. planting date, water use, harvesting date). The government is already supporting this through the Royal Thai Meteorological Department (RTMD) which seeks to digitise agriculture by, inter alia, providing agricultural information systems and mobile phone alerts, including those related to rice farming.

The RTMD has established a Smart Water Operation Centre (SWOC) with link data with relevant agencies for synoptic weather forecasts. The SWOC also provides real-time weather data and historical weather information from the Thai Meteorological Department (TMD), the Hydro and Agro Informatics Institute, the Geo-Information Science Institute, and the National Water Resources Office. It is connected to the Hydrographic Department of the Royal Thai Navy, where it is compiled for forecasts. Working in 3

## Technology / Practice

- **Land laser levelling (LLL):**
  - Strengthens soil structure
  - Reduces water runoff
  - Reduces fuel and chemical use
  - Reduces incidence of water-borne pests
    - Enables farmers to access and prepare for, extreme conditions

- **Alternating wetting and drying (AWD):**
  - Improves fertilizer application
  - Strengthens soil structure
  - Reduces water runoff
  - Reduces fuel and chemical use
  - Reduces incidence of water-borne pests
    - Enables farmers to access and prepare for, extreme conditions

## Selling Points

- **LLL:**
  - More precise planting (typically every 5 years)
  - Traditional (brush) fertilizing is no longer needed
  - Farmers who till farmland themselves can benefit more
  - Typically undertaken in conjunction with other soil health improvements as it can be difficult to implement alone

- **AWD:**
  - Strengthens soil structure
  - Reduces water runoff
  - Reduces fuel and chemical use
  - Reduces incidence of water-borne pests
    - Farmers are rarely penalized for not using the system, as they have water to feed their fields.
    - In areas without public irrigation systems, farmers can retain as much water in their fields as they did previously when rains were uncertain

## Barriers to Adoption / Other Considerations

- **LLL:**
  - Requires knowledge about ARD technologies (even when combined with LLL)
  - Farmers are unlikely to use the technology if it is not cost-effective
  - Traditional (brush) fertilizing is no longer needed
    - Farmers who till farmland themselves can benefit more
  - Typically undertaken in conjunction with other soil health improvements as it can be difficult to implement alone

- **AWD:**
  - Delivery model causes some barriers:
    - Farmers are unlikely to use the technology if it is not cost-effective.
    - Farmers are unlikely to use the technology without the correct information may not be available.
  - Many farming areas use water sharing systems, and public irrigation systems continued to be a major barrier. Engagement with village heads or heads of mega farms

## Site-specific nutrient management (SNM)
- **Reduction of chemical fertilizers by ~10%**
    * Weather forecasts (TV, radio, traditional methods) are not available to farmers from drying fields
    * Complex to design and apply
    * Small-scale farmers have mixers machines to make fertilizer
    * Difficult to find the fertilizer
    * Farmers lack detailed knowledge of soil and nutrient requirements
- **Straw and stubble management (SSM)**
    * Straw and stubble preparation are limited to a few percent of the rainfed states or irrigated areas
    * Burning is quick, low-cost disposal method but awareness of the health risks is low
    * Some tractor service centers do not provide services if farmers have not purchased fuel
- **Fertilizer soil management (FSM)**
    * Farmers use old straw and stubble as fertilizer, which requires time and labor for plowing, as well as logistics for transportation
    * Only a few states have land amenable to the use of animal manure. In other states, other farmers are depleting resources possible during the wet season
    * Farmers burn straw and stubble to control pests, disease and weedy rice

## Integrated pest management (IPM)
- **Reduction of chemicals**
    * Biological pesticides are complex and time-intensive to make. Often, small-scale farmers are unlikely to have the necessary skills or resources themselves
    * Some chemical pesticides cannot be eliminated through training because of a misunderstanding of farmers
    * Farmers are skeptical that biological pesticides will work against all pests
    * The information being offered to farmers to date has focused almost entirely on chemical substances rather than scouting, monitoring and other IPM measures. Many farmers are unlikely to do their own scouting because it is too onerous
    * Some farmers are reluctant in protective spraying at the beginning of the season regardless of pest prevalence

# Rice variety dissemination (drought- and heat-tolerant and short-duration varieties)

* Lack of incentives to buy new rice varieties – many farmers would prefer to use seeds they collected from previous crops.
* Short-duration rice varieties are not available in markets (because they were perceived as inferior quality compared with long-duration varieties).
* Farmers will only grow a variety if they can be confident of good yields and market prices. Farmers are not keen to grow new varieties because it is harder to manage due to the shorter duration.

## Crop diversification/rotation

* Additional crops during dry periods
* Opportunity to grow any other crops or after-crop (e.g., legumes, vegetables) ponds and pumping can be expensive.
* Lack of knowledge on crop rotation – farmers are risk averse (leading to risk aversion).
* Farmers may not be able to grow alternative crops if they do not accept short-term crops (in addition to rice).

## Dry-district selected rice (DDSR)

* Requires less labour
* Reduced need for fertiliser and preparation
* Reduced percolation of water due to cracks
* Less damage from heavy rain immediately after seeding can enhance crop establishment

## Farm-level water management (FWBM)

* Irrigation water availability
* Tanks can be used to grow crops
* Water is associated with water distribution/pumping
* Land trade-offs: land that could not be used for crops
* Informing farmers about the use of alternative inputs
* Avoid crop damage/loss

## Agro-met services

* Last mile delivery of information to farmers has been a major challenge aspect of past initiatives (e.g., insufficient farmers need access to weather and climate forecasts into concrete
* Baseline initiatives – notably The Yield Gap Initiative, which uses weather and climate forecasts and satellite imagery as the solid foundation for additional support services such as index-based insurance.

# Many farmers are unaware of new rice varieties due to the limited number of buyers and rice traders who are interested in them.
* Farmers tend to grow varieties that they have been growing previously. This may not be appropriate for them or may not be suitable given their location.
* Crop choice is determined by rice miller needs and price setting.

# Crops that can be grown in drought-prone areas (e.g., sugar cane) are susceptible/ attract pests that can also affect rice.
* Sugar cane requires a high level of inputs, which may not be available to smallholder farmers.
* Chicken meat companies have been known to offer support – particularly for women – on diversification activities (e.g. handicrafts).

# DSR – particularly when direct drill is used – is more effective when used in conjunction with irrigation and the maintenance of soil that is moist but not waterlogged.
* Seeding (pre-hydration of seeds) and planting (in soil that is otherwise dry and sun-exposed).

Foundation Projects – Financial Mechanisms

84. The NSP channels financial assistance (A) through two mechanisms, both managed by BAAC as a revolving fund. This revolving fund finances bio-emission reduction investment as well as reduce the market entry risk for service providers and a loan programme that supports investments by service providers in the form of equipment and other inputs. The revolving fund is also used to provide subsidies on BAAC’s existing Green Loan Programme which provides loans to farmers for working capital and purchase of inputs.

* **NSP revolving fund for farmers:** LLL improves rice productivity and, at the same time, is an important but costly pre-requisite for popular use of AWD (which, by itself, is a relatively low-cost option). The NSP revolving fund provides concessional zero-cost loans to farmers to receive LLL services upfront and then pay back over 3 years. This loan is provided on a non-interest bearing basis during the pilot period. Service providers are paid through the revolving fund on behalf of farmers. Pre-financing is provided by BAAC to service providers for the purchase of equipment and inputs, with willingness to pay and actual costs for service providers during the pilot case, a price of 1,530 baht/ha (or approximately US$48 per ha) was applied. A repayment schedule of 20% in year one, 20% in year two, and 60% in year three is applied with farmers required to make repayments of 40%, 40% and 20% respectively over the period. The revolving fund also provides a subsidy for the LLL promotion programme for farmers to receive a 50% subsidy for LLL application.
