## B

### Project title: Inclusive Sustainable Rice Landscapes in Thailand (ISRL-T)
Implementing agency / funder: UNEP / GEF
Duration: 2023–2025
Budget: US $5.5 million

The ISRL is a multi-country programme under the GEF’s multi-local area Food Systems, Land use and Resilience (FSLR) initiative in collaboration with the Philippines. The Thai pilot aims to enhance the rice value chain by upscaling Good Agricultural Practices (GAPs) through the SRP Standard in an integrated landscape management approach. The project will focus on the provinces of Chiang Rai, Ubon Ratchathani and North East. The ISRL programme approaches rice farming from a landscape – and primarily biodiversity management perspective. Areas outside the scope of the ISRL programme, such as drought-tolerant rice varieties and crop insurance – are outside the scope of the ISRL programme. Nevertheless, these areas will be addressed by other projects in the region. The project is facilitated by the fact that GIZ is an Executing Unit of the ISRL-T. The SRIL-T project also aims to address climate change adaptation through landscape management approaches and the promotion of youth groups, scouts and camps in relation both to forest conservation and sustainable rice farming.

### Project title: Support Programme on Scaling-up Climate Ambition on Land-Use and Agriculture (SCALA)
Implementing agency / funder: Food and Agriculture Organisation of the United Nations (FAO) / BMU/ KfW
Duration: 2015–2025
Budget: US $3.7 million (in Thailand)

The SCALA programme is a Euro 20 million initiative that is funded by the International Climate Initiative (IKI). The programme is supporting 12 countries in Africa, Asia and Latin America, including Thailand. The objective of this project is to support the scaling-up of climate-smart agriculture practices on degraded lands and climate solutions in land-use and agriculture with multi-stakeholder engagement. In Thailand, the Office of Agriculture Policy and Planning (OAPP) is the lead implementing agency for the SCALA programme and are coordinating SCALA activities across agencies. The SCALA programme recently supported MACH, a Thai government initiative to promote sustainable agricultural practices on degraded lands as one of the policy functions of the Thai Rice Project. The Thai Rice Project will coordinate with the SCALA project in Thailand to ensure that it complements and strengthens the policy framework and supporting the Thai public sector to act on the GCF NAP.

### Project title: Thai-German Initiative on Energy, Mobility and Climate (TG-EMC)
Implementing agency / funder: BMU / KfW
Duration: 2015–2027
Budget: US $3.6 million for the biomass component and Euro 4 million for ThaIClimate

Funded by the International Climate Initiative (IKI), TG-EMC aims to accelerate Thailand’s energy transition towards a sustainable model, ensuring economic growth and compatibility with high climate ambitions. Among different sub-projects under this broad (renewable energy and transportation) project, two sub-projects are connected to the Thai Rice Project.

## Project 92: Public-Private Blended Finance Facility for Climate-Sensitive Rice Landscapes (CRLF)

The CRLF is a multi-country project covering Bangladesh, Cambodia, Viet Nam and Thailand that will support the design of a new blended finance facility to catalyze public and private sector investment to scale-up climate-smart rice landscapes in these countries. The CRLF will be implemented through a public-private partnership with the World Bank as the lead implementing agency. The CRLF will be implemented by a consortium led by the World Bank, with other partners including the Asian Development Bank, the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD), the Global Environment Facility (GEF) and the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP). The ICRF Agriculture project will promote climate risk financing for the agricultural sector in cooperation with ASEAN working groups (crops, climate change and finance) through policy recommendations, updated technical guidelines, capacity building workshops, and training of financial institutions and technology providers in the ASEAN member states. The project will design or improve resilience-enhancing insurance products and other innovative financial instruments to reduce the vulnerability of smallholder farmers to climate-related risks. The project will also support the implementation of the Thai Rice Project (TRP) in Thailand in achieving results in the Thai Rice Project, notably under Activities 2.1.1 and 2.1.2.

B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram

B.2.1 Problem statement

94. The rice sector of Thailand – the country’s most important food crop – faces adverse climate pressures. These pressures are expected to increase, and will continue to place increasing burdens on farmers’ livelihoods, as well as on the national economy. Simultaneously, the rice sector stands out as the largest agricultural contributor to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions in Thailand.

95. Thai smallholder rice farmers, including a disproportionate number of women, are extremely vulnerable to increasing climate-induced water scarcity, temperature increases and heatwaves. Such factors have already been observed in recent years. The current climate change scenario is characterised by longer dry spells and higher temperatures (including temperature spikes). Smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable because they lack the resources to adapt to these changes.

96. The Thai rice sector is a significant source of GHG emissions. Rice tanning accounts for 8% of Thailand’s total emission and 35% of the country’s methane emissions.

97. Climate-smart technologies are best deployed in combination. Mitigation measures, such as AWD, that aim to reduce water use can also have unintended secondary effects which irrigation is temporarily abandoned due to water shortages. Similarly, their adaptation benefits – water conservation and increased yields – can be undermined by the lack of a reliable water supply system to conserve. The effectiveness of AWID, SSNM, IMIP, DSM and other measures is mediated by climate conditions. For example, the effectiveness of AWD depends on the availability of irrigation water to conserve. Empirical studies have shown that the combined deployment of multiple technologies can be more effective in seeking to address climate change. Furthermore, synergies exist between the technologies. Empirical studies show that the combined deployment of multiple technologies is more effective than individual mitigation effects on methane emissions stemming from the combined deployment of LYM and AWD in particular. The combined deployment of multiple technologies can also reduce water use and GHG emissions, but do so more effectively when used together.[1] There is a need to acknowledge these complementary effects between climate-smart technologies and to ensure that they are mutually supported accompanied by strengthening the innovation capacity of the rice sector – rather than relying on a one-size-fits-all approach.

98. The switch from conventional to low-emission, climate-resilient rice farming is directly reliant upon the actions of farmers and service providers, and their incentivisation through value-chain partners. Farmers are not necessarily aware that they can change their practices, and therefore the main barriers are those directly related to adoption of climate-smart technologies (e.g., lack of awareness, knowledge, skills, access to inputs, access to credit supply and potentially premium prices) can incentivise and motivate farmers to change their practices and improve yields. However, other factors such as market demand (i.e., the willingness of rice off-takers, traders, buyers, retailers and consumers – depend upon each other; facilitating change among them is necessary to achieve market transformation towards low-emission, climate-resilient rice farming.

B.2.2 Barrier analysis

Why Climate-Smart Rice is Currently Not Being Grown and Sold at Scale in Thailand

99. Despite the commitments and policies outlined in Section B.1.3 and the foundation and baseline projects outlined in Sections B.1.4 and B.1.5, a range of barriers serve to prevent the climate problem from being fully addressed.

# Cross-cutting barriers

## Knowledge and capacity constraints permeate many of the barriers presented below, spanning farmers, service providers, extension services, financial institutions, policy-makers and consumers.

### How to contribute to GHG emissions, as well as lack of technical / practical knowledge on how to improve farm productivity and reduce GHG emissions.

## Gender inequalities cut across the barriers presented below. For example, a number of relevant areas are characterized by persistent male dominance, including institutional bureaucracy, technical skills, access to resources, and decision-making power.

### In advancing careers, improving minimum working conditions or addressing the specific needs of women farmers.

## In rice farming, mechanization and digitalization are considered "male" activities and may therefore tend to create disproportionate opportunities for men. This is a key concern as women constitute the majority of smallholder farmers in Thailand. Women are often responsible for household chores and burdened with disproportionately more unpaid household work, and they are often not taken seriously as agricultural producers.

### Women's participation in rice farming is also limited by their lack of access to land, credit or water management. The proportion of all farmers is growing – more than 40% of farms are operated by women (2017). However, women tend to be less involved in decision-making roles and/or (moving away from rural areas entirely) their technical skills and adaptability to new technologies tend to be lower.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V10.0 | PAGE 48

## Barriers for Service Providers

### Climate-smart rice farming is at its nascent stage in Thailand: aspects of the emission and/or mitigation potential of climate-smart farming have been posted or synthesised, rolled-out by foundation projects, but baseline projects, but they have not been promoted and disseminated at a large scale nor in an integrated manner. The lack of awareness among farmers about the benefits of climate-smart technology and equipment carries substantial risk due to the unclear business case (particularly for smallholder farmers) compared with the well-established and well-understood sugar cane service provider sector. There is also a perception that the climate-smart rice service provision model is not financially viable, compared with the well-established and well-understood sugar cane service provider sector.

### Financial Barriers

105. Access to finance for smallholder farmers and SME service providers can be challenging, as banks tend to be cautious and reluctant to lend to marginal clients who are already exposed to volatile weather conditions and/or have limited access to land or other collateral (e.g., livestock) for rice. Many rice farmers, and notably women farmers, earning barely a subsistence income struggle to meet their basic needs and cannot afford to pay interest rates on loans. Small-scale farmers (who own less than 10 ha of land), may not produce sufficient annual income, or may be insolvent as a result of other debts used to leverage agricultural inputs. In addition, smallholder farmers are often reluctant to borrow from service providers as farmers themselves and hence face the same challenges.

106. Existing agricultural loan products are not suitable for climate-smart agriculture. The transition from business-as-usual agriculture to climate-smart agriculture takes place over a number of years. LLL, for example, requires 5–7 years before it can be harvested. As such, farmers will need to make repeat applications in the following 4 years; the annual yield increase enabled by LLL is not sufficient to offset interest payments on loans taken out at the beginning of the project period (i.e., the baseline). These cashflow profiles call for medium-term loans with an initial grace period when climate-smart practices are still being established and yields are low. Such loans should be repaid in full after harvest, revolving credit (cash flow), medium-term loans (e.g., 5-year loans for the purchase of land and equipment) or long-term loans (e.g., 10–20 years with a grace period followed by annual repayment over 15+ more years).


---
**Page 52**
---

Market linkage barriers

107. Currently, farmers find it difficult to sell rice that conforms to a sustainability standard, such as the TAS or SRP, due to limited market linkage arrangements. Also, in the current situation of competition between large and small producers, there is a tendency for private companies to segregate and aggregate high volumes of climate-smart rice, although there is high potential for this.

108. For market linkages of rice straw: there is a high demand from private companies that would like to purchase rice straw for producing biomass energy, paper pulp and agrochemicals. Farmers would likely be willing to sell their straw at a price higher than the current market price if they were assured more than the demand because of farmer inertia (due to information and awareness constraints), risk aversion, or lack of infrastructure such as there is a limited number of rice straws collectors and baling machines.

Policy and Regulatory Barriers

109. Policy and financial frameworks do not favour climate-smart rice production and are not coordinated. There are a range of baseline policies, financial instruments and government support programmes that could be used to promote climate-smart rice production. However, these are fragmented and their climate-smart impacts are not maximised. Irrigation water for farmers, for example, is provided by the Department of Water Resources (DWR) at a cost of 10 baht per cubic metre, who are the largest users of water in Thailand[24] to save or employ more water-efficient technologies and practices. The government has also introduced several policies that promote climate-smart agriculture - and, indeed, currently lack the capacity to do so. BAO’s loan programme to the rice farmers is a good example of this. It provides loans for rice farming activities such as land preparation, farm activity, but they are not generally focused on climate-smart technologies and practices, and nor are they designed in a way that would encourage farmers to adopt them. The government’s national insurance scheme for rice farmers is already facing operational challenges and will struggle to provide adequate coverage if the number of rice farmers increases. Similarly, the voluntary carbon crediting scheme provides financial incentives for emission-reduction activities, but it barely operates within the agricultural sector, which is the second largest emitting sector in Thailand[24].

B.2.3 Baseline gaps

110. Mapping the foundation projects and the baseline projects onto the barrier analysis provides useful insight into the “gaps” – i.e., enduring barriers that need to be addressed by the Thai Rice Project.

# Barrier Removal by the Foundation and Baseline Projects: Remaining Challenges

## Barriers for farmers

- Awareness raising and training on the LLLP (e.g., through farmer field schools, curricula, trainers, seminars, etc.)
- FAS services to reduce transaction costs.
- Access to premium rice markets.
- Research and development have recommended that rice varieties with high yield potential in combination with rice farming: improved water management and pest protection.

## Barriers for service providers/ market barriers

- Development of business model for service providers.
- Training for service providers on the use, operation and maintenance of climate-smart technologies.
- Service payment through NSP (National Social Protection).
- BACI loan programme.

## Technical/ technological barriers

- Expanding access to climate-smart technology manufacturers and distributors to smallholder farmers with 30–50 HP tractors: e.g., smaller tractors are more affordable and better suited for rice farming.
- Collection on the use of drones for soil moisture mapping, crop observation, etc.

## Market linkage barriers

- Developing a market for sustainable rice farming aligned with international standards.
- Access to premium rice markets.
- Awareness raising and training on the benefits of straw incorporation into soil and its market linkage strategies.

## Financial barriers

- NGRP (National Grant for farmers to pre-finance LLLP).
- Development of business model alignment with international standards for access premium rice markets.
- NSP funding and investments in LLL machinery.
- Establishment as a national grant–funding mechanism for partner projects.

# Remaining Gaps and Challenges

Adoption has not been a principal focus (with limited exceptions). The adoption rate is low, partly because of weak demand from climate-smart water scarcity; the straw incorporation rate is also low. This is partly due to lower water access conditions, including poor irrigation infrastructure. In addition, there are concerns about increased GHG emissions from diesel pumps: farmer awareness and training on the use of solar-powered pumps is limited. Farmers are not aware that they can derive benefits from business-as-usual practices and technologies (e.g., improved water management). Service providers: distribution to crop diversification and other crops; lack of technical support for service providers; service providers: distribution to crop diversification and other crops; lack of technical support for service providers; distribution to crop diversification and other crops are even more sensitive to climate change than rice. The adoption rate is low, partly because of weak demand from climate-smart water scarcity; the straw incorporation rate is also low.

Service provider market entry into climate-smart technologies has been limited so far, partly because of lack of technical support for service providers. Service providers face a catch-22 situation: they are reluctant to invest in new technologies and services without guaranteed demand, but at the same time, they are unable to implement climate smart techniques without technical and financial support.

Adoption has not been a principal focus; mitigation measures have been limited (e.g., use of improved rice varieties): larger farmers are more efficient as the smallest farmers are less likely to adopt new technologies (not rent); then land: up-taken potential of digital technology is low; lack of awareness on climate-smart insurance; up-taken potential of drones (up to 40 LLL farmers) is low.

Promoting the adoption of the TAP in a lower cost and nationally owned sustainability standard, as well as promoting the use of certified sustainable rice. The demand from private companies that would like to invest in the production of high-quality, premium rice for farmers: policy makers need to ensure quality assurance; service providers are providers in public-private partnerships (PPP) and need to build confidence in the services market.

The NGRP is not aligned with the LLL (high (paperwork) transaction cost for farmers to access the NSP funding); the NSP funding and investments in LLL machinery programme has not met expectations: NSP does not cover climate-smart rice farming. This does not solve the need for improved water management, reduced farmers’ costs; bureaucracy is and is not aligned with the NGRP (high transaction cost). The need for a financing mechanism for mitigation in the agricultural sector is high. There are opportunities to create synergies between financial support instruments.

## Knowledge and capacity barriers

- Training of local-level government extension officers on mitigation technologies
- Promotion of private extension services
- Establishment of a Working Group to develop a rice levy system and tools

Adaptation has not been a principal focus, conservative institutions and individual capacity gaps remain, particularly in the case of women farmers. The operationalizing ThaCiiT is seeking accreditation as a key step towards increasing its impact. There is limited understanding of weather forecasts, especially in the context of climate change. Training and awareness raising are needed for extension officers and farmers such as training-of-trainers, 'smart farmers' and larger scale farmer field schools. This includes building awareness (detailed understanding of the TAS and associated technologies) and increasing access to information.

Adaptation has not been a principal focus; no holistic initiatives have been implemented in response despite drought, in particular being identified as a key challenge for rice production. There is limited capacity within the sector to measure the elements of the NDC relating to agriculture. The lack of data on agricultural emissions makes it difficult to measure and monitor progress towards the national standard with considerable potential but requires further development. There are no existing policies to strengthen their beneficial climate impacts, especially in relation to smallholder farmers who are generally dispersed, small-scale, multi-gas emission sources and often vulnerable.

## Policy and regulatory barriers

- GHG calculation tool (Emission Inventory) developed, accompanied by training and user manual
- Development of the TAS and associated technologies across government
- Support needed for progress in integrating climate mitigation in ongoing initiatives

Although the majority of smallholder rice farmers are women, they have limited access to information and services. Women in general, and vulnerable women in particular, face a range of barriers to accessing land and - in extreme cases - violence; the concept of 'women's empowerment' is not well understood by groups of women, notably migrant workers and displaced persons, who are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts.

## Gender barriers

- Proactive engagement of female farmers by offering special training on mitigation services.

111. (Figure 10) and in Annex B2b: The Thai Rice Project will put in place a comprehensive suite of measures to remove the identified barriers in order to achieve the project objective and, ultimately, address food security.

112. The successful implementation of this project will guide Thailand in reducing emissions from rice agriculture (thus contributing to MIR4), supporting the most vulnerable people and communities (ARA 1), addressing climate change adaptation needs for smallholder farmers through the adoption of climate-resilient management practices (ARA 4). This will ensure Thailand's alignment with the Paris Agreement, as well as its commitments under the Green Climate Fund.

113. The GCF’s Sectoral Guide on Agriculture and Food Security identifies 3 pathways to achieving transformation towards climate-resilient and low-emission agricultural systems: (i) promoting resilient agroecological practices; (ii) strengthening access to climate-smart technologies, inputs, extension services, and climate information services; and (iii) reconfiguring food systems. Through its support to, inter alia, climate-smart rice varieties, improved irrigation management, water-saving technologies, and climate-resilient rice insurance and the Thai Agricultural Standard (TAS) for sustainable rice, the Thai Rice Project addresses all three pathways.

114. The project logic can be stated as follows:

IF farmers and service providers are capacitated, equipped and able to apply climate-smart technologies and practices, and their linkages to markets are strengthened, and if public policies and incentives are in place to support these changes, then the following will happen:
THEN paradigm shift will be achieved through the development of a climate-smart rice sector built upon self-outsourcing business models, mainstreamed climate-smart technologies, market-based value chains, and improved livelihoods.

BECAUSE rice farming in Thailand will be timely set upon a climate-resilient and low-emission pathway, with significant investment for its implementation.

115. The theory of change diagram depicts how the Thai Rice Project aims to transform the sector towards low-emission, climate-resilient rice farming in Thailand.


116. Smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable to several threats: drought, floods, yield and fields and water security in Thailand. Moreover, rice farming produces considerable GHG emissions, exacerbated by the prevailing practices of direct and double management (burning) and fertilizer application.

## The Thai Rice Project Will

The Thai Rice Project will enable climate-smart rice farmers—increasingly, women farmers—to adapt to a changing climate (Outcome 1) while simultaneously reducing GHG emissions (Outcome 2).

### Project Design and Outcomes

#### Bottom-up Approach
- **Behavioral Changes**: Triggered at the level of the farmer through behavioral changes.
- **Adoption of Climate-smart Practices**: Climate-smart practices (Output 1.1) and climate-smart technologies will be made available by service providers—industrialized, mechanized, and well-equipped service providers supported by a strong institutional framework.

#### Financial Support
- The climate-smart rice that is produced will be verified and sold at higher market prices.
- A financial support mechanism will be established to ensure the sustainability of this sector, designed and implemented to unlock additional revenue flows to support Thailand’s transition towards a low-carbon economy (Outcome 3).

### Project Components

1. **Rice Facility Coordination**
   - Tools and new institutional frameworks—the Rice Facility—that coordinates investments from the public and private sectors.
2. **Gender Equality**
   - The project will provide strong advocacy for gender equality and acquire significant insights into, and experience of, implementing a CCF project.

### Regional Benefits
- Governments across the region will benefit from the project’s experiences through peer-to-peer learning (Outcome 4).

### Institutional Framework
- The project is led by the Office of Natural Resources and Environmental Policy and Planning.

## Project programme description

### B.3.1 Project scope and geography

The Thai Rice Project targets approximately 253,400 smallholder rice farmers, including a minimum of 100,000 currently being served by the NRP.

The project area includes a total of 21 provinces, consisting of 12 provinces in the Central Plains, 7 provinces in the Northeast, and 2 provinces in the South East, as shown in Figure 11 below. The farmland targeted by the project extends over a physical area of ~718,000 hectares, of which ~360,000 hectares are under wet season rice production.

The effective farmland is defined as the total area where rice is planted at least once in the same field; the effective farmland – the wet season planting area plus the dry season planting area.

### Notes:
125. For the selection of the 21 target provinces, 24 provinces were assessed based on consultations with the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MoAC) and the Department of Agriculture (DoA). The assessment was based on a Climate Exposure Index (CEI), consisting of 20 climate variables constructed specifically for this FIP. These variables include: mean temperature; mean precipitation; number of days with rainfall >5 mm; number of days with rainfall >10 mm; number of days with rainfall >30 mm; number of days with rainfall >60 mm; number of days with rainfall >90 mm; number of days with rainfall >120 mm; number of days with rainfall >180 mm; number of days with rainfall >240 mm; number of days with rainfall >300 mm; number of days with rainfall >360 mm; number of days with rainfall >420 mm; number of days with rainfall >480 mm; number of days with rainfall >540 mm; number of days with rainfall >600 mm; number of days with rainfall >720 mm; number of days with rainfall >960 mm; and number of days with rainfall >1,200 mm. The CEI also takes into account the open-burning of agricultural residues and extensive use of chemical fertilizer are prevailing practices). In addition to these 24 provinces, three provinces were selected based on their potential for high-value rice production (i.e., grade rice for which there is a demand from high-income consumers who are potentially willing to pay a premium price), namely: Phetchaburi, Prachuap Khiri Khan and Suphan Buri. These three provinces have projects such as the NSP and the SGRIT, which are already addressing specific barriers and which have been identified by the MoAC and DoA as having potential for high-value rice production. The CEI also takes into account the impact figures in terms of number of beneficiaries reached and the total mitigation outcome achieved. It cannot be guaranteed that all provinces will achieve their target area under this FIP, because some data from the indicative allocation provided in Table 1 is due to a range of factors that cannot be assessed ex ante.

132. The Thai Rice Project is structured across 3 complementary Outcomes and Outputs:

* **Outcome 1:** Climate-smart farmers’ incomes and food security are increased despite adverse climate change
* **Output 1.1:** Farmers invest in and apply climate-smart technologies and practices.

### Output 1.1.1: Rice farmers invest in and apply climate-smart technologies and practices

#### Activity 1.1.1: Building capacity of smallholder farmers and extension services on climate-smart rice farming.

- To promote the utilization of climate-smart technologies and practices in rice production, the Thai Rice Project will train farmers in climate-smart, low-emission and climate-resilient farming methods, as well as provide them with access to inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, and irrigation services for climate-smart services. Under this approach, farmers will acquire knowledge from training - implementation of climate-smart practices - and will be able to share their experiences within the private sector actors - and consequently adapt their farming practices. The project will also ensure effective communication between the public and private sectors by providing financial support and incentives through market opportunities. It will engage high-readiness farmer groups in the project, with a focus on women farmers.

### Target rice farmers will be selected in line with Rice Department guidance and will include:
- (i) Farmers previously under the NSP
- (ii) Participants of MiAC's mega farm policy (i.e., members of mega-farms)

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 10.1.0 | PAGE 62

## Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1: Training for farmers on climate-smart techniques, practices and management

### GCF financial instrument utilized: technical assistance grant
Addressing: climate change adaptation and mitigation.

Executive Summary:

* **138.** Approximately 253,400 farmers will receive farmer-friendly and context-specific training, both theoretical (in a classroom setting, including virtual formats and online training) and practical (in the field). The training will cover topics such as: climate-smart agriculture (CSA), improved rice production (as indicated outlined in Table 11 and including inter al., LLL, AWD, SMM, SSM, PM, etc.), water management, soil health, agroforestry, integrated pest management, conservation of farmer field days and agricultural fair and expos. Particular emphasis will be placed on reaching women farmers, youth, indigenous peoples, smallholder farmers, landless people, and other vulnerable groups because of their socio-economic circumstances, particularly vulnerable to climate change – including those from conflict-affected areas, internally displaced persons, and stopped-generation households, women with disabilities, and migrant farmers and farm workers.

* **139.** Additional elements of the farmer training will include, for example:
    * Gender equality
    * Solar energy (e.g., solar cooking)
    * PV water pumping (as a means of reducing potential GHG emissions associated with AWD + FWM: riverbank erosion and sedimentation)
    * Basic management and finance competencies, including information on the financial support available to farmers. Training materials will be developed under Sub-Activity 1.2.2 and delivered under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1.
    * Outright land tenure security
    * Environmental and social safeguard (ESS)-related topics, including occupational health and safety for all workers involved in the project activities, environmental protection (e.g., water pollution and biodiversity), sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH); and farming practices that are consistent with the principles of sustainable agriculture and aligning with the stipulations of the Environmental and Social Management Plan. Annex Gaa – see also Table 20.
    * Gender climate inter-linkages, including the particular challenges faced by migrant women (in line with the provisions in the ESM).

* **140.** A sub-set of 95,000 advanced farmers seeking deeper engagement with climate smart rice farming techniques will be offered additional training. Such farmers will typically have a pre-existing basis for seeking further training, such as having already received some form of training (e.g., through a government or foundation project), or they may be belonging to a progressive mega-farm. They are also deemed to have high potential for adoption and scaling up of climate-smart rice farming techniques and/or organic certification. But the key criterion for selection as an advanced farmer is expressing an interest. Since training will be provided on a voluntary basis, it is expected that only a small proportion of smallerholder farmers, most the only most motivated farmers are expected to seek additional training. In advanced farmers, the project will have a greater impact in terms of adoption and scaling up, which may be less confident in volunteering for additional training; the project will make a pronounced effort to encourage them to participate.

    * Sustainability standards: The TSC in particular, but with additional international standards (such as the SRF) covered according to need (training materials will be developed under Sub-Activity 1.2.3 and delivered under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1).
    * Carbon finance (training materials will be developed under Sub-Activity 3.1.2.3 and delivered under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V10.0 | PAGE 63

## B

### Training curriculum and materials will be developed to be farmer-friendly (e.g., in local languages), literacy-sensitive and written from the farmers' own benefit-oriented perspective; draw to the maximum extent possible on existing resources, including those of the NIP, to ensure quality improvement to meet farmer needs. The training materials will also include specific refresher modules for extension service providers, with a focus on adaptation and resilience elements that go beyond the mitigation-focused approach currently being promoted by the NIP. These materials will be piloted in the field and integrated into the development of the new material. In this manner, successful farmers from the NIP will be developed to act as community leaders on climate smart agriculture and share their experiences with their peers.

### The training will be developed and rolled out in close collaboration with the relevant government institutions (e.g., the Department of Agriculture, the Ministry of Rural Development, the Royal Community Centres, the Royal Irrigation Department (RID), the Department of Agricultural Extension, etc.) and civil society organizations. These partners include organizations that are already supporting climate smart farmers or have climate-smart farmers within their portfolio. The training will be delivered through a combination of face-to-face and distance extension service infrastructure. This existing infrastructure will be augmented by support from IRRP, relevant government institutions, and other development partners (e.g., the International Rice Research Institute, Mongiat University of Technology), ACFS and TCG, as well as non-government and private sector actors.

### The project will facilitate private-sector participation in extension services through: (i) sustainable rice off-takers such as EBR Foods, MARR, Olam and Dymex; and (ii) additional climate-smart agriculture and value chain development. These private sector actors will be involved at all stages of the project. Areas of support will include (inter alia): (a) conducting supplementary capacity building to support farmers in developing their own business plans; (b) providing technical assistance to extension service providers; (c) capacitating farmer groups on traceability; (d) showcasing replicable incentive models to drive market demand for climate-smart rice and other climate-smart agricultural products within value chains; (e) educating suppliers and business partners to similarly incentivize climate-smart rice farming practices. The project will also involve the private sector in the development of new products, institutions, input providers, technology providers, millers, traders, exporters, retailers and producer organizations.

### Involving farmer organizations and the private sector in the provision of extension services can contribute to both the effectiveness of the intervention as well as the overall sustainability of the project. The project will also involve the private sector in the development of new products, institutions, input providers, technology providers, millers, traders, exporters, retailers and producer organizations.

### In order to coordinate training events and monitor training results and impacts, a simple but effective monitoring and reporting system will be developed and set up at the national and provincial levels; this will also include the development of a database for tracking climate-smart rice farmers, their individual products or brands, and all information provided will be fair and neutral. The project will ensure that all training materials are available in multiple languages.

### In order to coordinate training events and monitor training results and impacts, a simple but effective monitoring and reporting system will be developed and set up at the national and provincial levels; this will also include the development of a database for tracking climate-smart rice farmers, their individual products or brands, and all information provided will be fair and neutral. The project will ensure that all training materials are available in multiple languages.

### A comprehensive body of learning materials will be developed, tested, piloted across counties, institutions, and programs/programmes. The Thai Rice Project will assemble a body of high-quality training literature opening agronomic techniques and practices, and technical guidelines for the development of climate-smart rice production systems. These guidelines will be based on the principles outlined in the FAO (2002) Technical Guidance: Integrated Water and Fertiliser Management for Paddy Rice Production in Asia. Like many other such initiatives, the project will also draw from the experience gained by the International Rice Research Institute (IRRI) and the World Agroforestry Centre (ICRAF), or other appropriate tools, to be applied to improve the gender sensitivity of extension materials and approaches.


---
**Page 67**
---

146. All project-supported farmers will benefit from the training under Sub-Activity 1.1.1 by the end of project implementation, approximately 250,000 smallholder and women farmers will have received training (of whom a minimum of 115,000 will be women). A sub-set of 50,000 farmers will be enabled to achieve TAD certification through the use of climate-smart agricultural practices and technologies (see Sub-Activity 3.1.2.3). The project will devote particular effort to reaching women farmers, young farmers, and smallholder farmers in remote areas, as well as those with disabilities or other special needs (focused primarily on the North-East) which is improving local farming conditions through a landscape approach. The project will also focus on training for older farmers (60 years of age and above), given that the aging of the farmer population is not a focus of the Thai Rice Project (see Table 14), significant benefits can be achieved by providing training to this group, which includes women farmers in the rice sector as opposed to younger women farmers. Capacity building of farmers and the poor-to-poor transfer of knowledge and skills will be promoted through the project. The project will also consider implications of climate-smart agriculture in the context of their current farm and broader household situation. This will include training on how to adapt to climate change, including water management information to stakeholders in the sector on the extension activities will enable further support and early planning for future needs such as water supply and expected demand for services provided by implementing climate-smart techniques.

147. In addition to classical training approaches, the project will also provide comprehensive extension tools, including mobile phone applications (for both farmers and technical staff) which can actively contribute with their experiences and success. This will be coordinated with the relevant government agencies and private sector stakeholders. The mobile phone application for farmers will be developed under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1 while the app itself will be developed under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.2.

148. In order to scale the materials developed, the project will also develop a dynamic training toolkit on sting® Arrog, which means to 'succeed' in Esperanto. A free-of-charge, open-source digital learning platform that can be used by farmers and extension workers to access training materials. More than 200 GCF projects worldwide use over 100 public sector, private sector and CGI institutions. It is also possible for the project to develop a mobile phone application which will provide a 'one-off-the-shelf' solution to ensure that the Thai Rice Project can scale-up its training materials – with reduced costs in terms of printing, packaging, storing, issuance of certificates, etc., protection, etc. – quickly and cost-effectively.

149. The decision on which contents to implement as digital training will be taken during project implementation. It is expected that the content developed for sting® Arrog can also be used by other projects in Thailand. Such a 'personal' approach could be led with case studies from specific rice farmers, and/or through the use of mobile phone applications. The project will also develop campaigns for sustainable rice and quality aspects of future carbon credits (Sub-Activity 3.1.1.4), as well as the development of a mobile phone application.

**Sub-Activity 1.1.1.2: Public sector capacity building, with an extension on services and networks**

GCF financial instrument utilised: technical assistance grant

Addressing: climate change adaptation and mitigation

Execution period: 01/04/2018 - 31/03/2021

150. Thailand already has a strong agricultural extension network that reaches across the country, however, the extension system is fragmented. The knowledge and expertise generated under Sub-Activity 1.1.1 will be delivered in close cooperation and cooperation with existing extension structures to complement them with farmers' needs and gaps in networks, and additional sector extension support (including expertise in traditional rice farming – will, therefore, be crucial to switching the rice sector to a low-emission pathway). The current design of the Thai agricultural extension system will be capitalised for new tasks that are relevant for climate-smart agriculture, such as water, gender climate inter-linkages and ESG considerations. Furthermore, the current design of


---
**Page 68**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE 56

The extension system does not incentivize extension professionals to promote climate-smart agricultural practices to farmers: there is, for instance, no reference to climate-smart farming in the system’s internal policies and incentive schemes. The project will work with the Ministry of Agriculture to revise internal policies and incentive systems in order to anchor sustainability and climate change issues within the extension system.

151. At the national level, a group of agricultural extension officers, farmers’ organisations and technical experts will be established. This group will include RD, DoAE, and—through the GCF-IND-EWASA project—UNICEF. The group will develop an overall extension strategy for climate-smart rice farming. Stakeholders will be invited to provide specialist inputs as required—including non-governmental organizations (NGOs), research institutions, farmers’ organisations, private sector representatives (smallholder and migrant workers) and gender-related sectors. They will develop the overall extension strategy and approach in close collaboration with the Ministry of Agriculture. The group will also establish a group of Master Trainers to be established out to roll out the national level training to extension staff. Master trainers are experienced extension officers who have a good understanding of climate-smart rice farming knowledge within their respective organisations and provide training of further trainers within the extension system.

152. At the provincial level, provincial representatives from RD and DoAE, local farmer organisations and local service providers will facilitate the development of provincial-level extension strategies in conjunction with the national group. The provincial group will develop a provincial-level selection of the climate-smart techniques and practices most relevant to the provincial conditions (based on the national strategy). They will also develop a provincial-level training manual and develop training modules to the local context. They will also train a pool of provincial coordinators.

153. The provincial coordinators, in turn, train district extension staff and liaise with district extension staff to ensure that they are able to provide climate-smart rice farming services to farmers (e.g., arranging local activities [peer-to-peer learning, training, field days, demonstrations, etc.]).

154. In parallel, Sub-Activity 1.1.1.2 will design and implement private sector integration into the extension work of Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1. The two sub-activities are interlinked: Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1 can take place seamlessly and coherently with the public sector extension support.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.1.2: Development, verification and transfer of new technologies for climate-smart rice farming

GCF financial instrument utilised: technical assistance grant.
Addressing: climate change adaptation and mitigation.
Execution period: 03/2024 – 02/2026.

### Sub-Activity 1.1.1.3 contains 2 strands of work:
(i) digital solution development, and (ii) verification of new agronomic technologies.

#### Digital Solution Development

Mobile phone penetration in Thailand is very high—137%, indicating that there is, on average, more than one mobile phone per person. This compares with 119% in North America and 105% in Europe.
The number of the Thai mobile subscribers increased by 3.8 million (4%) in the period from January to June 2022. The total number of mobile subscriptions is now 76.5 million, which represents a penetration rate of 137%. Of these users, 90% are using smartphones.
The average monthly internet connection speed is 31 Mbps (i.e., 3G, 4G or 5G). The top three broadband providers in Thailand are True Digital (29 Mbps), AIS (28 Mbps) and DTAC (26 Mbps).
In rural areas, the mobile app use widespread: 81% of the population uses mobile apps to access information. Mobile phone penetration is highest among young people aged between 30 years per year.
Thai spend an average of 5 hours per day on their mobile phones second only to the Philippines and far more than the global average (3 hours 45 minutes).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE 66

## Mobile Apps for Thai Farmers

### Background and Context

157. The use of mobile apps by Thai farmers is widespread, strongly encouraged by the government (notably in the context of the Thailand 4.0 strategy) [2] and offers significant agronomic and rural development potential. However, the adoption rate remains low compared to other countries such as India or China. This is partly due to a lack of awareness among farmers about the actual needs they have, partly because generating user awareness of new apps is challenging[3], and partly because there are no incentives for farmers to use these apps. The government has provided some extension support to farmers to adopt new agrotechnologies or practices.[4] The full potential of digital agriculture can only be realized if the above-mentioned challenges are addressed.

### Project Overview

158. The project will support the use of mobile digital apps and tools in a number of contexts:

* **Capacity building:** Training for farmers on how to effectively use digital tools (such as agro-met advisories and content) [content developed under Sub-Activity 2.1.1.2, 2.1.1.3 etc.]
* **Research and development:** Development of new mobile apps
* **Match-making between farmers and service providers** [Sub-Activity 2.1.1.1]
* **MPPs:** The project will support the use of MPPs in the context of the pilot phase, based on data-driven and demand-based approaches, and also in support of Sub-Activity 3.1.2.3 for carbon market (T-VER) implementation.
* **Monitoring and evaluation:**

### Sub-Activity 1.1.1.3

159. Sub-Activity 1.1.1.3 will develop a single digital solution or, if advantageous to do so (e.g., for technical reasons), multiple solutions that can be integrated into the MPPs. The project team will work with stakeholders to identify and prioritize the functions in this order: To save costs and reduce development time, the project will seek ways to build on and extend existing digital tools and services rather than developing new ones from scratch. This will include a role, notably the Mali-Som app (see Sub-Activity 1.1.3.2) and the Farmbook app [Section E.7.2], and each such app will be evaluated for its potential to be integrated into the MPPs in terms of technical system compatibility, server scalability, size of existing user base, ownership model etc.). Another potential solution is a match-making app developed by IRRI in New York (Eastmanport) that could be tailored to the Thai context.

### Detailed Assessment

160. A detailed assessment of candidates apps and discussions with relevant stakeholders will be undertaken to determine which app(s) should be prioritized for development. The criteria for selection of app development will be formulated and approved – potentially also including the development of new content, such as a mobile app for agro-met advisories and content. The project team will work on the integration of functionality for farmers. Enhancements will be placed upon simple technology from a user perspective to make it more useful and accessible. The selection process is expected to take place in Q1 2023, with the app itself taking place under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.3 while content development for the app – such as training materials and other resources – will be carried out by Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1. The farmer training under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1 will serve as a channel for app awareness-raising.

### Verification of Modern Agrotechnologies

161. A second strand of work will focus on the verification of new agrotechnologies that could support climate-smart agriculture and increase farmers’ income and their application to the local context. One aspect of this work will be devoted to drone technology. Building on a number of promising technologies already implemented by the private sector as well as the CGI-UNDP Amman project [5].

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE 67

#### B

**This project will work with farmer groups, entrepreneurs and IRI to evaluate different use-case scenarios of drones in conjunction with climate-smart rice agriculture and promote the most promising use-cases for scaling up. The evaluation will be based on a cost-benefit analysis (CBA) that takes into account elevation to guide LLI activities, and targeted fertilizer application and pesticide spraying to reduce farm input costs and increase yields.** The project will also assess the potential of drones in rice farming being used, giving attention to unintended impacts. Where drone technology is found to be feasible, effective and efficient, it will be promoted through Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1; to extension officers under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.2; and to service providers under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.3.

**162. Dry Direct Seeded Rice (DSR) is a crop management technology that offers considerable potential to farmers in terms of reduced labour input costs as well as reduced irrigation water requirements and nutrient use efficiency. The technology can be applied with different types of rice varieties, different type of machines, hill or row seeding, etc., and thus its benefits are, however, influenced by the agricultural context. In some cases, it has been found to increase yields; in other cases, it has had no effect on yield. In still other cases in the past, climate impact analysis, and dissemination network and investment needs will be assessed through a cost-benefit analysis (CBA) that takes into account elevation to guide LLI activities by the Department. Based on these findings, the technology will be rolled out to farmers – with appropriate geographical targeting and continued user guidance – through the extension work under Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1.**

**Activity 1.1.2: Enhancing farmers’ access to finance for climate-smart farming services and technologies**

**163. Climate-smart agriculture is a novelty among Thai rice farmers. They are not familiar with the technologies involved and, as a result, are unable to quantify the financial benefits and impact of these technologies on their farm income. This lack of awareness may lead some farmers – particularly those that farmers – in their decision whether to adopt climate-smart agriculture or not – may overweight the short-term costs associated with the adoption of new technologies. In order to overcome this barrier, an attractive financial return generated by climate-smart agriculture over a 5-year period (see Section D.E.S.) is required for Thai rice farmers to be convinced that they should invest in and adopt climate-smart agriculture in the context of their current farm and broader household income and allow them to make informed decisions.**

**164. Such financial capacity building may also persuade farmers of the opportunity to borrow in order to implement climate-smart rice farming, smoothing the negative impact of climate-smart technologies and practices on farm income. The majority of Thai rice farmers currently borrow from the informal or sectoral inform and literacy regarding loan products is limited. The World Bank estimates that approximately 17-20% of Thai rural households are in debt (World Bank, 2002). According to the National Statistical Office, approximately 30% of rice farming households are in debt (National Statistical Office, 2008).**

**165. Sub-Activity 1.1.2.1 will provide a suite of supportive financial training for smallholder rice farmers. The risk of borrowing is high and the lack of information on loan products and their associated costs, as well as the limited access to credit, are major barriers to adoption of climate-smart technologies and practices by Thai rice farmers. ### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE 68

#### Sub-Activity 1.1.2.1: Financial literacy for farmers

GCF financial instrument utilised: technical assistance grant  
Addressing: climate change adaptation and mitigation  

Executive Summary: 

Sub-Activity 1.1.2.1 will develop a suite of training measures. Measures will include the development of a farmer-friendly financial literacy manual offering guidance on personal financial management (including savings, loans, insurance) and investment planning. The manual will be developed in a climate-sensitive manner under Sub-Activity 1.1.2.1 and will be delivered to farmers as part of the extension training programme. A separate module on investment planning and cash flow analysis, based on investment planning and calibration, will also be developed for a sub-set of 50,000 advanced farmers (Sub-Activity 1.1.3).

The manual, investment planning materials and training will be developed by SAAIC in coordination with RD and farmer organisations, building on inputs from the Thai Credit Guarantee Corporation (TCGC) and TCGC’s Debt Clinic initiative. The manual will also draw upon lessons learned from other farmer support programmes, notably BACAI’s Literacy for Agri and TCGC’s Debt Clinic initiatives. The training programme will be developed in collaboration with SAAIC, RD, and the Thai Credit Guarantee Corporation (TCGC). In conjunction with the Thai Government Savings Bank (GSB), DSK has been involved in developing a financial literacy manual for farmers in Thailand. Drawing on this experience and material from the German-Thai Cooperation Project to Promote the Sustainable Use of Agricultural Resources in Thailand.

Both the manual and the training will focus on: 
- The basics of farm economics, with an emphasis on climate risk and climate-smart agriculture.
- How to run a small business, and how to increase income through diversification.  
- General financial literacy and awareness of the importance of proper financial planning at both farm and household level.

Analysis of the basic financial situation of a farm, with focus on revenues, investments in equipment, livestock, etc., and costs.
Analysis of the contribution of farm income to overall household income (the majority of farmers also employ family members).
Simulation of the financial effects of climate change (e.g. a drought year) on farm revenues and income.

Analysis of the financial implications of a farm’s switch to climate-smart agriculture, including the additional investment required for new equipment and inputs, as well as the expected increase in revenues, costs over five years, etc.
Analysis of the conditions under which a farm can transition to climate-smart agriculture on overall household income over 5 years, savings and ability to assume new debt (or refinance existing debt) and potential insurance coverage.

Analysis of the terms and conditions of the Climate-Smart Loan (CSL) provided by SAAIC (Sub-Activity 1.2.3), including interest rates, repayment periods, etc., as well as eligibility criteria according to terms.
Guidance on how to complete the required documents and all formalities for the BACAI CSL.

Analysis of the implications of climate-smart agriculture on insurance pay-outs (if a farmer has insurance) and/or the opportunity to expand insurance coverage. (Coordinated with Sub-Activity 2.1.1.3).

### Sub-Activity 1.1.2.2: Incentive payments to support uptake of climate-smart services and technologies

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE 69

## GCF financial instrument utilised: farmer support grants.
Addressing both climate adaptation and mitigation.

### Executing Entity: BIAG

**Sub-Activity 1.1.2.2 will provide an incentive payment – a grant payment that essentially serves as a subsidy to rice farmers to cover a partial share of the costs of their climate-smart services (see Activity 2.1.1).**

*Currently, no support payments are available that are specific to the application of climate-smart technologies and practices in Thailand. However, there is a range of existing government subsidies for farmers (of whom are themselves farmers) that have received a partial subsidy for LLI, machinery investment under the TRIS, and other agricultural inputs. These include:*

* **A high promotional rate that recognises the pilot nature of the NSP as well as the challenging post-COVID-19 environment:** This is available to all rice farmers in Thailand who have received a partial subsidy for farm subsidies, notably harvest support and the subsidies underlying the TRIS, are that are unavailable under the NSP. The total amount of this subsidy is 20 billion baht (1.00 billion € or 4.32bn $). These existing government subsidies by no means create a windfall for farmers, as they are only available to those who have received a partial subsidy and can therefore be considered barely profitable farms (see the financial model provided in Annex 3a).*

*Despite the possible financial NPR generated by climate-smart technologies and practices without subsidies, it is unlikely that this will be sufficient to cover the costs of adoption and to increase penetration of climate-smart technologies and practices among Thai rice farmers – for 2 principal reasons:*

• Climate-smart technologies and practices are an area that is completely new in Thailand, and the only applications to date being those promoted by the NSP and the other foundation projects described in Section 1.1.2.1. The adoption of climate-smart technologies and practices has been slow until there was widespread evidence of adoption and usefulness (a chicken and egg problem). Increased adoption is expected as a result of the NSP, which has demonstrated its success by the NPR generated by the NSP. Recognising the success of the NSP subsidy, in order to minimise consumer resistance, it would be important for the NSP subsidy to continue at least until 2035 at the national level.

• While producing a positive IRR over 5 years, climate-smart technologies and practices, and in particular LLL can cause a meaningful decline in farm cashflows in the first year of adoption. For instance, if a farmer adopts LLL and does not receive any subsidy for machinery investment under the TRIS, then adopting a climate-smart package including LLL would experience an 83% drop in cashflow in the first year (see the financial model provided in Annex 3a).

*The incentive payments are also designed to boost the initial uptake of the climate-smart technologies and practices and generate a catalytic peer-to-peer effect. Evidence and experience from past projects indicate that early adopters can be critical for overcoming barriers to adoption, as they are essential to achieve a successful roll-out of practices considered new or innovative. Farmers in Thailand have been shown to be more likely to adopt climate-smart technologies and practices, so incentives generated by early adopters will be critical to overcome these barriers.*

*The incentive payments provided under Sub-Activity 1.1.2.2 will be made available for 5 years of the Trial Period (see Table 3a). The incentive payment is expected to be paid after the completion of the payment scheme and commencing the relevant training for farmers. The scheme is expected to become operational after one year of project implementation and will remain in place in Years 2, 3, and 4.*

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE 71

## Sub-Activity 2.1.1: Promotion of quality assurance and match-making for climate-smart agricultural services

### GCF financial instrument utilized: technical assistance grant.

Adaptation focus: food security, adaptation and mitigation.
Execution Entity: CGI, IGRF.

In order to ensure a high quality of service provision, a quality approval process – tailored to each climate-smart technology or practice – will be established. Service providers who receive capacity building support will be eligible to apply for quality approval. Service providers will receive technical assistance in developing their business plans and marketing strategies that meet the quality standards for one or more climate-smart technologies and practices that will be listed as part of the GCF approved list of climate-smart service providers. Only registered service providers will be eligible to benefit from farmers’ incentive payments (Sub-Activity 1.2.2) and the BAAC Climate-Smart Loan (Sub-Activity 2.1.2).

In order to ensure a high quality of service provision, a quality approval process – tailored to each climate-smart technology or practice – will be established. Service providers who receive capacity building support will be eligible to apply for quality approval. Service providers will receive technical assistance in developing their business plans and marketing strategies that meet the quality standards for one or more climate-smart technologies and practices that will be listed as part of the GCF approved list of climate-smart service providers. Only registered service providers will be eligible to benefit from farmers’ incentive payments (Sub-Activity 1.2.2) and the BAAC Climate-Smart Loan (Sub-Activity 2.1.2).

**Sub-Activity 2.1.1.2: Strengthening agri-met service provision**

GIZ financial instrument utilised: technical assistance grant.
Addressed by: climate adaptation and mitigation.
Executing Entity: GIZ

* **187.** The TAD is currently piloting a programme called ‘Climate Field School’ (CFS) to demonstrate the potential of using agri-met services in decision-making accompanied by crop simulation to provide precise weather forecasting and tailored crop planning to farmers. CFS assimilates information on current crop conditions, soil moisture content, soil nutrient levels, soil temperature, soil pH, soil water balance, past meteorological conditions (e.g. temperature and rainfall in recent weeks) and future weather forecasts (e.g. temperature and rainfall for the next 10 days). The TAD has developed a mobile app that provides tailored advisories for users. Through Sub-Activity 2.1.1.2, the Thai Rice Project will collaborate with GIZ to scale up CFS in Thailand and other countries in South-East Asia where agri-met advisory services could play an essential role for rain-fed rice farming.

* **188.** Sub-Activity 2.1.1.2 incorporates 3 strands of work:
    * The first strand is the development of a tool that TAD is able to reach out to farmers during the training under Sub-Activity 1.1.1 and to extension officers under Sub-Activity 1.1.2, to build relationships with them and provide them with information on agri-met services and how they can be used.
    * In order to expand the scope of CFS so that it can address the specific needs of climate-smart rice farmers in Thailand, TAD will develop a mobile app under Sub-Activity 2.1.1.3 to provide support data-sharing, as appropriate. TAD will then (further) develop advisories, or inputs to advisory services, for farmers who are implementing ASWD, SSIWM, alternative rice varieties and the other climate-smart technologies.
    * Content development for the mobile app developed under Sub-Activity 1.1.3 to supplement the existing CFS content will be based on the needs of climate-smart rice farmers in Thailand, the advisories and the availability of a searchable library of good practice guidance for climate-smart rice farming. The app will also provide information on how to access training opportunities, technical literacy, technical skills or simple life to absorb detailed text.

* **189.** The emphasis will be on improving the so-called ‘last mile’, which is group-oriented delivery of high-quality advisory services to individual farmers and smallholder farmers – so that they are able to make better on-farm decisions. In addition to TAD, RD and DAEK (Sub-Activity 2.1.1.2 will harness the climate-smart advisory service provided by GIZ in Thailand). The data collected from farmers and used to refine TAD’s modelling and advisories under Sub-Activity 2.1.2 will be shared with GIZ, RD and DAEK for use in their respective advisory services. This will enable service providers to develop new products for climate-smart farmers and by GIZ/STARR for development of crop insurance in the context of rice farming on index-based crop insurance (see Sub-Activity 2.1.1.3).

## Sub-Activity 2.1.1.3: Improving insurance services offered to rice farmers

### GCF funding instrument utilised: technical assistance grant.
Addressing: climate change adaptation.

Executive Summary:

190. In addition to reducing GHG emissions from the Thai rice sector, Activities 1.1.1 and 1.1.2 serve to reduce smallholder farmers' risk exposure to climate change. Sub-Activity 2.1.1.3 complements these risk reduction efforts by increasing access to insurance services in this sector through a voluntary insurance market.

### Background

191. Since 2011, a number of insurance companies in Thailand initially (Dhopay, Thavoloid and Viriya) have offered voluntary rice insurance products are not available throughout the country.
- Rice Insurance Scheme (TRIS) managed by the TDA and distributed via BAAC.
- The TRIS seeks to provide coverage against yield losses due to droughts, floods, diseases and pests. Tier 1 premium mures basic insurance coverage is subsidised by 100% by the TDA.
- Tier 2 voluntary top-up insurance covers additional risks for non-Tier 1 farmers.
However, Tier 2 voluntarily purchased top-up insurance is not subsidised.

### Awareness

192. Awareness of the rice insurance scheme is low among farmers, and it is regarded as some people consider it a waste of money. The current insurance scheme is restricted to in-season (main season) rice only – despite the fact that drought, pests and floods can occur at any time during the growing season.
- The TDA has a reputation for being bureaucratic and overly rigid.
- It also not well equipped to address anticipated future climate change impacts on agriculture.

### Challenges

193. Hence, Sub-Activity 2.1.1.3 focuses on short-term interventions that increase the efficiency and customer value of the existing TRIS model (focused on in-season rice), accompanied by a medium-term focus on transitioning to an index-based [parametric] model.

## Short-Term Focus: Improving the Existing TRIS Model

### Challenges Faced by Farmers

194. One of the challenges faced by the TRIS is farmers' low awareness of the benefits of insurance as a climate risk transfer tool when disaster occurs.
- Take-up rate of Tier 2 (voluntary insurance) remains very low, despite the fact that it provides coverage against multiple risks and enhances overall resilience to climate change.
- To ensure their absorption capacity for climate shocks is improved and also to ensure they are not exposed to adverse selection, farmers need to be made aware of the benefits of insurance on a continuous basis.

### Interventions

195. Sub-Activity 2.1.1.3 will develop or build on existing training materials and materials that explicitly make links between climate change adaptation and insurance; and specifically, the benefits available to farmers from transitioning increasing climate-driven risk management practices into the financial training materials developed under Sub-Activity 1.1.2.1.
- Climate risk insurance literacy training for farmers will be provided through extension officers.

### Farmers (Delivered through Sub-Activity 1.1.1.1) - with support from BAAC and other relevant partners, farmers will be trained on the benefits of insurance and its application in business based on climate change adaptation.

### Agricultural extension officers (Delivered through Sub-Activity 1.1.1.2) - with support from TDA and Oxfam.
- The benefits of an enhanced app for purchasing insurance, reporting damage and receiving insurance information will also be assessed.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE 74

## Medium Term Vision: Translating the TRIS Model

### Loss assessment under the TRIS is currently conducted manually by DoM extension officers and local insurance companies, which is time-consuming and prone to errors in electronic programmes. This is also consuming – leading to delays in insurance pay-outs - and introduces an element of subjectivity in the process.

197. The proposed system will be more efficient and accurate as it will be automated and enhanced; if the system transitions to an index-based system, Insurance cover that is triggered by a specific event (e.g., droughts, floods) can be provided at lower cost than traditional insurance. Droughts and heatwaves become triggers whose indices can be linked to various parameters (Indices – such as soil water content, temperature, rainfall, etc.) which are then used to determine the extent of losses in defined geographical areas. Insurance pay-outs can be automatic and immediate if the proposed system is implemented.

### Sub-Activity 2.1.1.3 will build on the baseline initiatives and, through collaboration with GSTDA, TMD, DAF, and other relevant stakeholders, support TRIS stakeholders to develop an index-based insurance model that, for example:

* Is technology-enabled and automated with minimal human intervention.
* Has the confidence and support of all the relevant TRIS stakeholders.
* Builds on existing data management systems and infrastructure for the project to enable high-resolution geographical price differentiation of insurance premiums, such that farmers who are less at risk pay lower prices than those who are more exposed. This will also allow farmers in drought-prone areas to pay lower prices for insurance than more climate-exposed farmers.[27] Data management modalities will be compliant with the Personal Data Protection Act (PDA) and other relevant laws. The elements of this work and all data management will be compliant with the Personal Data Protection Act (PDA).

* Possibly incorporates modules or product development on areas currently not insured, such as extension services.

* Is accompanied by an agreed roadmap for transitioning the current indemnity-based TRIS model to an index-based system in a phased manner. This could be done over 5 years of project implementation period, resulting in partial application or piloting of index-based insurance during the project implementation phase and full transition after the project completion.[28] The roadmap will include milestones (as contained in the 13th National Economic and Social Development Plan; NESP3; see Section B.1.3.1) for transitioning to an index-based system.

### Activity 2.1.2: BAAC climate-smart and farmer-friendly financial product development and roll-out

198. The adoption of climate-smart technologies will reduce farm cash-inflows in the first year of implementation, especially if LFF services are part of the suite of technologies chosen by a farmer. Unless there is an increase in farm income from other sources (e.g., livestock), farmers may not benefit from the subsequent large increase in farm income expected in Years 2-5 of climate-smart non-finance products. Therefore, it will be important to ensure that Sub-Activity 2.1.1 does not deliver the first year cash decline but will completely eliminate it.

### The BAAC Climate Smart Loan (CSL) scheme (see sub-Activity 2.1.2 in this document) will allow producers to access credit for investments related to climate-smart technologies – such as the grace period in the first year and subsequent amortisation over 4 years with pre-determined interest rates. The CSL scheme is designed specifically to support farmers’ access to farm loan products, none has terms specifically designed to meet the needs (e.g., a 5-year time horizon, large value loans) of climate-smart technologies related to climate smart agriculture. The BAAC CSL scheme will function based on farmer demand.

[27] Policy options (e.g., a price cap regime) to protect high-risk farmers from excessively high insurance prices will also be explored.
[28] In addition to the roadmap, other stakeholders such as the National Insurance Commission, the Kenya Agricultural and Livestock Insurance Corporation, have expressed interest in developing and offering such extreme event insurance to rice farmers who practice climate smart agriculture.

## Sub-Activity 2.1.2: Climate-smart agricultural lending by BAAC

### GCF financial instrument utilized: none (entirely BAAC co-finance)
Addressed through: Adaptation and mitigation.
Entry title: BAAC.

**201.** BAAC call and credit officers are currently not knowledgeable on climate smart agricultural technologies and their impact on cash flow patterns at the farm level, and are therefore unable to evaluate the potential of these technologies for increasing farmers' income or reducing costs.

Because of their lack of knowledge, they are also unable to proactively recommend climate-smart loan products to farmers. This is particularly true in rural areas where credit officers have limited experience, especially of credit officers at provincial branches, which results in limited uptake of the CSL, depriving farmers and service providers interested in climate-smart rice farming of 4 meaningful amount of funding.

### Sub-Activity 2.1.2.1: Climate-smart agricultural lending by BAAC

**202.** BAAC will provide a CSL credit line of up to Euro 50,000*, to be disbursed on a demand basis to farmers who are willing and able to adopt at least one (out of 7) of the Thai Rice Project's supported climate-smart agricultural technologies and practices.* The terms of the CSL will be concessional and will include an interest rate of 2% per year, with a grace period of up to five years. This is a new investment in the first year, followed by an increase in farm income (net of climate-smart agriculture costs) over time. Farmers will have access to various loan packages including credit-linked service packages that include LLS services, which are especially expensive in the first year of application.

**203.** The precise terms of the CSL will be confirmed at project inception, but are expected to feature: * Use of a revolving fund for disbursement of loans; * A grace period of up to five years from the implementation of 7 of the project's climate-smart agricultural technologies and practices (Table 13); * Average loan amount per farmer is estimated at Euro 60,000 (~Euro 1,620); * 5 year maturity with a 1-year grace period; * Repayment in equal monthly installments over Year 5, with the installment payment due – in the case of former loans – after each main harvest season; * Concessional interest rate of 2%.
