## B

### Sub-Activity 2.1.2: Climate-smart lending capacity development for BAAC

**GCF financial instrument utilized:** technical assistance grant.

**Addressed thematic area:** change adaptation and mitigation.

**Executing Entity:** GSZ

#### 207. Sub-Activity 2.1.2 will enable BAAC and its staff to:

* Understand the climate-smart agriculture (CSA) enabling smart-rice agriculture technologies and practices at farm level.
* Fully implement the CSA Cycles of the BAAC CSL scheme described in Sub-Activity 2.1.3.1.
* Coherently explain to farmers and service providers how the CSL schemes can then help them to fund a climate-smart agricultural transformation, including through the use of credit instruments.
* Better monitor the risks associated with the BAAC CSL portfolio after loans are disbursed.
* Understand the needs of smallholder farmers and other vulnerable groups of farmers who are currently under-served by financial products (notably: women farmers).
* Develop a set of risk management tools and social safeguards, for instance, in relation to occupational health and biodiversity conservation.

#### 208. BAAC's loan activities are designed to be supportive of the government’s agricultural objectives. Until now, these have been focused on rice production, with a particular emphasis on the development of high-yielding varieties (HYVs) and hybrid seeds. With the introduction of the government’s Bio-Circular-Green (BCG) Economy Policy in 2011*, which forms part of the National Development Plan (NDP), BAAC has been required to change its focus, and is now changing. The BCG Agriculture and Food Focus Area has 4 targets (2021-2030): (i) increase agricultural production by 50%; (ii) increase the number of farmers with access to credit from 6 million to 2 million; (iii) increase the proportion of high-value (premium) products to 30%; and (iv) increase the number of smallholder farmers in agribusiness. To this end, BAAC has been developing a new set of CSA Cycles, which are designed to support these targets. The CSA Cycles are climate-smart lending instruments that are directly linked to the BCG objectives. They are also aligned with the GCF’s Strategic Objective 2: “Supporting countries in their efforts to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and adapt to climate change.” Indirectly, they are illustrative of the profound strategic reorientation that BAAC is undergoing.

#### 209. As a result of Sub-Activity 2.1.2, BAAC will be able to continue, expand and adapt the CSL scheme as well as other existing or new lending products, as needed after the Thai Rice Project, in order to support the government’s BCG objectives. The project will also contribute to the development of other agricultural sub-sectors. In this regard, the project will also complete a comprehensive data set

## B

### Sub-Activity 3.1.1: Development of the sustainable rice and biomass residue markets

#### GCF financial instrument utilized: technical assistance grant.

Addressing: climate change adaptation and mitigation.
Execution period: 2021–2025
Sustainable Rice

### 214. The project will also support the development of a Thai Rice Standard (TRS), with support from GIZ and the Management System Certification Institute (MASCIS). Currently, it developed the Thai Agricultural Competitiveness Enhancement Fund (TACEF) in collaboration with GIZ until end-2023. AFCS and ROD have received early interest from domestic companies and farmer groups seeking to adopt the TRS. It is expected that the TRS will be recognized as equivalent to selected international systems (e.g., the GIZ Fair Trade Rice Standard). The project will support the development of a market for Thai farmers to purchase sustainable rice certified according to a recognized standard. Sub-Activity 3.1.1 will lead to the establishment of a sustainable rice market.

### 215. With regard to biomass residues, there is a significant opportunity to: (i) promote technologies for on-farm management of rice straw and stubble (e.g., ploughing, aggregation) to farmer groups and/or DMS; (ii) provide training to farmers (e.g., transforming rice straw into source of pellets); to potentially increase the sales price and reduce the cost of collection and transportation; (iii) establish a network of local collectors and networks to match demand and supply of aggregated rice straw; (iv) engage high-readiness farmer groups, DMS, and other stakeholders in developing a business plan for the value chain; (v) collect, clean, store, transform and/or sell (transformed) rice straw or to utilize it locally within the community; (vi) promote the use of biomass residues as feedstock for biogas production. Under the Gender Action Plan (Annexes Baa and c), there is a particular opportunity with regard to local women’s groups in terms of income generation from the collection, storage, and sale of rice straw or use - bio-fertilizer from a mix of cow dung and vegetation (typically grasses). Rice straw represents a viable alternative for women who are already involved in other activities. By supplementing the total incomes of women, this will serve to reduce the need for (GIZ) granting development aid.

### 216. Market-driven solutions that engage rice value chain actors – including service providers, technology producers, agricultural input providers, private sector actors interested in by-products (sub-value chains), and consumers – are essential to ensure a sustainable market for biomass residues from the consumer. Sub-Activity 3.1.2 will expand engagement of private sector actors across a range of activities, including the development of new markets and value chains; the promotion of existing markets and in relation to consumer awareness campaigns to boost demand for, and the value of, climate-smart rice and biomass residues.

### 217. Four mutually supportive strands of work will be undertaken to develop sustainable rice markets:

- Development of materials for farmer training and TAS certification, thereby creating a large body of farmers who not only practice climate-smart agriculture but who do so according to a recognized standard. The development of the training curriculum and materials for farmers will be designed for roll-out jointly with ROD and DOATL.

## Biomass Residues

### Straw
218. Currently (in the baseline), burning of straw is common and the prevailing practice in the North and, partially, the North-East. On those occasions when burning is not practised, a common use of high-quality rice straw is as animal feed. The average yield of straw from paddy fields in Thailand can generate 20-35 kg/bale, sellable at 20 baht to 20.50 baht (USD 0.5-0.8 per bale) on average.
The government has been promoting the use of rice straw as a bio-fertilizer and in organic-fed rice production systems have an advantage over irrigated areas in this respect, as rain-fed systems are more prone to nutrient leaching during the rainy season. However, when there is a cropping season in irrigated areas, they tend to produce high-quality rice straw. There is not yet a commercial market for rice straw as a bio-fertilizer or other uses such as animal feed or energy. The use of biomass for energy applications, but have not yet found a viable business model that also generates income for farmers. In some cases, the straw is left to decompose in-field or can be brought underground to support soil management.
219. Based on international practices, IRRI contributions and experiences, and a soon-to-commence GIZ- ASEAN project (see below), there are several potential business models that could provide viable options for the usage of collected rice straw: stables as alternatives to burning. Details for each alternative will be provided in the following sections. These include the use of rice straw in paper production from rice straw pulp, biochar production, etc., will be termed; for example, their required inputs and outputs, the process involved, the costs and benefits associated with it, and the end product. Options currently perceived to have the highest viability for scale-up include the production of animal feed (e.g., cattle bedding), use in organic farming systems, use as a bio-fertilizer, cellulose extraction or the production of bio-fertilizer from rice straw (i.e. through composting or advanced technologies such as anaerobic digestion) and its use to improve soil fertility, water retention and carbon and water retention properties.
The economic viability of different uses will be clearly outlined in the following sections. The project will also work with public agencies and private sector partners to scale-up viable business models for rice straw valorization.

### Project Implementation
220. The project will also set up or incorporate this approach in an easy-to-use, data-matching platform that can be used by farmers, extension officers, and other stakeholders. Public and private financing partners (such as MARIS, ECHO and PlanCongo) will facilitate linking up farmers with off-takers of rice straw for the production of bio-fertilizers and other products (i.e., quality-assured climate-smart service providers), including straw management practices.

### Additional Information
221. As part of the new "Rice Straw Sustainable Use of Rice Straw" project an ASEAN Cooperation Project that will be implemented by GIZ in cooperation with IRRI, Thailand will be the pilot country and will be on Thailand, with a broader regional perspective thereafter.
Implementation will be for 1.5 years during 2023 and 2024.

### Sub-Activity 3.1.2: Linkages to private sector partners, value chains and public-private partnerships

#### GCF financial instrument utilized: technical assistance grant.

Addressing climate change adaptation and mitigation.
Executing Entity: GIZ

**221.** Stable business linkages between rice farmers and downstream value chain actors, such as processors, traders, and consumers, represent a challenge to the development of resilient and sustainable rice and rice residues, as farmers have little assurance that higher quality rice (and associated residues) will be purchased at fair prices or sold in markets where they can influence prices and behaviors (for instance, transparency of production and processing practices). As a result, there is currently a lack of incentives for farmers to invest in climate-smart technologies, improved varieties, and guaranteed quality; sustainability-minded consumers are less likely to generate the demand needed to support these investments.

**222.** Given this low degree of market organization, farmers are generally in a weak position within the value chain, constrained by limited access to know-how and impartial technical advice; limited bargaining power with processors and traders; lack of access to credit (and/or high-interest loans), or even debt; limited availability of good-quality farm inputs, machinery and services; and a general absence of reliable information on market prices. This is particularly true for smallholder farmers.

Concurrently, there is increasing pressure on farmers to modernize and adopt production systems, especially in the face of climate change impacts.

**223.** Under Sub-Activity 3.1.2, the supply-side interventions promoted under Outputs 1.1 and 2.1 to drive best practice adoption of climate-smart technologies and practices in Thai rice farming will be complemented by a series of market strengthening measures.

The project will engage with domestic and international off-takers, building their understanding of, and commitment to, the need for sustainable rice production and the importance of supporting smallholder farmers. The project will also partner with retailers, sources of sustainably produced rice and public agencies, such as the Department of Agriculture, to build their understanding of sustainable rice (TAS and SRP labelled rice in particular). Support will include reinforcing existing market linkages between farmers and processors/traders, building farmer capacity (or equivalent), off-taker buyer support, and the development of a market development strategy for TAS-certified rice. The project will also work with key stakeholders to develop a marketing plan that will boost consumer demand for sustainable rice both locally and on the global market, which will, in turn, further incentivize farmers to adopt climate-smart practices and technologies. This will directly contribute to the long-term sustainability and exit strategy of the project.

**224.** The project will also develop more extensive – and deeper – link value chain partners, including public agencies, processors, traders, retailers, and consumers, to ensure that the adoption of improved practices and best practices on off-taking arrangements will be completed and published, as appropriate.

**225.** As an integral element of these market strengthening measures, the project will work with MoA, other relevant government agencies, and private sector actors to develop a market development strategy (including export promotion) strategy for that sustainable rice, with a particular emphasis on TAS-certified rice and the linkages (and potential gaps) between TAS, SRP and sustainable rice standards.

### Activity 3.1.2: Strengthening the enabling institutional and policy environment for climate-smart rice farming

**Under Activity 3.1.2,** the Thai Rice Project will assist government bodies to enhance the institutional, policy and regulatory environment for climate-sustainable rice agriculture. The principal strands of this work will be:

- **Policy support:** This strand of work will focus on strengthening capabilities relating to MRV to support NDC implementation and reporting in the rice sector. This strand of work directly responds to Thailand's 2nd Updated NDC request for

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE B2

## Sub-Activity 3.1.2: Policy support for climate-smart agriculture

### GCF financial instrument utilised: technical assistance grant
Addressing: climate change adaptation and mitigation
Executing Entity: GIZ, RRI

#### 227. Sub-Activity 3.1.2.1: Policy support to assist the Thai government in shifting agricultural policies and programmes onto a climate-smart development trajectory.
Policy support will focus on national-level linkages between agriculture and other sectors, such as energy, water, and transport. At provincial level, policy support includes sub-national initiatives, such as provincial marketing or climate finance strategies and projects. At district levels, the project will provide technical assistance to district governments in developing district-level plans for climate-smart agriculture. At sub-district levels it is starting up build-up; it can be supported through exchange of good practices at the sub-district level. A policy analysis and strategy support upon request, with the aim of supporting at least 5 such initiatives.

#### 228. The project will also support the implementation of the MAAP Climate Change Strategic Plan for Agriculture (CCSPA) in Thailand. This plan is a key component of Thailand’s NDCs and significantly contributes to the Strategic Plan's key performance indicators relating to GHG emission reduction, adaptation, and resilience. The CCSPA has been developed by RRI in collaboration with the Rice Department and GIZ. A preliminary database has been developed for analyzing GHG emissions from paddy fields, accompanied by trained rice researchers who can conduct field measurements. In addition, a preliminary database of soil carbon stocks and emission fluxes have been determined for irrigated and non-irrigated rice cultivation systems. The Sourcebook on Climate-Smart Agriculture in Thailand has also been developed by RRI in collaboration with the Rice Department and GIZ.

#### 229. GHG emissions tracking in the Thai rice sector has benefited from a number of recent baseline development activities, including the establishment of a database for analyzing GHG emissions from paddy fields, accompanied by trained rice researchers who can conduct field measurements. In addition, a preliminary database of soil carbon stocks and emission fluxes have been determined for irrigated and non-irrigated rice cultivation systems.

#### 230. The Thai Rice Project will extend the M&E system from its current coverage of 6 Central Provinces to provinces in Thailand’s 21 provinces in the Central Plains, the North and the North-East. Further, it will upgrade the M&E system in a number of important respects.

* The project will work with the Rice Department, particularly its Division of Rice Research and Development (BRID), to increase RD capabilities to lead MRV in the rice sector. ## Conversion of new methodologies (with AWS, SSIM and SBFM identified as immediate priorities) to a simplified and more flexible approach to multi-methodology projects (to allow farmers to implement multiple methodologies in the same project). This will also enable the use of different sampling methods for different numbers of farms, the use of standardised baselines to simplify mitigation estimation, simplified M&E (MRV) procedures, and the development of a new methodology for carbon sequestration through soil sampling™ and an alternative approach to additionally (combined with linkages to the TAS – see below). This will enable the creation of a flexible framework that can be used by any project developer to create a project that meets the needs of T-VER Rice buyers (see below) - and hence will catalyse a additional, long-term increase in demand for carbon credits from the sector. The new methodologies will also address some of the circumstances of the sector.

### 233. The project will enable Thai farmers to separately register with the T-VER Rice Scheme as an autonomous entity (i.e., not under the umbrella of a larger scheme). The adoption of this approach is essential, since certification is undoubtedly a significant deviation from the business-as-usual baseline. Such additional costs are likely to be prohibitive for smallholder farmers who may not have access to credit or farming or, but it facilitate fraction-less entry into the carbon market for those who do. As TAS certified farmers will be able to sell their credits at a premium price (such as premium prices for their rice and greater resilience to climate change), this linkage between the TAS and the T-VER Rice Scheme is essential.

### 234. Approximately 50,000 farmers will receive training on the TAS and TAS certification under Sub-Activity 1.1.1. These farmers will also receive training on the T-VER Rice Scheme so that they are aware of not only the carbon market but also the wider benefits of the T-VER Rice Scheme.

### The T-VER Rice training materials will be developed under Sub-Activity 3.1.2.5 and delivered to farmers in a number of ways, including through farmer field schools, extension officers, etc., and will provide a number of additional benefits, notably (i) data sharing and MRV synergies between the TAS and T-VER Rice Scheme; (ii) increased awareness of the wider benefits of the T-VER Rice Scheme beyond just supporting emission reductions but also strong sustainable development co-benefits (gender considerations, improved livelihoods, food security, health, education, enhanced water quality, etc.).

### 235. Approximately 50 trainees will be trained for the T-VER Rice Scheme so that emission reductions can be easily assessed according to the Scheme’s rules and monitoring. Verifiers – who are typically auditors and accountants - under the existing T-VER Scheme are independent and certified by TOG. Those involved in the new T-VER Rice Scheme will be independently certified by the TAS. Farmers will be offered training, as will the TAS auditors who were trained under Sub-Activity 3.1.1.1.

### 236. The project will work with TOG and other key stakeholders, notably SABC and AGC to ensure that existing T-VER Rice Scheme methodologies are adapted for the new T-VER Rice Scheme’s objectives and modalities of the T-VER Rice Scheme. The broad elements are currently envisaged as follows:

#### TAS-certified farmers will, through their climate-smart farming practices, generate emission reductions in accordance with the MRV procedures of the T-VER Rice Scheme. This will be approximately 10 kg CO2 per year, depending upon the size of the farm and the suite of climate-smart practices adopted by the farmer. The MRV procedures are currently under development for farmers.

#### Emission reductions will be subject to T-VER Rice Scheme MRV and verification procedures, which will, to the extent possible, build on and utilise technologies (e.g., remote sensing, drones, mobile apps) that have been developed in the context of other carbon projects. The project will also provide products, NDC support and other project-supplied services.

#### The T-VER Rice Scheme will use a carbon credit exchange platform, FTX (see Section B.1.3), to set up an online market for Rice-TVERS.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 | PAGE B5

## The Rice-T-VERs will be issued in line with total verified emission reductions (across the thousands of participating farmers). These Rice-T-VERs will be tradable on the global carbon market, such as the Verified Carbon Standard (VCS) and the Gold Standard (GS), or on the FTX exchange. They will be purchased by carbon buyers (see below). The resulting carbon revenue will be used to support the project’s activities, including: (i) ongoing support to the Rice-T-VERs pilot phase; (ii) ongoing support for the development of the Carbon Support List (CSL) scheme, potentially targeting specific beneficiary types who would not usually qualify for BAAC loans or other forms of financial assistance (e.g., smallholder farmers living in remote farming households – which tend to be poorer than the average farming household, etc.).

### During project implementation, the project will identify, engage and work with (inter alia):

- International institutions, such as the World Business Council for Sustainable Development (WBCSD) and the Carbon Disclosure Project (CDP).
- Carbon market standards, such as the Verified Carbon Standard (VCS) and the Gold Standard (GS), or on the FTX exchange.
- Private sector partners to identify rice-relevant methodologies and to generate emission reductions, economic benefits, and co-benefits for smallholder farmers in their respective countries. Proof-of-concept pilots, generating farmer-owned carbon credits in a ring-fenced setting will be developed with private sector partners to demonstrate the viability of these approaches for scaling.
- National stakeholders who are interested in purchasing Rice-T-VERs. Some of the project’s current proof-of-concept pilot participants have expressed interest in participating as buyers.

### These activities have the objectives of developing the supply and demand-sides of the market, ensuring robust carbon accounting and sustainable development standards, and achieving international competitiveness for the Rice-T-VERs.

## The Rice-T-VERs will be issued as a voluntary carbon market mechanism. Like existing T-VERs, Rice-T-VERs may be eligible for use for domestic offsetting purposes (details and use cases to be clarified with the relevant government authorities). In addition, the Rice-T-VERs will be used to offset other emissions of greenhouse gases that are offset, will be promoted to buyers as a preferred option if there is no corresponding adjustment required. Buyers will not be eligible for offsetting use – and hence there will be no need for corresponding adjustments.

## The project will work with the relevant government institutions responsible for the national GHG inventory system and the rice sector MRV system to ensure that the uses of the Rice-T-VERs (referred to as “Rice-T-VERs”) are consistent with these systems.

### The intention is to have all the elements in place for successful, full-scale T-VER Scheme roll-out by the end of the Rice Trial Project. While T-VER Scheme operations will commence at limited scale during the pilot phase (as described above), the project will work hard to improve them, require Rice-T-VERs, except those generated in the proof-of-concept pilots, will be subject to a quality assurance process and will be used for offsetting purposes only. The reduction contribution conflicts between the GCF and the T-VER Scheme while providing sufficient time for the pilot phase to demonstrate its viability.

# Activity 3.1: Thai Rice Facility and ThaiIC implementation

## Sub-Activity 3.1.3: Design and operationalisation of the Thai Rice Facility

### GFOF financial instrument utilised: technical assistance grant.

Addressing: climate change adaptation and mitigation.
Execution period: 2018–2024

#### 241. Under Activity 3.1, the Thai Rice Project will create and operationalise the Thai Rice Facility, initially as a working group under the project management unit (PMU). The Thai Rice Facility is a multi-institutional initiative that brings together and coordinates the financial instruments supported by the project – the incentive payment for climate-smart agriculture (Sub-Activity 3.1.2), the TRIS products and evolutionary nudges (Sub-Activity 3.1.2.1), and the ThaiIC climate-smart agricultural projects (Sub-Activity 3.1.3.2) – as well as promoting alignment between key rice value chain actors, including farmers, input suppliers, traders, processors, and financial institutions; climate-smart farming; enhancing synergies between the TAP and index-based insurance; and harmonising with other national initiatives.

#### 242. The Thai Rice Facility is intended to be a lean body that does not reproduce (and/or take existing) institutional bureaucracy but, rather, acts as a peer-to-peer voluntary coordination mechanism that serves as an advisory board for the project. It will also provide a platform for dialogue and decision-making and build self-driven momentum for innovation and reform. The project will gradually hand over the responsibility of managing the Thai Rice Facility to the relevant national authority (e.g., the Chief or BAC) of the project in order to ensure its long-term sustainability after the end project.

#### 243. The Environmental Fund Division (EFD) of ONEP will implement ThaiIC with an additional (now) environment-friendly approach, which is expected to be more effective and efficient than the current resources. EFD will also participate in the Thai Rice Facility management process, giving EFD experience in climate-smart agriculture and other related areas.

#### 244. The institutions implementing the project’s financial instruments will be represented in the Thai Rice Facility working group, alongside NSAC; farmer representatives; private sector representatives and other stakeholders. The NSAC has expressed an interest in climate-smart agriculture, such as KasikornBank and Krungthai Bank as development partners. In addition to exchange with GCF project FP199 in Cambodia on the Farmer-led Agricultural Research Mechanism (FARM), the Thai Rice Facility will also be a platform for knowledge sharing between NSAC and FARM.

#### 245. A key role will be to oversee the transition of carbon finance into the T-River Scheme – from sub-national level to national level, with the aim that the Thai Rice Facility will become an effective financial driver of mitigation action in the rice sector (after the project). The Thai Rice Facility will also act as an advisory role for NSAC on climate-smart agriculture and other related topics (Sub-Activity 3.1.2) and provide technical assistance to farmers (Sub-Activity 3.1.2.1), including export production and market linkage services (Sub-Activity 3.1.2). The Thai Rice Facility will also be a platform for developing knowledge, sharing, and disseminating climate-smart agricultural practices and financing in the rice sector.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V 10.0 PAGE B7

## Sub-Activity 3.1.3.2: EFD strengthening and ThaCi implementation

### GCF financial instrument utilised: technical assistance grant and investment grant

Addressing climate change adaptation and mitigation.

**Executing Entity:** ONEP

246. **EFD**, a department within ONEP, occupies a unique position in Thailand's public finance architecture. It is responsible for the development of policies on environmental protection, conservation, sustainable land restoration and utilisation of natural resources. EFD supported interventions can include: (i) capacity development; (ii) project preparation; (iii) project implementation; (iv) technical assistance to private sector participation in production and consumption; and (v) supporting mitigation and adaptation actions, including the ThaCi climate-smart rice projects.

247. **Sub-Activity 3.1.3.2** will support EFD in two respects: (i) it will strengthen EFD's capacities as a complement to ongoing GCF readiness support; and (ii) it will give GCF grant resources to be channelled into the ThaCi climate-smart rice projects.

### ThaCi Implementation

248. The Thai Rice Project will facilitate peer-to-peer learning exchanges between EFD and the project's Executing Entities, as well as GIIZ as AE. It will enhance EFD's capacities to set up and run ThaCi and develop a sustainable institutional framework for the implementation of ThaCi in line with the GCF. In order to avoid overlaps with ongoing GCF readiness projects, which are mainly focused on the capacity development of ONEP and its departments, Sub-Activity 3.1.3.2 will focus on funds management, cooperation with the private sector, and monitoring, evaluation and reporting according to the GCF standards and requirements.

#### ThaCi Implementation

249. ThaCi is currently able to provide grant support to small-scale, innovative projects in the electricity and transport sectors. With support provided under Sub-Activity 3.1.3.2, EFD will be provided with the technical assistance needed to expand ThaCi's grant support to small-scale agriculture.

250. Further, The Thai Rice Project will assist ONEP with a total of 95 million GCF grant funds so that it can operate within ThaCi's newly expanded sectoral scope by providing technical assistance and capacity building for the implementation of ThaCi projects. A portion of the overall impacts achieved by the project in terms of land benefits (reclaimed and cultivated) will be attributed to the support provided under Sub-Activity 3.1.3.2. Approximately 10–15 projects will be supported by The Thai Rice Project. All supported ThaCi projects will have a duration of up to three years, with an average project duration of two years per period, and will generally have a duration of 18-36 months. Entities that are legally eligible to receive funding under the GCF must meet one or more of the following eligibility criteria: (i) must be public or private environmental organizations; village committees, the Community Network Councils, academic institutions, research institutes, or other non-profit organizations; (ii) must have a legal status and must have public benefit status. For private corporations, the project must aim to achieve social benefits for the community and/or environment. The project must be implemented by a corporation; *d* must keep accounts and the bookkeeping system must enable a third party to gain overview of the project's financial situation at any time; (e) must have an independent audit; (f) have internal and external control systems, as well as the executives who have not broken the law and/or are not subject to criminal proceedings. In addition to meeting these eligibility criteria and standard GCF conditions, applicants will be required to meet a set of additional project imposed requirements informed by the project's ECO activities.

This will ultimately contribute to a paradigm shift at the regional level.

## Organic rice farming

The promotion of sugarcane production would require the implementation of appropriate regulations and incentives to overcome considerable obstacles to crop switching – including the need for farmers to acquire knowledge about production and markets that could make them more competitive.

Rice is a staple source of calories and micronutrients, such as iron, zinc, magnesium, selenium, vitamin B6, acid, Phosphorus, Magnesium, etc., for over 3 billion people. Rice accounts for 19% of daily energy globally (20). Currently, rice production meets global demand by 2050 and the limited scope available for expansion in arable land is not sufficient to meet projected increases in demand.

The Thai government has identified climate change challenges as addressed, to a large extent, through its national food security supply. This is critically important in the context of Thailand’s vulnerability to rice shortages (19) and also acknowledges that rice production must be sustainable beyond just a subsistence or cash crop income. The proposed project will address this by allowing organic farming.

The number of organic farmers in Thailand has increased steadily over time – from 40,000 in 2005 to more than 378,000 in 2019 (1). However, shifting to organic rice farming is a complex process that requires the use and access to production inputs. This is why The Thai Rice Project needs to address.

Organic rice farming offers benefits (gimmick free, no chemical fertilizers or pesticides), but it also has its challenges – input costs are less clear.

Instead, the Thai Rice Project will focus on the TAS and other initiatives for rice cultivation.

The project aims to reduce – and better manage – the environmental impacts of organic rice production in a framework that is

## Changing the rice planting date

### Shifting the crop calendar: a bold and practical response to changing seasonality

The gains from shifting rice planting dates are marginal in Thailand, where the climate is already quite favorable for rice growth.123 In most provinces, rainfall patterns dictate moving the planting date forward to avoid the wet season, which coincides with periods of high temperature and increasing water requirements.

### More than 40% of farms in Thailand are operated by older farmers (defined as those aged over 50 years). Younger farmers may lack the necessary skills and adaptability to master new rice-planting techniques.

The Thai agricultural labour force is declining annually at a rate of 2.0–3.4%, having fallen from 18% in 1976 to just 10% today. Many older farmers (and, instead, opting for urban or better-paid jobs) are leaving their farms. It equates to all agricultural sub-sectors.

To address the ageing profile of farmers, it is necessary to encourage more young people to enter agriculture and to provide them with the skills they need to be successful. The average age of a farmer in Thailand is, in fact, higher than that of any other country in Southeast Asia. Moreover, the lessons from other countries are clear: there must be fundamental reforms relating to, inter alia, land tenure, access to credit and ownership, education, rural development and infrastructure, and the need for greater collaboration with determined government action, revitalising the agricultural sector.

### Addressing farmer demographics

The Thai agricultural labour force is declining annually at a rate of 2.0–3.4%, having fallen from 18% in 1976 to just 10% today. Many older farmers (and, instead, opting for urban or better-paid jobs) are leaving their farms.

To address the ageing profile of farmers, it is necessary to encourage more young people to enter agriculture and to provide them with the skills they need to be successful. The average age of a farmer in Thailand is, in fact, higher than that of any other country in Southeast Asia. Moreover, the lessons from other countries are clear: there must be fundamental reforms relating to, inter alia, land tenure, access to credit and ownership, education, rural development and infrastructure, and the need for greater collaboration with determined government action, revitalising the agricultural sector.

The Thai Rice Project will support a range of activities that directly or indirectly increase the attractiveness of rice farming among younger farmers and their families. These include:

## B.A Implementation arrangements

### B.A.1 Role of GIZ

GIZ, with its head office located in Germany, is the Accredited Entity (AE) for the Thai Rice Project. GIZ will continue to act as an Executing Entity in relation to specific activities of the project. For the remainder of doubt, GIZ as the Accredited Entity and GIZ as the Executing Entity are a single legal entity, but the two GIZ entities have different management structures.

GIZ is one of the largest international providers of project development and technical assistance on climate change adaptation in Thailand. It has implemented over 300 climate-related projects with combined funding of over US$1.9 billion. GIZ has been operating in Thailand since 1956. The GIZ office currently employs approximately 270 staff, including 40 Thai nationals.

The Thai Rice Project is undertaken in close cooperation with, and the approval of, the Government of Thailand, based on a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) signed between the two parties in June 2013. Since the conversion of GIZ support in Thailand since 2009, projects with a combined budget of more than Euro 75 million have been implemented under the GIZ Climate Change Programme, where accounted for Euro 135 million. The Thai Rice Project forms parts of the GIZ Entity Work Programme.

## B

Department employs approximately 1,800 professional staff, including provincial officers. More than 50 regional RD centres operate in 26 provinces to support local research on rice and seed distribution. The Rice Department is the principal government partner for a number of foundation and baseline projects, including NSP, MSCP, and others.

### 26. Bank for Agriculture and Agricultural Cooperatives (BAAC)

Established in 1966 as a government-owned (99.8%) Ministry of Finance and 0.2% agricultural cooperatives bank. In 1993, BAAC was restructured to become a commercial bank with the objective of providing financial services for farmers for agricultural-related activities and, more recently, for rural non-agricultural activities. BAAC is supervised by the Office of the Deputy Governor of the Bank of Thailand (BOT) and the Attorney General (TAG). BAAC dominates lending to the agricultural sector in Thailand, accounting for 97% of total loans to farmers. It also provides credit insurance services and participates in government lending on and lending to agricultural cooperatives. In the fiscal year 2021, BAAC’s total assets amounted to approximately THB 354 billion (approximately USD 11.6 billion). The largest single sector of agriculture in the country (followed by rubber, sugarcane, cassava and maize), providing services to these farmers is BAAC. It also provides credit insurance services and participates in government lending on and lending to international partners, including in the NSP where BAAC operates a loan programme for agricultural service cooperatives.

### 26. Office of Natural Resources and Environmental Policy and Planning (ONEP)

ONEP is a public entity under the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MNR&E), operating in accordance with the Administrative Act B.E. 2537 (1994). It was established on April 1, 1996, to implement the National Policy for Environmental Protection. ONEP has the mandate to formulate policy, plans and measures to enhance and preserve environmental quality and biodiversity. It also coordinates with other government agencies in implementing policies related to the local point for international environmental conventions, including the UNEP COP, CEP, etc., as well as the GCF. ONEP is a lead executing entity of the NSP and is also an implementation partner of the GIZ NAMA Support Project (NSP), the International Climate Initiative (IKI), the World Bank, and others.

### 26. International Rice Research Institute (IRRI)

Established in 1960 as an independent, non-profit international research and educational institute dedicated to reducing poverty and hunger through rice research and development for a sustainable environment for future generations. It is headquartered in the Philippines, has an office in Thailand and is a founding member of CGIAR. IRRI is one of the world’s leading agricultural research institutions and the most prominent regional research institution in its field and a significant provider of technical expertise and capacity building to national and international partners through public sector funding, private sector funding, and private sources. Its active portfolio includes more than 150 donor-funded projects, ranging in size from USD 2 million to USD 30 million per year. IRRI has received approximately USD 487 million in grants and other funds since its inception. Total grant income in 2021 amounted to approximately USD $2.5 billion. IRRI has a long history of international cooperation, including by German federal government (GIZ) as the lead sectoral partner in Thailand and the broader region.

For local communities. Through their participation in the developPPP programme, Ebro Foods, MARIS, Olam Agri Group and PepsiCo have well-established relationships with GIZ in Thailand. ### B.4.3 Governance and management

The Thai Rice Project will be overseen by a Project Steering Committee (PSC), which will serve as the primary decision-making body for the project (as shown in Figure 19). The PSC will meet twice a year and consist of representatives of Thai national ministries and departments – including the Office of Natural Resources and Environment, Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives, and Department of Agriculture – and project partners. Private sector interests will be represented by a seat reserved for the private sector participant in the Project (PepsiCo). The PSC will be chaired by the GIZ representative.

The PSC will be responsible for political oversight and coordinating partner cooperation. The specific functions of the PSC will be:

B.5 Justification for GCF funding request

B.5.1 Additionality

281 The Thai Rice Project addresses mitigation and adaptation priorities identified by the government of Thailand in its NDC and the SAP. It targets a key economic sector – rice farming – that is emissions-intensive and especially vulnerable to climate change. The Thai Rice Project builds on a strong baseline of existing policies, programs, and practices for sustainable agriculture and climate-smart rice farming from a diverse set of public and private sector actors in order to address an identified set of barriers. The project will generate significant co-benefits through the mobilization of substantial additional funding from the private sector, involving actors along the entire value chain.

282 GCF support is needed for technical assistance (TA) – notably, training for farmers and service providers on climate-smart rice farming practices; development of a national standard for climate-smart rice farming; standard evolution of the Thai Rice Insurance Scheme (TRIS); and policy support for relevant Government agencies. The project will also require support to address barriers to scaling up climate-smart carbon market development, etc.) as well as financial assistance (FA) in the form of incentive payments for farmers and service providers, including through the Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) Component of ONEP to Thai climate smart rice projects.

283 While Thailand’s rice sector is partially subsidised, with a large portion of domestic finance in the rice value chain coming from public sources such as BMAC, these finances are not targeted at climate-smart rice agriculture. Significant and long-standing barriers have jeopardized any significant progress towards achieving the national targets for reducing greenhouse gas emissions from rice farming systems, the Thai Rice Project is needed in order to achieve substantive mitigation and adaptation benefits.

284 The Thai Rice Project is unique in targeting cross-cutting mitigation and adaptation needs in the rice sector, operating at scale, and deploying coordinated technical and financial assistance. The project will also generate significant co-benefits, including increased farmer incomes and reduced water and air pollution.


B.5.2 Concessionality

285 The vast majority of the grant (Euro 29.7 million out of Euro 36.1 million) is dedicated to technical assistance for climate-smart public good-type activities, for which use of a grant is fully justified. GCF funds are used to capitalise only 4 financial instruments: (i) the incentive payments scheme for rice farmers (Sub-Activity 2.1.2.3); (ii) the Climate-Smart Agriculture Component of ONEP (Sub-Activity 2.1.2.2); and by ONEP (Sub-Activity 3.1.2.2).

286 The on-grant implementing through ThACI amounting to Euro 5.9 million will be directed at achieving the project’s co-benefits, such as technology demonstration, community outreach and MRV that will not generate significant financial returns. While Thailand as a whole is considered an upper-middle-income country, the rice sector in Thailand is poor, and, in particular, non-farming – is considered highly vulnerable, justifying a more concessional approach.

287 Only the incentive payments scheme – with a GCF budget of Euro 7.6 million – will generate financial returns to the project. The other instruments are designed to support the implementation of climate-smart agriculture measures, and are mediocally set at Euro 1,700 / Euro 18 for farmers whose package includes both CSA and MRV activities. These incentives are justified on a cost-benefit analysis that includes LLE. These incentives are justified based on a detailed financial model (see Annex 3a) and the additional co-benefits they will generate.

288 Despite the positive financial internal rate of return (FIRR) generated by climate smart technologies and practices without the incentive payments (for example, 14.2% FIRR in the Central Plains and 7.1% in

The FFRIs (as detailed in Table 17 and Annex 3a), incentive payments are, in fact, essential to rapidly increase the penetration of climate-smart technologies and practices among Thai rice farmers – for the reasons stated below.

Note that the FFRIs do not reflect the value of technical assistance that farmers will receive to help them implement climate-smart agriculture. If farmers paid for this technical assistance in the hypothetical scenario where a financial provider was willing to provide it for free, then the FFRIs would be lower than those shown in Table 17.

**Rationale for Climate-Smart Incentive Payments**

Farmers are inherently risk-averse and sceptical of new technologies – in Thailand and elsewhere. The most powerful factor to persuade farmers to switch practices is evidence that the new practices have worked well on nearby farms. For instance, a 2019 study finds that “almost 35% of Thai farmers have an extreme degree of risk aversion” and that this risk aversion, combined with scepticism about new technologies, can be overcome by showing them how other farmers are using the technology successfully.

The proposed GCF grant-financed incentives of THB 7,000 and bath 4,000 (and with no LUL, restrictions) would provide a strong incentive to switch to climate-smart agriculture by a majority minority of rice farmers in the 3 regions. Specifically, the incentive scheme will be in force for 2 years and will be available only to farmers who have not yet adopted climate-smart agriculture practices on their farms. These increased early-adapters will be required to show their evidence of climate-smart agriculture adoption.

As shown in Table 17, the switch to climate-smart rice farming would result in FFRIs that, while positive, are not by themselves sufficient to persuade first-time adopters to switch. The FFRIs without incentive payments are negative for all regions except North-East. This is also worth noting: the high sensitivity of FFRIs to changes in yield (Table 16). Farmers are aware of this and will be more likely to switch to climate-smart technologies and practices, and more willing to pay a premium for them, if they can see evidence that these technologies and practices have worked well on nearby farms. This is why the incentive scheme will be in force for only two years.

The FFRIs are also worth noting: the high sensitivity of FFRIs to changes in yield (Table 16). Farmers are aware of this and will be more likely to switch to climate-smart technologies and practices, and more willing to pay a premium for them, if they can see evidence that these technologies and practices have worked well on nearby farms. This is why the incentive scheme will be in force for only two years.

294. Encouraging farmers will build the approval of climate-smart rice farming not only to farmers who are expected to generate a catalytic network effect to drive in those and peers, but also particularly for households that meet one or more of the following factors: (i) household income below a certain threshold; (ii) inability to borrow money from financial institutions due to lack of collateral; (iii) exposure to climate change and other natural hazards (e.g., flood, drought, heatwaves); (iv) farm location in an area determined to be particularly exposed to climate change and other crop risks; (v) lack of irrigation resulting in only one farming season; and (vi) gender considerations.

### Financial Incentives and Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) Grants

295. While financial incentives will result in an increase in the FIRR, this increase is not deemed to be overly generous. Figure 17 shows the FIRR with GCF grant in the 3 regions. The FIRR in the Central Plains region would increase to **26.8%**. The FIRR in the North East representative form would increase to **36.5%**.

296. Financial incentives will be discontinued once the Thai Rice Project ends. Since climate-smart rice farming is expected to become more attractive, and farmers realise the financial benefits of the new technologies and practices, it is expected that they will prefer to adopt CSA practices in the future. The GCF grant will continue to support the establishment of smart agriculture (Sub Activity 2.1.2) and some farmers who were previously not eligible for BCA loans may be able to access them through the GCF grant.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 104

#### Hypothetical Concessional Loan Scenario

297. The Thai Rice Project will replicate a substantial BAAC loan as co-finance, dedicated to climate smart rice farmers and funded out of BAAC's existing balance sheet (with no need for a GCF concessional loan).

298. Nonetheless, a hypothetical scenario is presented below, in which GCF lends at concessional terms to the Project, with the following characteristics:

- **Loan Terms:** 
  - (i) adopt climate-smart farming technologies and practices.
  - (ii) pass BAAC's credit screening process.
  - (iii) have a loan term of 5 years, with an interest rate of 2% per annum on-lending.
    - (a) 5 year maturity with 1-year grace period;
    - (b) 4 equal instalments in Years 2-5;
    - (c) 5% interest rate on the outstanding balance at each instalment date, plus 0.75% per annum on the principal amount.
  - (iv) cover BAAC's operating costs; and
  - (v) a loan amount equal to 50% of the LLL service fee in the Central Plains and North-Eastern regions (assuming that the full LLL is implemented).

299. Unsurprisingly, FIRRs increase relative to the base-case (negotiated) scenario (see Figure 18), since the cost of debt is lower than the unfettered FIRRs shown above.

300. It is worth noting that a loan, albeit at concessional terms, would still be considered a subsidy by risk-averse farmers. Therefore, it may be necessary for BAAC to ensure that only creditworthy farmers – not all farmers – and any relaxation of BAAC credit criteria would likely result in some farmers defaulting on the loan (making the loan de facto a grant).

## Activity 3.1.2 Strengthening the enabling environment for climate-smart rice farming

### A capacity development strategy for TAS verified rice (equivalent) is developed.

#### Government bodies shift policy and institutional programmes (sub-national agricultural policies, Strategic Plan for the Agriculture Sector, etc.) into a climate-smart framework (e.g., Climate Change Committees), onto a climate change project implementation plan.

- The T-VER Rice Scheme is coupled to the voluntary carbon market coupled to the TAS.
- The Thai Rice Facility is established as an implementation coordination body and commercial operations entity. It will develop a strategy for the rice sector, etc.), to be integrated into the climate-smart rice and other related climate-smart rice projects are supported with grants.

#### All activities are implemented in collaboration with the World Rice Conference side-event and knowledge sharing events.

### The project is enabled by technical and financial support from service providers, and motivated by financial incentives for climate-smart rice farming and other climate-smart initiatives beyond.

- Commercial off-takers and end-consumers are provided with a steady supply of certified sustainable rice, and agricultural extension helps to build demand and facilitating further market growth.
- The two pillars of the project's exit strategy are: (i) to ensure the continued involvement of farmers (farmers, service providers, off-takers, consumers) so that they can continue to benefit from the project, and (ii) to put in place enduring structures – particularly in terms of institutional arrangements – which will allow the project to operate beyond the duration of The Rice Project.

#### Strengthening the policy, programmatic and institutional framework for climate-smart rice farming through engagement with climate-smart rice and a clear policy direction.
- The commencement of the T-VER Rice Scheme is expected by 2018.
- Coupling the T-VER Rice Scheme with the TAS will provide farmers with additional income streams as well as premium prices for their rice and greater resilience to market fluctuations, particularly in the implementation period of the project, providing support to farmers to become accredited at a faster rate. EFP's capacity to manage ongoing TheCrops, including the Thai Rice Project will be enhanced.
- The Thai Rice Facility will continue to operate beyond the implementation period of the project, providing support for the implementation and dissemination of climate-smart rice practices and financing in the Thai rice sector.

#### Overall project impact on the rice sector’s climate-smart rice farming: The Thai Rice Project will be disseminated and exchanged in the region as a model for the commercialization and fertilization of best practices for sustained deepening and expansion of climate-smart rice farming in Thailand and throughout the region.

B.6.2 Sustainability

309. The Thai Rice Project will scale up and broaden a suite of foundation projects (see Section B.1.n), which have already demonstrated: (i) the strong interest (but currently limited capacity) of rice farmers to adopt climate-smart practices, including those that are more resilient to climate change impacts on their environment; and (ii) the genuine and proactive willingness of the government to support a paradigm shift to value-added, sustainable, and climate-resilient rice production in Thailand – as reaffirmed by the updated 2022 NDC and the updated LT-LEDS published in November 2022.

310. The Thai Rice Project is assessed as having high sustainability potential. GCF grant financing will:

* Mainstream the use of a range of climate-smart farming technologies and practices that can enhance rice yields, improve water-use efficiency, reduce greenhouse gas emissions, increase rice quality, reduced costs, premium price, reduced vulnerability to drought, etc.). Training will be carried out in collaboration with relevant government agencies, private sector entities, civil society sector support and involving local farmer and community organisations, in order to ensure the widespread adoption of climate-smart practices and technologies that are appropriate for local farming practices.
* Foster the development of new markets for climate-smart rice and establish the self-sustaining business models necessary for private sector provision of technologies to adapt farming practices. The Thai Agricultural and Cooperative Development Bank (TACDB) will be involved in establishing the private sector will be established and market linkages strengthened through the Thai Agriculture and Co-operative Development Bank (TACDB).
* Scale-up the TAS as a national standard to create market differentiation for climate-smart agricultural products, including rice. The project will ensure that the TAS is aligned with international alignment with internationally equivalent standards. Regular enhancements to the TAS will be facilitated by the private sector.
* Support the design, operationalisation and launch of the T-VER Rice Scheme as a sustainable financing mechanism.
* Strengthen the individual capacities of national institutions, including MiAC, MAHRE, EFD and FAD, to provide technical assistance to farmers in adopting climate-smart practices. This will expand and capacitated ThaGC – and enhance their ability to coordinate and join up efforts through the T-VER scheme.
* Mobilise a substantial volume of public and private finance in support of climate-smart rice, including by establishing a dedicated financing mechanism for the T-VER scheme explicitly designed to improve capital availability for climate-smart farmers and service providers.

## C.3.1 Capacity building
311. The Thai Rice Project has a strong capacity-building element, directed at both individuals (notably, rice farmers) and institutions (including major farms, extension services and service providers). This capacity building directly addresses a need highlighted in Thailand's NDC, which states that "there are still gaps between the knowledge of farmers and the technology available to them. The project will address these gaps by providing training for farmers on climate-smart technologies, practices and management."

312. The following Sub-Activities, with a total GCF grant allocation of approximately Euro 24.6 million, contain agricultural capacity-building activities:

* **Sub-Activity 1.1.1:** Training for farmers on climate-smart technologies, practices and management
* **Sub-Activity 1.1.2:** Public sector capacity building, with a focus on extension services and networks
* **Sub-Activity 1.2.1:** Financial literacy for farmers
* **Sub-Activity 1.2.2:** Climate-smart lending capacity development for BAAC
* **Sub-Activity 1.4.1:** Regional exchange and peer-to-peer learning

## C.3.2 Technology development / transfer
313. The Thai Rice Project is a project in the country of "enhanced access to technology development and transfer." It goes on to note that "it is crucial that international cooperation through the UNCCD, FAO and other relevant organizations be strengthened for the benefit of developing countries." The LT-LEDS specifically cites CIVs, reduction technologies in rice cultivation as a priority. However, it also notes that there are significant constraints on R&D in Thailand meaning that international transfer of advanced technologies is urgently required to address these constraints. These include climate change adaptation and mitigation technologies which are addressed by the Thai Rice Project: weather forecasting and early warning systems, crop technology (including drought-resistant rice varieties), water management techniques (specifically identified as pressing needs), precision farming practices (including water and GHG management) and post-harvest technology.

* Office for Natural Resources and Environmental Policy and Planning (2020). Thailand's Second Updated National Determined Contribution. https://unfccc.int/sites/default/files/resource/Thailand%20Second%20Updated%20NDC.pdf
* OECD (2020). Thailand’s Long-Term Low Greenhouse Gas Emission Development Strategy. https://www.oecd.org/tad/thailand-long-term-low-greenhouse-gas-emission-development-strategy-9789264351222-en.htm
* National Science Technology and Innovation Policy Office (2012). Technology Trends Assessment Report for Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation. https://www.nstic.go.th/images/attach/2012/technologytrendsassessmentreportadaptation_english.pdf

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The section outlines the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## D.1 Impact potential

### D.1.1 Impact performance overview

315. The Rice Tech Project is a cross-cutting initiative that offers both climate mitigation and adaptation benefits.

#### Technological and Geographical Scope

The Rice Tech Project targets 21 provinces, in which the adoption of 10 technologies and practices offering climate mitigation and adaptation benefits will be supported.

### Table 20: Climate-Smart Agricultural Technologies and Practices Supported by The Rice Tech Project

| Technology / Practice | Type of Climate Impact |
| --- | --- |
| Laser land leveling (LLL) | Adaptation – improved water use efficiency, notably in the context of ASWD. |
| Alternate wetting and drying (AWD) | Mitigation – reduced CH4 emissions. |
| Site-specific nutrient management (SSNM) | Adaptation – reduced water use. |
| Straw and stubble management (SSM) | Adaptation – reduced soil erosion, improved water use efficiency, and reduced N2O from avoided fertilizer application. |
| Integrated pest management (IPM) | Adaptation – reduced crop losses and enhanced food / income security. |
| Rice variety diversification | Mitigation – reduced CH4 and N2O emissions. |
| Crop diversification, rotation | Adaptation – reduced water use and reduced crop failure risk. |
| Direct seeded rice (DSR) | Adaptation – reduced water use. |
| Farm-level water management (FWM) | Adaptation – increased water retention and storage. |
| Agro-met advisory services | Adaptation – avoided crop loss / damage through better-informed farmer decision-making. |

317. The suites of technologies and practices promoted by the Rice Tech Project differ between the target regions, according to their specific needs and priorities. A summary of these differences is presented in Table 21. The final distribution, which will closely resemble this indicative distribution, will be confirmed with project stakeholders (at national, provincial and local levels) during the inception phase of the project.

318. The mitigation and adaptation climate impacts presented below in Sections D.1.3 and D.1.5 are based on the indicative distribution. Any changes made to the distribution – in terms of technologies and/or their geographical spread – will be reflected accordingly.

#### 3.9. Bid materials detail a GIS map database[2] that contains data on water management practices in the target provinces. The Department of Agricultural Extension (DAe) maintains databases of farmers registering for extension services, including information about their land use and cropping patterns. These databases on rice areas and planting dates, enable detailed baseline and project scenario calculations to be performed.

#### 3.10. The targeted physical area of farmland is ~718,000 ha, which was used completely in the wet season and partially in the dry season. The 'effective farmland' – the wet season planting plan area plus the dry season planting area – is estimated to be approximately 2,574,000 ha.

#### 3.11. The season length varies between agro-ecological regions and even within regions, depending on rice varieties, the cropping calendar and the physical characteristics of the region. The average season lengths of different regions are estimated using MoAC's rice cropping calendar[2].

### Project lifespan

#### 3.2. The Thai Rice Project Mekong is considered to be 15 years, assuming that three 5-year LLL cycles are sustained as a result of the technical capacities, strengthened service provider market, financial instruments and enhanced policy environment. However, given the uncertainties in project implementation, such as the availability of funds and LLL equipment, it are likely to have lifetimes of approximately 20 years or more. Nonetheless, to ensure consistency with other projects, the project lifespan is assumed to be 15 years.

### D.1.2 Emissions reduction estimation methodology

#### 3.23. The emissions reduction methodology is described in detail in Annex 22b. A summary is provided below:

**Baseline Scenario**

#### 3.24. Water management practices
- For irrigated rice in the Central Plains, a high adoption of water-saving practices, including multiple drainage (AWD) and mid-season drainage during the middle of the rice season, is generally observed due to 4+ years of NSP implementation in this region. Comparative baseline data is captured by a farm survey conducted by MoAC in 2013, which shows that 95% of farmers practice AWD and mid-season drainage (7%) in the Central Plains.
- For the 6 additional provinces in the Central Plains, it is assumed that farmers do not practice AWD but at least 80% of them use mid-season drainage during both dry season and wet season planting periods, respectively. This baseline also allows conservative estimates in the estimation of the project's adjustments.

#### 3.25. The provinces in the North – Chiang Mai and Chiang Rai – have not yet adopted new water management which is why they are assumed to be using traditional practices with no mid-season drainage or continuous water in the field as possible. Therefore, the analysis assumes 100% continuing floodings in irrigated rice in these regions.

#### 3.26. For provinces in the North-East, continuous flooding is widely applied. The analysis assumes 75% continuous flooding for all provinces in this region.
- For rainfed rice, it is observed through in-field measurements that significant methane reduction can also be achieved by using a mid-season drainage technique (7%). This baseline data was captured from a 2013 farm survey data shows very low adoption of AWD (8%) and mid-season drainage (7%) practices in the Central Plains. The same assumptions are adopted to estimate baseline emissions for the Central Plains.
- For all provinces in the North and North-East regions, continuous flooding is assumed to be fully practiced (100%) in both dry and wet seasons.


---
**Page 120**
---

305. Mitigation of rice use: In the Central Plains, the average rate of nitrogen fertilizer used is assumed to be 95 kg N/ha and 107 kg N/ha in the dry and wet seasons, respectively. (Based on empirical observations, it is estimated that the average rate of nitrogen fertilizer applied per hectare is 84 kg N/ha for rice cultivation.)

306. Rice residue management: Average rates for different rice residues (straw and stubble) management practices (i.e., burning, incorporation and removal) under irrigated and rain-fed rice in different regions that are reported in the empirical studies are shown below. The highest average rates of residue incorporation occur during the wet season but those in the dry season and that incorporation rates are commensurately higher.

307. Methane EF: A number of academic studies calculate EFs for Thailand, with a wide range of values cited. No comprehensive study has been undertaken to determine the national average value of methane emissions from rice fields; hence, no single value can be provided. Therefore, the standard GHG EFs provided by the IPCC for Southeast Asia are used as an estimate in this document. The EFs can and will be refined during project implementation, as empirical data becomes available.

Project Scenario

308. Project Interventions by region: The Focus Interventions outlined in Table 11 are assumed to be implemented. A number of potential Supportive Interventions are included in the calculation of all Focus Interventions or Supportive Interventions included in the project's mitigation impact (see below).

309. Among the 253,400 smallholder farmers targeted by the project, it is (conservatively) assumed that they apply fertilizer at rates of between 70% and 80% of those recommended for their crops and on 60% of their non-mega-farm farmland—which equates to 72% of the farmers' total farmland. These application rates are based on an average of the empirical studies cited in Section 305, which also indicate that these farmers tend to overapply fertilizer. It should be noted that the figures given above do not take into account the fact that the plots differ in their characteristics (soil type, slope, irrigation, etc.) and their biophysical suitability for rice production. The empirical studies cited show that application rates are higher on consolidated and established plots, accompanied by better access to support services. Hence their higher application rates reflect the fact that they differ in their characteristics (soil type, slope, irrigation, etc.) and their biophysical suitability for rice production.

310. Central Plains: Water management: Applying water-saving practices (single pivot and AEWD) is feasible under the biophysical conditions of the Central Plains. With the support of the Thai Rice Project, farmers will shift from the current practice of flooding to applying water in a more controlled manner.

311. SSUE: It is assumed that direct and indirect NOx emissions are mitigated by reducing nitrogen input by 10% (see Section 298).

312. SSM: Burning of rice residues is a common practice in the Central Plains. More than one-third of rice residue is burned, with the remainder being incorporated into the soil. The burning of rice straw is assumed to be conservative— that one-quarter of the baseline straw burning will be avoided. This amount will be removed from the total emissions for this scenario and will not be included as a mitigation measure due to wet conditions at the end of the season. Incorporating residue into the soil can generate high CH4 emissions, but these are offset by the reduction in N2O emissions. The incorporation of straw is assumed to reduce N2O emissions by 50% (see Section 307). In addition, the incorporation of stubble will only be promoted in the wet season. When a combined fertilizer application and residue incorporation is applied, it is estimated that the average rate of nitrogen fertilizers, the incorporation of stubble will only be promoted in the wet season. # North

- Water management: The 2 provinces—Chiang Mai and Chiang Rai—will start to adopt innovative water management practices by shifting 30% of irrigated rice area from continuous flooding to single aeration for a shorter period.
    - **SSM:** The baseline nitrogen application rate for rice is relatively low in the North; 77kg N/ha in the dry season and 125 kg N/ha in the wet season. This intervention is, therefore, not included in the GHG mitigation impact estimation.
- SSM Adaptation: In the case of Chiang Rai, the baseline is the Central Plain. It should be noted that, in the baseline, the incidence of burning rice stubble and slurry is significantly higher in the north region (Chiang Mai and Chiang Rai) than in the Central Plains.

# North-East

- Water management: 25% of rice land will transition from single aeration to AWD, while 25% of farms will be converted to double cropping. The remaining 50% will continue with the current practice. Therefore, update rates can be reached without an emphasis on LUL, but introduction of LUL is considered to be beneficial.
    - **SSM:** Due to the low nitrogen rates that are currently applied, no nitrogen reduction will be pursued for this project. Instead, a focus will be placed on reducing N2O emissions by using more efficient fertilizers (applying smaller doses of nitrogen fertilizer and introducing the rotation of rice and nitrogen-fixing crops). This will also reduce the risk of waterlogging. There are no established ways of estimating the (negative) impact.
- SSM Adaptation: GHG in the North-East is similar to that in the Central Plains.

## Other Interventions

### Four mitigation technologies and practices introduced by The Rice Project are not included in the assessment of the mitigation impact of the project, because baseline information (such as level of IRRP adoption by region) or expert judgment indicates that these technologies and practices will have minimal to no significant additional impacts. These 4 technologies and practices should be regarded as additional climate benefits that are not considered part of this project's GHG mitigation impact assessment. The following four technologies and practices may provide additional climate benefits can be credited quantitatively and reported.
- **SSM:** In the case of rice, it is a multi-tariff system (covering paddy fields, inundated pastures, fish ponds, and birds feedlots).
    - Later land leveling (LULL): LULL can certainly play a crucial role in supporting and enhancing water-saving technologies. Poorly levied fields are a major constraint on farmers effectively managing irrigation water use in order to reduce the amount of water used per unit of rice produced. This is because it reduces water efficiency, as well as profitability! Further, LULL is scale-neutral; meaning it is not biased towards large farms. The benefits of LULL can be seen within 1-2 years and are likely to persist for approximately 3 seasons due to higher yields and reduced inputs.
    - **SSM:** An unpublished multi-factorial regression analysis of yield data from the Rice Project's on-farm trials in Thailand, based on sample plots and, in the case of biodiversity, (a) a multi-tariff system (covering paddy fields, inundated pastures, fish ponds, birds feedlots).
    - **SSM:** The reduced input cost for farmers arising from LULL will be monitored: kg of seeds, kg of fertilizers, kg of pesticides and the volume of irrigation water reduced.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 119

## Integrated Pest Management (IPM): Reducing herbicide and pesticide use from a high / intermediate level to the recommended low level will potentially mitigate 190 t CO2e/ha/year.

The following metrics will be monitored to assess the adaptation impact of IPM: the number of pest species, the number of insecticides used, the number of species in rice fields (biodiversity).

## Rice crop duration and yield: Rice crop duration and yield at CCAh/season depends on the soil type, the rice season and the irrigation conditions (irrigated and rain-fed). Reducing 1 day of crop duration can result in a reduction of 3.5 t CO2e/ha/year. For example, for a paddy field located in Thailand of 125-150 days. Depending on the growing conditions, this could result in a reduction of 2.97–8.1 kg CO2e per person-hour.

The rice crop duration and yield at CCAh/season depends also on the number of varieties: the number of days that the crop is in the field.

## Rice crop duration and yield: Rice crop duration and yield at CCAh/season depends on the soil type, the rice season contribute to a mitigation potential of 331 kg CO2e/ha/year. In addition, multiple drainage water management events can be expected to reduce the amount of water used for irrigation by up to 50% (i.e., reducing the amount of AWD and in other areas through dry soil drainage). The following metrics will be monitored to assess the adaptation impact of rice crop duration and yield: the area under dry land, the area under dry soil drainage, the volume of water saved and the amount of reduced labour required (person-hours).

## Calculation Tool

334. The estimation is performed using the SECTOR®calculation tool[26] in conjunction with the conversion and scaling factors provided by the 2019 refinement of the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories.[27]

### NAMA Support Project (NSP)

The NSP is implementing climate mitigation technologies and practices in 6 provinces in the Central Plains region. The project has been designed to reduce GHG emissions from rice production by 15% compared with baseline. The emission reductions achieved through the implementation of the technologies and practices described in the NSP provides is characterized by markedly higher baseline adoption rates of AWD, SSMM and SSMR. Reductions are attributed to the NSP and the Thai Rice Project mitigation scenario. Double-counting of emission reductions – attributable to the NSP and to the Thai Rice Project mitigation scenario involved.

### D.1.3 Emissions reduction impact

#### Baseline emissions

336. Total GHG emissions under the baseline scenario are 6.18 MCGg/yr. The average emissions intensity is 5.8 tCO2e/ha. Since the planting area in the wet season is twice that in the dry season, the wet season is responsible for the majority of total emissions (90%). Most of these emissions occur during the dry season and some emissions occur during the wet season. Approximately 60% of emissions (3.7 MCGg/yr) are from irrigated areas and 40% of emissions are from rain-fed areas.
