# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 122

### Rice ecosystems are increasingly exposed to temperatures above 33.7°C during growth and especially above 31°C during flowering, which leads to stunted sterility and risk of crop failure and decreased yields. These heat wave impacts are exacerbated by the currently low uptake of water-heat tolerant rice varieties across Thailand.

#### 32nd.
And, in parallel, increased average temperature and frequency of heatwaves also increase evaporation from soil and plants, which leads to reduced soil moisture availability for rice plants. Reduced soil moisture availability reduces root growth and development in rice plants, which, in turn, causes stomatal closure and photosynthesis inhibition and results in reduced grain weight per plant.

#### 33rd.
And, in combination with reduced rainfall during the wet season and more frequent drought conditions impacting water availability for irrigation of paddy rice, water-related climate change impacts will especially affect irrigated rice fields. In addition to the increased frequency of heatwaves, the current low uptake of water-saving techniques such as AWD (alternate wetting and drying) is expected to have the highest impact on the fields. Increasingly frequently, there will be insufficient water to irrigate all cultivated areas in a single irrigation cycle. This will lead to reduced yields per hectare due to poor soil health.

#### 34th.
Four more extreme rainfall especially during the wet season can cause more frequent flooding in low-lying cultivated areas and fields. Flooding reduces the availability of oxygen for rice plants, which leads to reduced organic and inorganic nitrogen fertilizer uptake, which results in further decreasing yields when rice plants have insufficient access to nutrients. In addition, flooding also increases the risk of disease outbreaks such as brown spot and bacterial leaf blight. Nitrogen fixation is undermined as Azotobacter only grows on flooded fields. The impacts on soil fertility are exacerbated by the current low uptake of water-saving techniques such as AWD (alternate wetting and drying) by keeping the water level just above optimal, with the associated increased evaporation under high temperatures.

#### 35th.
Finally, the likelihood of areas where periods of flooding precede droughts will increase, leading to more frequent occurrences of rice plants suffering from both heat stress and water stress as those caused by the brown spot hopper. Additionally, a lack of irrigation water and associated insufficient water levels in irrigated rice fields will undermine the capacity of farmers to manage their crops effectively. The current low uptake of water-saving techniques such as AWD (alternate wetting and drying) is already under stress as the sector is characterised by microleakage and lack of crop rotation, as well as a lack of awareness on climate-smart agriculture interventions.

#### 36th.
As rice farming in Thailand is already characterised by smallholder farming with low yields and low incomes, further reduced rice yields due to climate change threaten the livelihoods of smallholder farmers and, by extension, their families. Smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable because they: (i) have very limited access to inputs such as fertilizers and pesticides; (ii) have very low uptake of voluntary insurance products; (iii) their lack of access for training for (and), partially, lack of awareness on climate-smart agriculture interventions, and (v) the limited market for sustainable rice and rice residues.

### D.1.5 Adaptation impact

- **347**: The Thai Rice Project targets ~98,600 smallholder rice farmers in the Central Plains, ~142,000 farmers in the North of Thailand. Rice farmers in Thailand are especially vulnerable to climate change impacts on rice harvests as reliable harvests form a major component of their food security or income generation.

- **348**: Project interventions focus on climate change impacts on water availability, soil fertility, pest and disease pressure, and yield variability. The project will implement a range of adaptation measures including institutional instrument interventions related to field-climate smart technologies and practices, including laser land levelling (LLL), precision irrigation, improved rice varieties, integrated pest management (IPM) for rice, crop diversification and the utilisation of rice biomass residues (straw and stubble management: SSM), and climate risk insurance.

- **349**: AWD reduces the overall consumption of irrigation water compared to the currently widespread practice of continuous flooding over each growth cycle and thereby contributes to adapting to reduced available water while also improving soil fertility. The project will implement a range of measures including the reduction in duration of irrigation water during the critical growth stages, which is currently not available – due to climate change - in large parts of Thailand. These include the use of drip irrigation systems, precision irrigation, and optimal timed seeding and planting, and hence optimal use of the increasingly scarce water resource. Consequently, the project implements measures to improve farmers' access to high-quality agri-met advisory services.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 124

#### 350. The proposed funding period during plant maturation well, in some cases, requires water storage systems to provide sufficient irrigation water availability as part of the water management component: the project promotes farm- and soil-based adaptation measures that include the use of drought-tolerant rice varieties (e.g., IR64), cover crops and some pests affecting rice (e.g. brown plant hopper), and supports the growth of nitrogen-fixing Azolla, supporting the development of a sustainable rice production system. The project also promotes the use of biochar to flank the water-saving adaptation measures to avoid negative impacts on soil and plant health and, ultimately, rice yield per hectare.

351. SSNAM also aims to farmers to better control fertilizer application costs while increasing yields. Across sample sites in the region, SSNAM is found to improve rice yields by 80% of fertiliser, depending on soil properties, agro-climatic conditions and other factors. However, the adoption rate for SSNAM has dropped due to recent increases in fertilizer costs, which may impact yield levels in the near-term as soil nitrogen availability decreases. The project will provide training and technical assistance to farmers, extension workers and soil doctors to introduce SSNAM as their relative investment costs drop. Farmers adopting SSNAM and other climate-resilient practices are expected to see a 20% increase in rice yields.

352. Poverty in Thailand exhibits a distinct rural-urban dimension, with approximately 80% of Thailand's poor living in rural areas. The majority live on small plots and many are landless. In their agricultural lives, their farming methods and the very ways of life are threatened by climate change, in particular by the increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. This is especially true for the most vulnerable communities who are therefore addressing the climate adaptation needs of some of the most vulnerable members of Thai society.

#### Health and Well-Being, Food and Water Security

353. Climate change, notably in the form of widespread droughts in 2016 and 2019, has not only reduced the urban water supply but also affected rural areas as reported on, but it has also served to further exacerbate it. Thailand ranks 9th in terms of countries most affected by climate events in the period 2000-2019[38] and 20th for the period 2015-2019[39]. The effects are felt across all sectors, including agriculture, which is heavily affecting water security and, in turn, the security of rice supply, which represents a long-standing food staple for the Thai population. Rice accounts for about 25% of the country's agricultural export revenues.[40]

#### Ecosystems and Ecosystem Services

354. Both, human utilisation of natural resources and climatic changes, have significant impacts on ecosystems[41]. Agriculture, and thus the livelihoods of people, are heavily reliant on well-functioning ecosystems. Rice is Thailand's most important food crop, accounting for roughly half of that country's land use and employing 10 million people.

# D2.1 Potential for scaling-up and replication

305. Rice is grown in 114 countries (FAO). Currently, rice production accounts for approximately 2.5% of total anthropogenic GHG emissions and 10% of agricultural emissions. The emission footprint of the rice sector is complex and varies widely across regions. In Southeast Asia, rice contributes to about 37% of global agricultural methane emissions and 11% of global agricultural rice oxide emissions (FAO). In South-East Asia, rice accounts for approximately 25% of total anthropogenic GHG emissions.

306. Accounting for 7% of global rice sector methane emissions, Thailand is the world’s fourth-largest rice sector emitter. Targeting this rice sector therefore offers not only the potential to address a globally significant climate challenge but also to contribute significantly towards national and international commitments on reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. The Rice Project in Thailand has been leading the way and demonstrating the potency of the rice sector in reducing agricultural GHG emissions at speed and scale, with the potential for replication across other countries.

307. Food systems are one of the most important contributors to GHG emissions, but they also need to be adapted to cope with climate change. Rice is a major food staple in many parts of Asia, particularly in South-East Asia where lowland rice agriculture and the figure is three times higher in South Asia. Irrigated rice is grown on approximately 20% of global rice area (FAO). The Rice Project has demonstrated that it is possible to reduce water use by up to 50%, while maintaining yields and improving soil health, which can also help to improve resilience against climate change.

308. Within Thailand, more than 3.7 million households engaged in irrigated rice cultivation on a total planned area of more than 17.4 million ha (of which approximately 6 million is irrigated). The Thai Rice Project targets 21 provinces and aims to scale up the approach. It is therefore considered feasible to replicate the Thai Rice Project approach in additional provinces in Thailand.

309. The Thai Rice Project can also serve as a model for promoting rice to be grown on dry land, and it has been adopted by other countries. For example, companies that grow rice have already declared their intent to shift to sustainable production not only. Indeed, Olam, PepsiCo, and Nestlé have already invested significant financial resources in the Thai Rice Project. However, many of these companies are still struggling with how to make this practice at scale, as there is currently an insufficient supply of rice that meets the needs.

**Page 129**
---

# D.2 Potential for knowledge-sharing and learning

360. The Thai Rice Project will maximize the potential for knowledge and learning by promoting the introduction of climate-resilient rice varieties, improved agricultural practices, and integrated pest management with the national agricultural extension system (including community rice centers and regional rice research centers). Knowledge building will be offered in a range of supplementary areas, including the TAS, insurance and carbon finance.

361. There will be a strong emphasis on data collection, data analysis and the use of digital tools for: (i) testing, under Sub-Activity 2.1.4.1, the impact of climate-resilient rice varieties in different agro-climatic zones; (ii) supporting the development, and Sub-Activity 2.1.3 on the content of insurance product development. The project will gather lessons learned from the implementation of the Thai Rice Project closely as it seeks to replicate sector paradigm shift in other Asian countries.

# D.2.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

362. A recent (November 2022) publication from the SRLI provides a succinct summary of the factors that contribute to an enabling environment for sustainable rice. To quote from the publication[18]:

* **Upstream value creation is paramount:** Farmers and service providers in production landscapes need value propositions that are aligned with their needs, preferences, and practices, as well as with their real-world context.
* **Cash flow management:** Farmers are needed to address payment delays and profit erosion across long value chains, and to mitigate fluctuations in farmer incomes throughout the year.
* **Trust building among value chain actors is essential for reducing side-selling in the context of pre-financing and off-take agreements.**
* **Capacity of essential enabling needs strengthening:** To bring effective, bankable counterparties to financial institutions, it will be necessary to strengthen the capacity of farmers, service providers, cooperatives, agricultural SMEs, off-takers in rice production landscapes is required over the long term.
* **Modeling and risk management tools for smallholder farmers are needed.** These need to be embedded within financial structures.
* **Size matters:** The current funding and risk-sharing mechanisms need to be adapted to relatively small funding needs at the farmer level (e.g., aggregating similar projects to support larger investments).

363. The Thai Rice Project addresses all of these factors, as well as additional enabling conditions (e.g., insurance, carbon finance, etc.). It will also provide a platform for farmers and service providers in production landscapes to link them to relevant in-quality regulated market settings (e.g. the TAS for sustainable rice and quality standards in the context of the TWEER Scheme). The project will support the development of the Thai Rice Project under the BAAC CSE claim, and the TWEER Scheme), and by promoting joined-up policy and knowledge dissemination activities to create a more enabling environment needed to catalyze and sustain the transition to climate-smart rice agriculture in Thailand.

# D.2 Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

364. The Thai Rice Project is aligned with the government’s climate policies and strategies, notably the 2nd Updated NDC, the National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS), and the National Climate Change Policy Statement (NCPS). Indeed, the LTED specifically states that the Government of Thailand is seeking GCF support to scale-up climate-resilient rice varieties. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the Thai Rice Project closely as it seeks to replicate sector paradigm shift in other Asian countries.

**D.2.5 Contribution to climate-resilient development pathways**

365. The project in Ukraine, supply chain disruptions and continued economic scarring from the COVID-19 pandemic have led to a significant increase in food prices. Ukraine is now one of the most important low- and middle-income country in the world. By mid-2022, rice prices were 8% higher than at the beginning of the year, and maize prices increased by more than 35%. The global price of wheat has also risen significantly.

* A global increase in temperature of 1°C is likely to reduce rice yields by an average of a 3.2% globally[60] and approximately 23 million hectares under rice production in Asia are prone to drought[61].
* By the end of this decade, the projected increase in global temperatures will lead to a reduction in daily caloric intake of the population of Asia, and a 5% increase in rice prices would push an additional 39.28 million people into poverty[62].

It is evident that climate change has the potential to severely disrupt the future supply of rice, with significant follow-on impacts on nutritional health and livelihoods. Moreover, one third of global GHG emissions come from the food sector (FAO, 2015), and rice production accounts for approximately a quarter of all global agricultural N2O emissions[63]. Measures – such as those included in the Thai Rice Project – to reduce the dependency on chemical inputs will help mitigate climate change impacts. The CDM credits associated with rice production therefore offer a substantial contribution to climate-resilient development pathways.

# D.3 Sustainable development

## D.3.1 Sustainable Development Goals

366. The Thai Rice Project advances multiple Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), with quantifiable metrics to document the progress of the project recorded through the TAS (or equivalent standard). The project supports a strong focus on SDG 2 (Zero Hunger) and SDG 8 (Decent Work and Economic Growth), as well as SDG 10 (Reduced Inequality) and SDG 16 (Peace, Justice and Strong Institutions). It also contributes to SDG 5 (Gender Equality) and SDG 7 (Affordable and Clean Energy). The project supports adherence to international norms on labour rights (SDG 8). Promoting responsible consumption (SDG 12) creates the opportunity for a more sustainable food system. The reduction of chemical inputs is an important element of the Thai Rice Project will enable the tracking and reporting of sustainable development benefits.

## D.3.2 Development co-benefits

### Environmental:

* Reduced environmental footprint by the reduction of unnecessary agrochemical use and practicing responsible use when needed.
* Reduced air pollution from burning of straw and stubble.

D.4 Needs of recipient
### D.4.1 Climate risk and vulnerability

Thailand is one of the 16 countries identified globally as being in the extreme risk category of those most vulnerable to climate change impacts. In terms of national exposure, Thailand ranks among the top five countries expected to experience the largest decline in GDP by 2045 (IPCC 2014) and ranks 97th in terms of countries most affected by climate change (UNDP 2013). The country is also ranked as one of the top three countries most vulnerable to climate change impacts. At present, Thailand faces an annual median probability of severe meteorological drought of around 4% (as defined by the World Bank) and a median probability of extreme heat waves of less than 5% and it is projected to double by 2089-2099 under RCP6.0 and RCP8.5 emissions pathways.

As described in Section B.1.2 and in the Feasibility Study (Annex B), Thailand's climate is already changing and will continue to change over time, with projected changes including: (i) an increase in mean annual temperature; notably, increasing temperatures and, in particular, decreasing water availability in the dry season (ii); (iii) yields of major crops such as rice, rubber, and palm oil are expected to decline by 2050 under RCP4.5; and (iv) declining rice yields will negatively impact the national economy.

Rice remains the dominant agricultural industry, accounting for 31% of cultivated area and contributing 15% of agricultural GDP. Not only is rice still a vital food crop domestically but it is also a major export, second only to rubber in value.

The countries with the highest income inequality level in East Asia and Thailand farmers are among the poorest and most heavily indebted occupational groups in the country. In the period 2007-2013, farm incomes declined by an average of 5% per year. The total number of Thai farmers fell from approximately 6 million to about 4.8 million. Approximately 3 million Thai rice farmers households carry a total debt of US$ 88 billion. Poverty in Thailand is concentrated among the rural population and the agricultural sector, which accounts for more than half of all poor people.

Climate change, notably in the form of widespread droughts in 2015-16 and 2019-21, has not only revealed the vulnerability of the agriculture sector and those that depend on it but has also served to further exacerbate this.### D.4.2 Financing needs

Thailand is classified as an upper-middle-income economy and in the second largest economy in South-East Asia. The agricultural sector accounted for 5% of GDP between 2013-2019, and its share increased by 6%, in 2020, as a result of COVID-19 job losses, disruptions to supply chains, reductions in trade and tourism, and a severe drought. The agricultural sector is expected to continue to play an important role in Thailand’s economic growth, but the recovery is expected to be sluggish, unevenly and subject to heightened uncertainty.

The pace of recovery will depend on the extent of the impact of COVID-19 on household income and labour income of Thai households has declined and average household debt has increased by a quarter or more.### D.5 Country ownership

## The project principles underlying the SEP are:

- Providing high-quality and accurate information to stakeholders throughout the project implementation period.
- Fostering a two-way dialogue that offers an opportunity for stakeholders to raise their views and concerns and engage in discussions with the Project Team.
- Promoting social acceptance of the project.

### Supporting the project’s success, which by its nature requires the willingness of stakeholders to change their behaviors: for example, smallholder farmers changing their agricultural practices, consumers altering their purchase decisions, private sector service providers investing in climate-smart agriculture, etc.

380. The key principles underlying the SEP are:

- The SEP is a living document of the project and runs continuously throughout the project implementation period – that is key issues can be identified and responded to rapidly.
- All stakeholders who are engaged, including women and vulnerable groups (such as migrant farm workers and ethnic groups).
- All information is presented clearly in culturally-and educationally-appropriate formats and in relevant languages.
- Minutes of meetings are well documented and archived. They form part of the project’s documentation disclosure policy.
- The project will strive to organize meeting sessions at a time that is convenient for all stakeholders, including women and vulnerable groups.

### External Communication and Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) (including SEAH GRM)

381. According to the GCF’s E&S policy, the purpose of the GRM is to receive and facilitate the resolution of concerns and grievances about the environmental and social performance of GCF-financed activities. In the context of the Thai Rice Project, the GRM will be used for:

- Providing a communication channel to receive feedback and grievances from stakeholders (including, but not limited to women and vulnerable groups) in an open and transparent manner with the goal of resolving grievances amicably where possible and minimizing the use of the legal system.
- Establishing a system for receiving complaints and reporting them in order to process stakeholder grievances in a timely and transparent manner.
- Establishing a system for receiving grievances and the measures if any put in place to respond to the grievance.
- Providing an opportunity for stakeholders, including women and vulnerable groups, whose rights reflect the particular gender, cultural and privacy sensitivities that can be associated with SEAH components.

383. The project’s GRM is predicated upon the following basic principles:

- Transparency: All communications related to the GRM will be conducted transparently, in a culturally-appropriate and gender-sensitive manner, and in the appropriate language.
- Consistency: The GRM will ensure consistency between the claimant and the GRM for the duration of the grievance process.
- Accessibility: The GRM will be readily accessible to all stakeholders.
- Accountability: All grievances will be recorded and archived, regardless of whether the grievances are justified or not. This will help in building trust with stakeholders.
- Disclosure: All justified grievances will be followed up by one or more discussions with the claimant – allowing for a dialogue of relevant or relevant, by a face-to-face by a project representative.
- Privacy: The GRM will be consistent with Thai data protection law and will respect complainant confidentiality and privacy.

### 384. A detailed description of the GRM and the SEAH GRM is provided in Annex Bb. In brief, the generic GRM enables grievances to be reported through a number of channels (including the project website, a dedicated phone number, local project representatives, grievance boxes located at project events, the local Damrongdhamma Centre, etc.). The SEAH GRM is an extension of the generic GRM and allows for more specific reporting. The SEAH GRM enables grievances to be reported through a number of channels (including the project website, a dedicated phone number [different from the general GRM phone number] or a dedicated e-mail address at the Damrongdhamma Centre). The SEAH GRM is designed specifically for SEAH-related grievances and the ESM Manager will develop a bespoke response approach for each grievance. Given the range of possible grievances, and the need to ensure that the most appropriate response is provided in a timely manner, it may be desirable. Nor also may the standard escalation model – start locally and then, if necessary, escalate to the national level – be followed. Instead, the ESM Manager will consider whether there are any other factors at play, such as a danger of the identity of the complainant becoming known to the local community (against the wishes of the complainant).

The SEAH GRM is designed specifically for SEAH-related grievances and the ESM Manager will develop a bespoke response approach for each grievance. Given the range of possible grievances, and the need to ensure that the most appropriate response is provided in a timely manner, it may be desirable. ### Ethnic Groups and Indigenous Peoples

#### 387. The projects’ Ethnic Group Plan (EGP) is provided in Annex Bb. Further details about the EGP are provided below (in Section G). A summary of EGP aspects relevant to stakeholder engagement are provided here:

For the purpose of developing the EGP, consultations were held in two provinces in the North – Chiang Rai and Chiang Mai – where the majority of the ethnic groups live. The consultation process was designed to ensure considered culture and gender sensitivity. Diverse consultation formats were applied, including one-on-one meetings with key individuals from each group, focus group discussions with women’s groups and farmers, in small groups and in full-group discussions. Community leaders comprise 3 types of persons, with each type having a different role:

* **Poyabangnan** (which translates as elder of the house) and their assistants: these individuals are elected by the community to represent them at meetings and to make decisions on behalf of the group. They have full authority over the group’s assets and resources.
* Villagers: this designation reflects traditional authority structures of the villages. Groups of elders who acquire this designation from age and wisdom collectively take decisions on traditions, such as selecting authoritative figures or deciding how to use communal land.
* Spiritual leaders: many communities, such as Hmong, Lao, Yao and Akha, worship their ancestors. Spirituality is an important part of life for these groups and they may pray together in times of need or for illnesses.

During implementation, the Thai Rice Project will implement activities to provide climate smart crop farming in mountainous areas by ethnic groups that still identify as indigenous peoples residing in highland areas in the North of Thailand, outside of the project’s target area. Therefore, direct engagement with ethnic groups that self-identify as indigenous peoples is essential for the success of this activity.

A small number of members of ethnic groups residing in highland areas engage as labourers in low-lying rice farming areas, particularly at non-harvest times. During consultations, it was identified that the Akha and Lahu

The Karen are located in lower-land areas and favour rice farming on the same parcel of land year after year. As such, they are likely to be located between farm-rice (where the project will focus) and the uplands where the majority of ethnic groups reside. As such, the Karen warrant particular attention for future engagement efforts.

## 390. Taking into consideration the needs and interests of these ethnic groups (particularly of the Karen, Akha and Lahu), it is important to ensure that the project does not inadvertently displace or negatively impact indigenous peoples who are not located in the project's target areas but who may provide a pool of talent and labour for climate-smart rice farming. The project will therefore need to engage with these groups as well. This will be done through engagement with stakeholders who have experience in engaging ethnic groups in the North, such as the Mah Fah Luang (MFL) Foundation. In addition, the project will ensure that all indigenous peoples affected by the project are informed of or for which capacities needed to be built in climate-smart farming in the languages of ethnic groups and communities they belong to. The project will also ensure that the capacity building process will ensure that unintended negative impacts to members of ethnic groups self-identifying as indigenous peoples based on their own understanding is avoided.

### D.6.2 Economic analysis

396. The economic model is provided in Annex 3a.

397. The principal quantifiable economic benefits of the projects are: (i) the estimated GHG emissions reduction from climate-smart agriculture and sustainable rice production in agriculture by 25,400 farmers; (ii) the increase in income for farms that adopt climate-smart agriculture; and (iii) the value of water saved through the implementation of climate-smart agriculture. The assumptions on which these figures are derived are discussed below.

**GHG Emissions Reductions**

398. The project is expected to mitigate 12.9 Mt CO2e over 15 years. The 15-year period is assumed to start in 2024 – the project is expected to begin in late 2023, but any activities conducted that year would be small and experimental. The project is expected to continue beyond this initial phase with further emissions reductions expected each year until 2038. The total reduction over the life of the project (15 years) is estimated at 11.1 MtCO2e. The project assumes the continued implementation of climate-smart agriculture and sustainable rice production in agriculture by farmers who have adopted these practices, with a further reduction for the subsequent 10 years after project end. The emission reductions will continue thereafter but, considering the limited number of farmers expected to adopt these practices, it is unlikely that any significant emissions reductions would be achieved.

399. The base-case assumption chosen for the shadow price of carbon is Euro 60/tCO2e. The Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) has published a study on the effective carbon prices needed.


---
**Page 138**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 135

To meet the Paris Agreement’s goal of limiting global temperature increases to 1.5°C by mid-century (2070), based on a comprehensive review of studies by academic and policy institutions, the OECD has selected Euro 60 as its mid-range estimate for the cost of decarbonising agriculture in line with this objective. The high-end estimate is Euro 120. To put the OECD’s mid-range estimate into context: 

* **Emission reductions:** In order to meet the Paris Agreement target, the world’s largest emitting countries (the US and China) will need to reduce their emissions by approximately 50% in 2030 compared with 2010 levels. The EU is on track to cut its emissions by around 40% below 1990 levels by 2030.
* **Emission allowances:** In the EU Emissions Trading System (ETS), the world’s largest, reached Euro 58 per tonne CO2-equivalent in October 2020.

### 4.00 The OECD mid-range estimates therefore appear reasonable, and possibly even conservative, in light of EU market prices.

#### Incremental Income for Climate-Smart Farmers

1. Consistent with the emission reduction estimate, the incremental income estimate assumes that all project components are implemented as planned: modern semi-intensive agricultural technologies and practices – 860,000 hectares in the Central Plains, ~147,000 farmers in the North-East and ~35,000 farmers in the North. As acknowledgment of the uncertainties associated with climate change impacts on agriculture, and based on a sensitivity analysis on the empirical experience of baseline and foundation projects, it is conservatively estimated that 80% of mega-projects will be implemented as planned. The remaining 20% are assumed to be delayed or scaled back. This estimate is used for climate-smart rice farming. The price of climate-smart agriculture mimics that in the emissions reduction estimate: Euro 175 per tonne CO2-equivalent. The incremental income estimates are based on a yield increase of 52.4% in 2027 and 72% in 2030. Each farmer is assumed to repeal the climate-smart investment for a new 5-year period, with an average annual discount rate of 10%. The incremental income estimates are based on cashflows shown in the financial (IRR) calculation for the respective region.

2. The total amount of water saved was estimated conservatively at 1.7 billion cubic metres, based on the following assumptions:

* **The principal water-saving technologies are AWW and LLL.** The amount of water saved by AWW varies according to context (climate, soil-type, agro-practices, etc.) but can be reasonably approximated as ~20%.
* **AWW will be adopted in the Central Plains and North but not in the North-East, where only LLL will be applied.** The annual cumulative water savings are estimated over 15 years for the Central Plains and North; the North, the annual and cumulative water savings are estimated over 15 years.

3. An authoritative academic review estimates the shadow price of water saved through water-saving modern rice farming, based on a range of studies in China, India, Indonesia, Thailand, Vietnam and other countries. Using this shadow price and the above volume estimate, an annual shadow value of water saved is estimated. This value is deemed to be conservative.

#### Base-Case Economic IRR (ERIR) and NPV

4.0 With the above assumptions and a total project budget of Euro 117.5 million (GCF grant + co-finance), the Thai Rice Project yields an attractive IRR of 36%. The economic net present value is estimated at Euro 214.3 million, based on a 10% discount rate.


---
**Page 139**

# Sensitivity Analysis

405. Sensitivity analysis indicates that the EIRR and NPV are robust in downside scenarios. For the purposes of the sensitivity analysis, two variables have been considered: (1) lower carbon prices, down to an extremely pessimistic scenario of $2/tonne CO2e; and (2) higher emission reductions and lower incremental farm incomes from the base-case, in 10% negative increments.

406. The analysis shows that the EIRR would be double-digit even if carbon prices dropped to Euro 20 below the OECD average of Euro 50/tonne CO2e, while NPV would still be positive with a carbon price of Euro 30/tonne CO2e and emission reductions / incremental income in line with the base-case. The latter extreme scenario could be the result of adverse weather events such as large scale droughts or floods.

407. The financial model is provided in Annex 3a. Additional commentary is provided in Section B.5.2 relating to assumptions.

## Baseline Assumptions

408. The incremental cashflows are modelled for farmers who switch from conventional rice farming to climate-smart farming over a 5-year implementation cycle. The cashflows of a representative baseline farm in each of the three major rice growing regions (South Asia, East and Southeast Asia, and West Africa) have been used to capture the conditions of the majority of – but not all – farms in each region. Detailed assumptions – derived from

### D.6.3 Financial analysis

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 137

The indicative farm findings of an extensive market study commissioned for the project[46], as well as from BBI data and GIZ field survey data collected for the NSF and other foundation and baseline projects – are described in Annexes.

## 409. The situation of the indicative basefarm can be summarised as follows:

### Central Plains: contains the largest farms generating relatively high yields, produced both in the wet and dry seasons.
The rice produced is of average quality and commands the lowest prices of all regions. Fertilizers, pesticides, and other inputs are applied at a lower rate than in other regions. The indicative farm in this region has relatively large rice sizes, double season and relatively high yields; the indicative farm in the Central Plains produces low pre-tax profits, both in absolute terms (EUR 1,050/year) and term per hectare (EUR 65/ha, Euro 10.4/hta).

### North: mid-size farms, not irrigated and therefore producing only in the wet season, with very low yields.
The rice produced is of poor quality and commands a high price. The indicative farm in this region has a high price, a high cost base despite farmers not paying rent (land is mostly owned) and mostly not using irrigation. The indicative farm in North produces the lowest pre-tax profits both in absolute terms (EUR 263/year) and term per hectare (EUR 10/ha).

### North: smallest farms producing the highest yields (partly due to application of transplating), over 2 seasons.
Rice is of better quality than in the Central Plains but is not at a premium level. Operating costs in these regions are high, partly because of the need for irrigation and land preparation. Irrigation and land preparation are the largest cost items. The indicative farm in the North produces low pre-tax profits in absolute terms (EUR 105/year) and term per hectare (EUR 4/ha). The indicative farm in the North East has a profit of EUR 36/ha, Euro 9/ha due to high yields.

### 410. In general, rice farming in all 3 regions produces only subsistence incomes for all of the farmers and their families. Farmers are not able to generate jobs typically as part-time work, as well as social security subsidies provided by the government. The financial model, however, only looks at farm income and the effects on this from the conversion to conventional rice farming to climate smart rice farming.

#### Climate-Smart Agriculture Assumption:
411. Farmers will have the opportunity to choose from a range of climate-smart technologies and practices, based on the specific conditions of their farms. Modelling all possible permutations would not be practical. The approach chosen is to model one representative farm in each region with a climate-smart package, combining a few technologies in each region. Detailed assumptions are described in Annex 3b.

#### Climate-Smart Agriculture Without GCF Grant:
412. The conversion to climate smart agriculture produces a positive financial IRR (FIRR) in each representative farm:

- For the Central Plains representative farm, the FIRR of switching to climate-smart agriculture is 14.2%. A substantial increase in cash flows is generated by the adoption of climate-smart technologies, which more than offset smaller negative cash flow impacts from other technologies.
- A similar dynamic takes place in the representative North-East farm. Here, however, the yield increase from LIL and SSRM applies only to one farming season and generates proportionally lower positive cashflows due to the reduced number of seasons per year.

#### LLL is not applied in the representative farm in the North, due to the uneven terrain.
Despite the absence of a large investment in LLL in Year 1, the conversion to climate-smart agriculture generates moderately negative cashflows in the first two years. The FIRR over 5 years is 7.93%.

| Scale | At the time of this Funding Proposal formulation, Thailand has a very underdeveloped climate-smart food systems sector. The country remains both emissions-intensive and especially vulnerable to climate change (notably water and heat stress). It is heavily subsidised, with a history of provision of free inputs such as fertilisers and state institutions such as BAAC, thus favouring the continuation of climate-smart rice agriculture | Low | Paradigm shift would involve a shift away from current practices – such as chemical fertiliser use in rice fields and burning of rice residues – that are both emissions-intensive and that leave farmers exposed to extreme temperatures towards a sector characterised by climate-resilient actors (farmers, service providers, smallholder groups) who can implement and finance climate-smart solutions. This would lead to step change shift on GHG emissions and city air pollution. | The Thai Rice Project is conservatively projected to 12.56 MtCO2e of emission reductions and to enable ~253,400 farmers to implement climate-smart changes in their rice production systems. It will also help step towards paradigm shift on GHG emissions and city air pollution. The project will ensure that farmers and other vulnerable farming groups (e.g., smallholder groups, women generation households) will ensure inclusive impacts.
| Replicability | Food systems are one of the most important sectors for reducing emissions, as well as being one of the most adversely affected by climate change. | Low | If rice sector in 21 Thai provinces were to be able to successfully transition to a low-emission and resilient system and do so while increasing farmers’ income, this would serve as a model for other sectors seeking to develop domestic and international markets for climate-smart rice. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL (V.10.3) PAGE 141

## Sustainability

The sustainability of the current rice farming model is questionable. Since 2015 global climate change has revealed the vulnerability of the rice sector to extreme weather events, and GHG emissions continue to increase and reach record levels despite significant problem despite repeated government efforts to reduce them in the rice sector.

### Low

#### Paradigm shift would see the development of a self-sustaining climate-smart rice sector in which farmers are able to adopt climate-smart technologies and practices with little or no external interest to do so and because they have access to information, awareness, financial and market-based incentives needed to overcome initial adoption barriers.

The Thai Rice Project is assessed as having high sustainability potential. The project will:

- Maintain the use of a range of climate-smart farming practices that are economically viable, financially beneficial at farm level and are therefore likely to be adopted by farmers.
- Reduce GHG emissions per unit of rice produced, quality reduction costs, premium price, reduced vulnerability to extreme weather events.
- Conducted by the national agricultural extension service and other relevant actors in the private sector, in order to ensure the widespread adoption of climate-smart practices and technologies.

The project will also:

- Create markets for climate-smart rice and establish the self-sustaining climate-smart rice sector through private sector provision of technologies to adapt farming practices and reduce GHG emissions.
- Strengthen market linkages strengthened through the TAS and other actors in the private sector.
- Scale up the TAS as a national standard to create market opportunities for Thai agricultural produce in the Thai market.

- Support the design, operationalisation and launch of a new Thaichan Climate Smart Agriculture (TCSA) as an additional source of additional finance for the climate-smart agriculture sector.
- Strengthen the individual capacities of national institutions responsible for the implementation of BACs to implement climate-informed measures in the agricultural sector. This will be achieved through and capacitated ThaIC - and enhance their ability to access international markets, including through the new Thai Rice Facility.

The project targeted area of production extends over a physical area of ~718,000 ha. Of this, 54% is rain-fed and ~46% is rain-fed. As rice can be planted on the same field, the 'effective farmland' (i.e., planting area) amounts to ~1,072,900 ha. The dry season planting area (~amounts to ~1,072,900 ha) is applied to the effective farmland (i.e., planting area).

Application of climate-smart agricultural technologies and practices is assumed to be applied on 50% of the total planting area in the following schedule (see Section D.13):

* Year 1 = ~362,450 ha
* Year 2 = 879,850 ha
* Year 3 = 504,450 ha
* Year 4 = 228,850 ha
* Year 5 = 114,450 ha

This equates to:

* Year 1 = ~77,200 ha
* Year 2 = 193,600 ha
* Year 3 = 154,400 ha
* Year 4 = 87,200 ha
* Year 5 = 45,200 ha

The mid-term estimate is calculated at a linear mid-way interpolation of the Year 2 and Year 5 estimates (since the project duration is 5 years)...

Development Strategy (S.T.)

Economic development relies on climate-resilient technologies in rice cultivation as a priority need.

The Adaptation Needs Assessment (ANA) for Thailand lists 5 technology priorities, including weather forecasting and early warning systems, crop technology improvement (drought tolerance), water optimization and disease and pest control. These are identified as pressing needs, precision agriculture measures (including water management and post-harvest management) and post-harvest technology.

Core indicator: Degree in which GCF investments contribute to the development/ transformation at the sectoral, local or national level

The markets for sustainable rice, including climate-smart agricultural services and technologies are under-developed and are constrained by capacity, policy and financial barriers.

Functioning markets, consisting of willing, informed and committed participants, exist for co-products such as residues, climate-smart agricultural inputs and rice sector GHG emissions. These markets provide opportunities to promote climate-resilient rice production and attract ongoing investment.

The project will facilitate the roll-out and enhancement of the Thai Agricultural Standard (TAS) for Sustainable Rice Farming for rice resisters will receive technical and financial assistance from service providers are enabled and incentivized to participate in the VER Rice scheme. The VER Rice scheme will monetize carbon sequestration in the rice sector and will recycle the carbon credits generated by the project to develop a market development plan for sustainable rice, including export promotion.

National level (one country)

The project will achieve reduced agro-chemical use and pollution through adoption of SSNM and IPM. Individual farmers will apply different suites of climate-smart practices, including the adoption of SSNM and IPM. The project targets are based on a detailed analysis about Focus Interventions and Supporting Activities that underpin the GHG modeling.

Because baselines have location-specific characteristics (e.g., region, by district, by irrigation network and even within clusters of rice fields), different methods are best suited as % reductions.

The active ingredients in agro-chemicals are more than just their quality; because the active ingredients in agro-chemicals can be applied at different rates, timing, and locations. Accurate detection and measurement of these active ingredients is difficult. Different active ingredients have different effects on water quality (e.g., herbicides vs. fertilizers). The effect of a single pesticide over timescales and on different components of the aquatic ecosystem. A reliable assessment of water quality somewhat depends on the ability to distinguish the effects superimposed by other pollutants not related to the project.

An indicator monitoring the change of groundwater quality is proposed for this project to estimate impacts on groundwater quality. However, changes in groundwater quality can be very difficult to assess through surface water samples.

## Baseline to be established at project inception

Project supported farmers apply climate smart management practices on 80% of their farming areas (mega farm/non-mega farm)

Project supported farmers apply climate smart management practices on 30% /20% of their farming areas (mega farm/non-mega farm)

Each year, a representative sample of farmers will be selected to ensure that their diversion of biomass resources for other purposes has not led to increased burning and haze pollution. If this is found to be a problem, farmer training and adaptive measures will be put in place (see section 10.2.1 & 10.2.2).

The indicator adopted is an indirect one: the atmospheric concentration of PM2.5.

Use of a direct indicator (atmospheric concentration of PM2.5) was not possible in this context because:

(i) The PM2.5 concentration is highly influenced by factors outside of the project's control, including transport, sugarcane and maize burning, and weather patterns (e.g., high burning, and weather patterns); and

(ii) There are no reliable data on PM2.5 concentrations particularly in the Central Plains and the areas bordering with neighboring countries.

The project will reduce smoke / haze pollution primarily through its support of climate smart management practices and different suites of climate smart management practices of SSM suitable to local conditions. The project will also test and validate the assumptions that underlie the project GHG emissions estimates for both Mitigation and Supportive Interventions.

## E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

### E.7.1 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

416. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (in addition to the ESMP and GAP) will comply with the relevant GCF policies, as stipulated in the AMA, FAO and other relevant agreements between the GIZ, the Project Management Unit and Implementation Partners, which Executing Entities will extend to sub-grantees.

417. The Thai Rice Project will apply a customised results-based Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) system. The M&E system will track project inputs, activities, sub-activities and impacts, as well as associated financial flows, across all outputs in all project provinces.

418. The system will be based on:
* GIZ’s Monitoring and Evaluation Procedures (GIZ evaluation policy – principles, guidelines and requirements).
    * Monitoring and evaluation plan (Annex 11a)
    * The M&E framework
    * The project implementation schedule.
    * Reporting requirements: Annual Performance Report.
    * Procedures and requirements of project partners and stakeholders.

419. The oversight for M&E lies with GIZ as the Accredited Entity and, specifically, the GIZ GCF Oversight Unit. The Project Management Unit (PMU) will implement the M&E system and will work closely with the Executing Entities as well as government, private sector and development partners.

### Recruitment of M&E Expert

420. Immediately when the Thai Rice Project commences, GIZ’s GCF Oversight Unit at head office in Germany will make available one expert to oversee and coordinate the project M&E reporting routines. As soon as GCF processes become available, the project will recruit one full-time M&E specialist. The specific responsibilities of this position will be defined by the PMU in consultation with GIZ. The PMU may also consider the job-sharing for dedicated M&E experts from the PMU, the Executing Entities and other stakeholders of the system where necessary and requested.


---
**Page 172**
---

Independent Monitoring and Evaluation Studies

421. GIZ/GIZ Overall Unit will initiate a non-term evaluation and a final evaluation of the project. GIZ evaluations are carried out by GIZ’s independent evaluation unit which is steered by a corporate unit that is separate from operational business and reports directly to the Management Board. Moreover, evaluations are conducted with the support of external evaluators.

422. GIZ applies the Evaluation Principles for International Development Cooperation (2008, revised 2020) on a standard basis in project evaluations. These are based on the evaluation criteria agreed by the Development Assistance Committee (DAC) of the OECD: relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, overarching development results (impact), coherence and sustainability, in addition to the OECD/DAC evaluation criteria, the following GIZ criteria will be evaluated: responsiveness, appropriateness, cost-effectiveness, quality of implementation, project management, stakeholder engagement, risk assessment, areas representing best practice, and unexpected results.

423. Regarding the GCF’s Evaluation Principles for Objectivity and unbiased and unobtrusive use and participation; all provisions for upholding the GCF evaluation principles described in Chapter V (a) of the Evaluation Policy are followed (see DAC standards and OECD/Development standards). In line with this, the project management team is not directly responsible for the design setting, design or implementation of an evaluation project.

424. Regarding GIZ’s Evaluation Principle for Creditability and Robustness; as a matter of principle, GIZ case uses to an approximation combination of quantitative and qualitative methods of empirical social research in order to assess the effectiveness of projects. A variety of methods is adopted for data collection and evaluation questions. To ensure robust evidence, GIZ uses a theory-based approach as a minimum standard for its central project evaluations. The contribution analysis has been designed with a focus on the identification of causal relationships between inputs, outputs, intermediate results, and outcomes. This allows to identify how changes can be associated with a project. Furthermore, the analyses should increase the understanding and knowledge of what works, what does not work and why.

425. Projects will be rated based on the 8 OECD/DAC and the 7 GCF evaluation criteria. Each of the OECD/DAC and GCF criteria is rated on a scale of 1 to 100. Using a points system serves to increase the transparency of ratings, which enable better comparability between individual projects.

426. An Interim Independent Evaluation Report and a Final Independent Evaluation Report will be submitted to the GIZ as per timeline agreed in the FAA.

427. Results from the mid-term evaluation should be reflected in the project design, to influence upscaling, and improve overall project implementation (adaptive management).

Data Collection and Frequency

428. The PMU will coordinate data collection for implemented activities through the responsible Executing Entities, their relevant executing departments / divisions at different levels, and any other relevant stakeholders. The PMU will supervise and guide the monitoring, reporting and evaluation and make all detailed reports as well as summary reports available to GIZ’s Oversight Unit at least every 6 months.


---
**Page 173**
---

E.7.2 Monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) of climate mitigation impacts

429. In addition to the project MAE system described above, the Thai Rice Project will utilise the MRV system that was developed at project level to monitor the mitigation progress of the NSP by the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MAAC) and the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MNRE) in cooperation with the Department of Agricultural Economics (DAE), the Department of Agricultural Extension (DAE), the Department of Agricultural Economics (GAE, under MAIC) and ONEP (under MNRE), the latter of which runs the Thailand Greenhouse Gas Emissions Inventory System (TGGEIS). The MRV system for the NSP is still in development. Therefore, the project-level MRV system has not been defined. Thus, the project-level MRV system of the NSP can serve as a blueprint for a similar MRV system under the Thai Rice Project, and mitigation progress will be monitored using this system.

430. Farm activity data is collected by RD through farmer’s monthly monitoring (and annual aggregation). The Thai Rice Project will utilise this data collection methodology and develop it further (under RD), mindful of data protection regulations and in adherence with the principle of data minimisation. The scope for use of a digital (app-based) system to collect farm activity data is currently under consideration by RD. This would be an important development, as it would allow for a key design principle, as neither the user-base (rice farmers, many of whom are elderly) nor the downstream data collection actors can afford a time-consuming, computer-literate approach.

431. Field sampling of GHG emissions from rice fields (methane, nitrous oxide), including subsequent analysis by gas chromatography (accompanied by staff training), have been undertaken since 2019 under the foundation project; the Thai Rice Project will address remaining institutional and capacity gaps in the national system to ensure that this methodology is used for MRV purposes. The focus of the field sampling will be on rice fields with a high GHG emission potential, with particular focus, in particular, on growing areas that have been poorly sampled so far, notably the rain-fed agro-ecosystems in the North and North-East of Thailand.

E.7.3 Monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) of climate adaptation impacts

432. Monitoring of adaptation impacts requires a robust enumeration of the number of farmers (disaggregated by socio-economic – e.g., smallholder vs major farm- maine), as well as an assessment of the extent to which they have benefited from the Thai Rice Project technical or financial support. Enumeration of direct beneficiaries (the farmers) will be undertaken in conjunction with the national rice sector MRV system (see above), as well, it with relevant stakeholders (e.g., RD, MAAC, MNRE). The number of indirect beneficiaries (e.g., households, communities, local government authorities, and other household members) will be enumerated using the farmer books in conjunction with surveys and official data sources (census, DAE databases, etc.).

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Selected risk mitigation measures

A detailed analysis of The Rice Project implementation risks is provided in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2). Environmental and social risks are considered in the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment, the Environmental and Social Management Plan, and the Gender Action Plan (Annexes B4 and B6). Gender-specific risks are considered in the Gender Assessment (Annex B5) and the Gender Action Plan (Annex B6).

From a safeguards perspective, The Thai Rice Project is rated as Medium (Category B).

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

### Description

The Thai Rice Project is constructed on the assumption that the Government of Thailand is fully committed to supporting climate-smart rice farming and will sustain its engagement accordingly. Furthermore, challenges associated with COVID-19 and the slowing global economy may impact project support.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

* The Thai Rice Project addresses a sector – rice farming – that is culturally, economically and politically important. The diversity of stakeholders involved in the sector, including farmers, traders, processors, and consumers, awareness of the impacts of climate change, and willingness to address them, are higher than ever before. Indeed, the government is currently implementing several policies and programs aimed at achieving mitigation and adaptation objectives, and a statutory role for the National Climate Change Committee. The 13th NESDP (2021-2030) sets out the national climate change strategy and action plan.

* The NESDP reaffirms the government’s commitment to the NDC and identifies climate change risks – notably, drought and flooding – as a priority for the country.

* Thailand's 4th Updated NDC (2022) identifies climate-smart agriculture - including methane management from rice farming and site-specific nutrient management – as a national priority. The LT-LEDS was also updated in 2022 with the specific objective of promoting climate-smart agriculture, which includes the adoption of improved rice varieties.

* Identified agricultural priorities in the updated LT-LEDS are: manure management, agricultural waste management, integrated pest and disease control, water use efficiency, soil fertility improvement, conservation tillage, intercropping of rice and alternative rice varieties, improved water efficiency, promotion of organic fertilizers, increased renewable energy use, and the development of climate-resilient infrastructure. The NESDP also identifies climate-smart farming systems for planning management (e.g., crop calendars, water management strategies, etc.). The NAMA Support Project (NAMAS) is a key project that will provide technical assistance to farmers in Thailand to scale-up climate-smart rice farming.

* Government institutions have been closely involved in the origination, development and design of the Thai Rice Project both at national level and at the local level. The project has also benefited from past and ongoing government support, including from institutions – the Rice Department, BAAC and ONEP - that will serve as key partners for this project.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

### Description

The Thai Rice Project will be implemented by 5 Executing Entities. All Outputs, and the majority of Activities and Sub-Activities, are associated with more than one Executing Entity. There is a risk that this will result in lack of clarity over who is responsible for what, leading to unnecessary duplication or gaps in decision-making or project accountability.


---
**Page 176**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 173

## Mitigation Measure(s)

The project management structure is not considered to be especially complex. Indeed, the involvement of 3 national Executing Entities – including the national institution responsible for rice sector policy, the national institution responsible for climate change and agriculture, and the national institution responsible for water resources – as well as the UNFCCC and the GCF! serves as a strong indicator of the high level of national support and ownership of the project. The project will be implemented by 3 national Executing Entities (NEEs) in collaboration with the CGIAR’s International Rice Research Institute (IRRI) – an institution that is recognized worldwide for its leading source of technical expertise on rice farming.

As indicated in Table 15, two-thirds of the project's Sub-Activities (i.e. 12 out of 18) each have only one Executing Entity and therefore are considered to be Single-Executing Entities. At the Activity level, three-quarters (i.e. 6 out of 8) of Activities have 2 or fewer Executing Entities.

Where a Sub-Activity or Activity is implemented by more than one Executing Entity, a Lead Executing Entity (identified in Table 15) has been designated to coordinate and manage the implementation of that Sub-Activity or Activity. The project has been co-designed by 5 Executive Directors, building up 2 years of intense collaboration: all the Executive Directors are aware of their roles and responsibilities and have very supportive of the supervisory, management and reporting structure that will be put in place to coordinate their interventions.

### Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Most rice farmers are poor and risk-averse, many (~20%) are old and many are old and reluctant to change their existing practices. The project will work with farmers to introduce new technologies that will reduce their vulnerability and potentially reduce their ability’s willingness to respond to climate change. As a result, farmers may not proactively engage with the This Rice Project and its associated actions.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The recent drought has witnessed thousands of farmers taking spontaneous, uncoordinated action to adapt to climate change by abandoning rice farming altogether or by switching away from rice altogether. The mobilization to date of smallholder farmer interest in climate-smart agriculture achieved by the set of foundations and building blocks that have been put into place over the past 20 years is not sufficient for farmers to make a rapid, urgent need – to change. The This Rice Project will provide farmers with the knowledge and tools to do so, and develop an enabling environment for them to do so. The project will also provide financial incentives to encourage farmers to take up climate-smart actions.

The project’s financial instruments - incentive payments, grants channelized through Thaïaid and the Rural Development Fund (RDF) scheme – are specifically designed to be low-cost and accessible to smallholder rice farmers.

### Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Mid-season drainage (a form of mild intermittent flooding that causes a single long duration event) brings complex challenges for the management of nitrogen fertilizer applied to the paddy field. The This Rice Project will work with farmers to introduce site-specific nutrient management (SSNM) practices which is also a condition of farmers receiving ATZ certification, thereby lowering N2O emissions.

In any case additional NO3 production as a result of AWD is controlled via the low AWD is an internationally managed process that allows water to drop down to 15cm below soil level, which roughly corresponds to a medium intermittent flooding event.

**Page 177**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 174

## Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

### Description

Water scarcity is a growing problem. Even rice farmers with access to irrigation systems, who can normally cultivate rice in the dry season (2019, 2020 and 2021) were only able to plant rice in the wet season. One of the Thai Rice Project's objectives was to increase rice production for irrigated rice. Therefore, there is a risk that climate change, especially severe drought events, will undermine the project.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

### The Thai Rice Project incorporates a strong adaptation strategy alongside the mitigation element, precisely to allow farmers to respond to the growing problem of water scarcity.

The GHG emission reduction estimates presented in Section 10.3.14 are based on the fact that: (i) baseline agricultural practices will continue; and (ii) for rice farmers who are currently practicing continuous flooding, open burning and excessive use of nitrogen fertilizer, continuous flooding is likely to become less prevalent because of water scarcity.

## Selected Risk Factor 6

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Description

In rural Thailand, a major challenge for the women who are currently employed in unpaid or informal work, including day-to-day household management, which entails obtaining clean water for household use, finding or procuring energy sources to cook food, and collecting firewood. Women also face challenges related to their lack of access to formal employment and they risk being excluded from training and access to finance. Women rice farmers in Thailand are often the most vulnerable group within the project area, targeted overwhelmingly in the paddy arena and consistently underserved by agricultural and rural support services.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

### These gender-related issues are fully acknowledged as part of the Gender Action Plan. A Gender Assessment and a Gender Action Plan have been developed (see Annexes Aa and Bb), and a comprehensive set of actions has been incorporated into the project’s implementation plan. The measures include: (i) providing training to women rice farmers, extension officers under Sub-Activity 1.1.2 and training of SAGC loan officers under Sub-Activity 2.1.2—so as to address gender-related issues; (ii) targeting the most vulnerable categories of women (such as single-generation households, women with disabilities and migrants).

**Page 178**

Selected Risk Factor: 7

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

Description

Rice farming is a water-intensive activity in an environment that is projected to experience increasing (frequency / magnitude of) water scarcity. By supporting rice farming, the Thai Rice Project runs the risk of encouraging environmental degradation and contributing to climate change.

Mitigation Measure(s)

Rice farming is deeply embedded within the social, economic, and political fabric of Thailand. To expect rice farming to simply disappear and be replaced by different crops is unrealistic and undesirable, particularly given the current reliance on rice as a staple food in Thailand. The Thai Rice Project should therefore seek to help farmers respond to climate change by modifying the ways they grow rice and by providing alternatives (alternative crops and irrigation methods) that will enable them to implement their own adaptation strategies as circumstances require, equipped with the appropriate training and methods.

Moreover, climate models are clear that the total volume of precipitation in Thailand is unlikely to decrease (and it may even increase slightly), but there is a high risk of an increase in the frequency and intensity of extreme events such as precipitation events – i.e., by rains being more concentrated in time and less evenly distributed across the year. Longer-term climate models suggest that this will lead to increased water scarcity, which could reduce rice yields by at least some of the projected water scarcity. These solutions are outside of the scope of the Thai Rice Project (see Thai Rice Project’s long-term proposition), but they do suggest that the rice sector can and will continue to play a prominent role in Thailand.

Given the projected 35% increase in global rice demand by 2050 and the limited scope available for other rice-producing countries, it is important to note that the Thai Rice Project has an opportunity to contribute significantly towards ensuring that Thailand continues to play a critical role in ensuring global rice supply. This is critically important in the current context of rising global food prices and wheat shortages.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment

### ESA: Environmental and Social Assessment

436. Environmental and social risks are considered in the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment, the Environmental and Social Management Plan, the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESA- ESMF), and the Project Design Document (PDD).

437. The ESA-ESMF was adopted according to the GCF’s Revised Environmental and Social Policy (dated September 2021). The information and data used to formulate ESA were obtained from reviewing the draft project document, including the environmental and social baseline studies; background studies and data, including government documents, academic papers and grey literature; and extensive consultations with stakeholders. These include representatives of indigenous peoples and local communities, civil society organizations, and other relevant groups.

438. The project is categorized as Category B (medium) / Medium 2 (high). This rating is based on the consideration that, individually or cumulatively:

* Each Environmental and Social Risk Element (ESRE) is rated Low (ESE 1, ESS 5, ESS 6), Medium (ESE 2, ESS 3, ESS 4, ESE 7, ESS 8, ESS 9) or rated Medium (ESE 1, ESS 4, ESE 10). Moreover, SEAH risk is rated Low; emergency preparedness and response measures are in place.

* Generally site-specific: ESREs are confined to smallholder rice fields and their environment.

* The project does not involve the use of hazardous substances (Hazardous Material (HM), IEDS (landmines) and IEDs (palestides))—either to reduction or avoidance of the use of a particular resource, so inventories are not required.

* Readily addressed through mitigation measures, which are described in detail, fully linked to detailed technical documentation.

439. The Rice Project has the potential to cause moderate negative environmental and social impacts. These potentially include impacts on water quality through contamination by chemicals and fertilizers, degraded soil quality, loss of biodiversity, and adverse effects on human health and safety. However, these risks are managed in the context of limited farmer knowledge about these technologies. The project does not require or involve land conversion, resettlement, displacement of people, or relocation of communities. Moreover, economic displacement will be conducted in protected areas or sensitive locations.

440. The project offers substantial positive environmental and social impacts for the beneficiaries in North, Northeast, East, and West Java (the four regions where the project is implemented) as well as other provinces (and others). The project offers positive global environmental impacts, notably in terms of reduced GHG emissions, improved water use efficiency, and reduction of greenhouse gas emissions from reduced water use and air pollution.

### and social risks and impacts

Department (LSD) and the Royal Irrigation Department (RID), all under the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MAC). Assessment, monitoring and reporting are carried out by the Rice Research Centre (RRC) and the Rice Research Institute (RRI) which operates an extensive branch network. Financial assistance to rice farmers is provided through the National Agricultural Credit Bank (NACB).

- **Water distribution**: Water distribution is a significant environmental issue in rice cultivation, as rice requires substantial but varying amounts of water throughout the entire growing period. The LSD has established a comprehensive system for water distribution and management.

- **Soil quality**: Soil quality varies throughout the project area, from approximately 2.3% in the North to more than 4%. This variation is due to differences in soil type, topography, and land use history. LSD also operates a 'soil doctor' service, among other services, which provides advice on soil management practices.

- **Land preparation**: Land preparation using the direct seeding technique is a significant environmental hazard associated with rice production. The Pollution Control Department and the Rice Research Centre have developed guidelines for the use of this technique to minimize its impact on the environment.

- **Tractor usage**: Conventional machines like tractors or hand ploughing machines and thus reduce workload and increase efficiency in land preparation, but also increase fuel consumption and air pollution. High demand for LLS services may reduce local demand for labour and increase the use of hired labourers who are often paid less than those employed by farmers.

- **Adaptation**: The Rice Project will strengthen environmental and social governance (land usage, water quality, air pollution, GHG emissions, working conditions, etc.). The project will also promote the adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices. These include improved soil management techniques such as conservation tillage, reduced fertilizer use in the environment, reduced water consumption and improved irrigation systems, and the use of organic fertilizers.

- **Ecosystem services**: The project's ESSS includes policy identification, definition of risks and opportunities, stakeholder engagement, governance mechanisms and monitoring and review. These will be strengthened. The project's ESSS includes policy identification, definition of risks and opportunities, stakeholder engagement, governance mechanisms and monitoring and review.

### ESS-2 Labour and working conditions

#### Low

- **Labour shortages**: Labour shortages are a significant issue in the rice sector, particularly during the wet season (as most farms are not fed). This can generate labour shortages and lead to higher costs for farmers. The project will address this by promoting the use of mechanized equipment such as tractors and ploughing machines.

- **Gender roles**: Men and women typically have different roles in rice farming, with men often responsible for land preparation and women for planting and weeding. This division is generally driven by cultural norms rather than economic necessity. The project will promote gender equality in the agricultural sector through training and capacity building programmes.

- **Child labour**: Child labour is a significant issue in the rice sector, particularly among children aged 5 to 14 years old. The project will address this by promoting child-friendly practices such as the use of mechanized equipment and the establishment of child-friendly work environments. Farm mechanization is weakening this traditional division of labour.

- **Wages**: Agricultural workers are employed year-round but not all have access to social security benefits, including health insurance, pensions, and other social protection measures. The project will address this by promoting fair wages and working conditions for agricultural workers through the implementation of the Labour Protection Act (LPA). Agricultural workers who are employed year-round but not on a permanent basis may be eligible for some social security benefits under the Social Security Act.

- **Agricultural Sector**: The inter alia, specific mandatory holiday, sick leave and maternity rights. Self-employed agricultural workers or those whose employment is for fewer than 180

### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and prevention

#### Low

- **Water use:** Water is a scarce resource in many parts of the world, including neighbouring farms and the local vicinity – by reducing water use, we can reduce the pressure on this resource. In addition to the project, chemical and fertilizer use will still be necessary. It is important that farmers are aware of the negative environmental impacts associated with these chemicals and fertilizers.
- **Soil erosion:** Soil erosion is a major problem in many parts of the world, including neighbouring farms and the local vicinity – by reducing soil erosion, we can reduce the pressure on this resource. The aim should be to ensure that environmental contamination and health impacts can be avoided or minimized.
- **Chemical use:** Chemicals are used extensively in agriculture, but their impact is not widely known. Monitoring, reporting and effective management of chemicals will help farmers and communities avoid negative environmental impacts. Project activities that promote straw baling will be very helpful in reducing the amount of chemical fertilizers needed for crop production.
