- **Water efficiency:** Water efficiency can be improved through ARRO and farm-level water management, but this is not a priority at present due to high demand of water and inefficiencies in water distribution. This will be addressed through the project.
- **Soil erosion:** Soil erosion is a major problem in many parts of the world, including neighbouring farms and the local vicinity – by reducing soil erosion, we can reduce the pressure on this resource. The aim should be to ensure that environmental contamination and health impacts can be avoided or minimized.

# Table 26: Thai Rice Project ESS Mitigation Measures

## Environmental & Social Safeguards

### Project Mitigation Measures

#### ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

- The project ESS management system will be established and operationalised.
- A dedicated ESM will be staffed, resourced and implemented throughout project implementation.
- Capacity building for the ESM will be provided to key stakeholders including institutions with EWS mandate (e.g. ministry, MAFIC) and civil departments, consultants, farmers’ organisations and other relevant stakeholders.
- The ESM will include a risk register (Activity Sub-Action 1.1.2), a list of risks (Activities 3.1.2, 3.1.2.1, 3.1.2.2) and a risk management plan (Activities 3.1.2.3 and 3.1.2.4).
- The ESM will be used to monitor and evaluate environmental, climate-smart rice (Sub-Action 3.1.1).

#### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions

- Measures will be included to ensure that the project is implemented in a manner which effectively and efficiently transfer farmers’ climate risks to insurance markets (Sub-Activity 4.1.1).
- The project will establish a National Rice Facilitator as a coordinating and peer-learning platform for the project.
- Development of the T-VER Rice Scheme as an environmentally robust carbon finance mechanism (Sub-Action 5.2.3).
- Support to identified NDC needs and sectors under MRV to improve the quality of climate data and information (Sub-Activity 6.1.1).

#### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

- Gender-based inequalities and divisions of labour will be addressed by the project’s activities.
- The GAP also includes measures to address the employment vulnerabilities of migrants working in rice production, including those from Myanmar (Sub-Action 7.2).
- Measures will be included to address the aging profile of rice farmers, including – as part of the project’s capacity building and training programme – a focus on young people.
- The project will implement activities that are implementing activities to reduce rural exodus (Sub-Activity 1.1.1.3).

#### ESS 4: Community health, safety

- The project will include measures which will reduce physical labour requirements of farming (Initiative Sub-Activity 1.1.1.3).
- The project will implement activities that are implementing activities to reduce working activities (premium rice, biomass energy, etc.) (Sub-Activities 1.1.1.1, 1.1.1.2, 1.1.1.3).

#### Farmers who feel that project activities threaten their working conditions or present new risks will be able to lodge a complaint with the grievance mechanism (Sub-Activity 4.5.3).
- The project will include measures which will reduce the risk of child labour and forced labour in the project – with consequences loss of technical and financial support – if they employ such workers.
- Site visits to farmers undertaken by project staff for the purposes of M&E and stakeholder engagement will be conducted in a manner that is sensitive to gender, age and social inclusion (Sub-Activity 1.2.3).
- Farmers who are found to employ forced labour or child labour in contradiction of national laws will be referred to the relevant authorities.

#### LLL, AWG, GISR, rice variety diversification and crop diversification will reduce water requirements for farming (Sub-Activities 5.1.1, 5.2.1).
- AWG will be coordinated with Water User Organisations to reduce the scope for irrigation water abstraction.
- The project will include measures which will reduce water pollution (Sub Activities 3.1.1.1, 3.1.1.1.1, 3.1.1.2, 3.1.2.1 and 3.1.2.1.1).
- Agro-met advisory services will improve efficiency and applications timings, reducing the risk of water pollution (Sub Activities 3.1.1.1, 3.1.1.1.1, 3.1.1.2, 3.1.2.1 and 3.1.2.1).
- LLL and AWG will markedly reduce water requirements for farming which, wet in turn, without farmers’ exposure to malaria, leishmaniasis and other insect-borne parasites (Sub Activities 3.1.1.1, 3.1.1.1.1, 3.1.1.2, 3.1.2.1, 3.1.2.1.1).

## ESS 7: Indigenous peoples

- Contact details for the project’s grievance redress mechanism (GRM) will be made available to all stakeholders involved. The GRM will include a toll-free telephone number, notice boards, local government institutions, community and CSO websites, etc., so that any affected person can lodge their grievances or complaints about how their land is being used without their permission and be able to submit official complaints.
- A survey of the project area will be conducted to identify indigenous peoples (IPs) who are likely to be affected by the project. The survey will include a review of existing data, if available, and will be undertaken as part of the project’s baseline assessment and mid-term review. Parental consent for children under 18 years old is required before they can participate in any surveys.
- IPs belonging to an indigenous group (such as women and vulnerable individuals) engaged in mixed farming will be included in the project interventions, although the project will not provide direct support to these groups. The concept of Free Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) participation in the project will be sought from all IPs who are likely to be affected by the project.
- The project M&E plan will be designed in an ethically sensitive way.

A survey of the project area will be conducted to identify indigenous peoples (IPs) who are likely to be affected by the project. The survey will include a review of existing data, if available, and will be undertaken as part of the project’s baseline assessment and mid-term review. Parental consent for children under 18 years old is required before they can participate in any surveys.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 183

#### ESS 8: Cultural Heritage

- Meaningful and informed participation of individuals and groups throughout the processes, with due consideration to generate equity, social inclusion and safeguarding the cultural heritage.
- To improve female representation, women from self-defined indigenous peoples will be prioritized in all project activities (Sub-Activity 1.2).
- The project will only implement activities on land that is classified as existing rice farming areas or other agricultural lands.
- The project will, at all times, be respectful of local traditions and customs. For instance, the project will not use any traditional names for new infrastructure projects unless they are planned their practices. The project will provide scientifically grounded input data and information to stakeholders in a timely manner (Sub-Activity 1.2).
- The project will respect and preserve cultural heritage as well as the rights of indigenous peoples to maintain and protect their traditions and customs.
- The project will ensure that all public activities (e.g., brochures, videos, etc.) will reflect traditional values and practices.

#### ESS 9: Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

- The project will implement a robust and inclusive Stakeholder Engagement Plan (Sub-Activity 1.3).
- Particular efforts will be made to engage with and support vulnerable stakeholders, including women, youth, older persons, people living in rural areas, indigenous peoples, ethnic groups, migrants and pilgrims (Sub-Activity 1.1.1).

#### ESS 10: Climate change resilience and adaptation

- The project will operate training plans that serve to provide farmers with real-world knowledge of climate smart technologies and practices.
- The project will support the development of a gender-sensitive disaster risk management plan, which will be integrated into the national disaster risk management framework (Sub-Activity 2.1).
- Where female farmers cannot be fully eliminated, the project will also support their participation in insurance schemes to mitigate risks related to climate change and other shocks (Sub-Activity 2.1.3).

#### Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Harassment (SEAR)

- Project staff members of all genders and/or financial support will have access to confidential counseling services provided by the project’s general manager (Sexual Exploitation and Harassment Management Plan) (see Section D.5).
- SEAH will be addressed immediately in a case-by-case basis.

#### Emergency preparedness and response

- Project staff members of all genders and/or financial support will have access to confidential counseling services provided by the project’s general manager (Sexual Exploitation and Harassment Management Plan) (see Section D.5).
- The project will ensure that all public meetings have third-party liability insurance to cover death and injury in case of accidents or other incidents.
- Project staff members of all genders and/or financial support will be capable of providing farmers with climate smart farming functionality (Sub-Activity 2.1.3).

#### Human rights

- The project will provide training for project staff on human rights issues, including gender equality and the rights of indigenous peoples.

# G.1.3 Environmental and social management framework (ESMF)

## ESMF General Principles

* Investments supported by The Thai Rice Project must, in all instances, be legal and aligned with the low-emission, climate-sensitive objectives of the project.
* No funding shall be approved or disbursed that expresses, or represents a significant risk of exceeding, a Category 1 ESMF threshold.
* Financial transfers shall be made only to identified individuals or institutions who meet the eligibility criteria of the relevant instrument.

Subject to the constraints set by instrument-specific eligibility criteria, all funding decisions shall be non-discriminatory and transparent in accordance with the project’s procurement policy.

* All funding decisions—positive or negative—shall be fully documented and stored for at least a period of 5 years (except where otherwise specified).

* Project-supported investments shall be subject to MEF to ensure compliance with eligibility rules and to assess environmental and social impacts.

* Funding decisions and resulting payments shall be subject to the project’s grievance redress mechanism.

* These principles are reviewed by the ESMF Committee at least once a year (or more frequently, if required) and, as required, amended to improve the social and environmental protections afforded by the project.

## Incentive Payments to Farmers

* Only registered farmers who have received training on climate-smart agriculture and financial management and who are using one or more of these technologies shall be eligible to receive incentive payments.
* Incentive payments can be used by farmers to finance any of The Thai Rice Project’s supported climate-smart technologies (see Table 13: LLL, AWD, DSMM, SSM, crop diversification, DSR and FIRM).
* Climate-smart activities financed or part-financed by incentive payments shall be conducted on land that is classified as rice farmland.
* Farmers who are using one of these technologies and practices and that are procured by farmers using incentive payments must be included in the register of quality-assessed service providers that is maintained by MEF.
* A registry of incentive payment recipients shall be maintained by BAAC and this will include location information. All data on the registry, including location information, shall be kept confidential.

## Climate-Smart Loan (CSL) Scheme

* BAAC standard lending conditions and eligibility criteria (as relating to creditworthiness) shall apply to the CSL scheme.
* In addition, only (i) registered farmers who have received training on climate-smart agriculture and financial management and that are procured by farmers using incentive payments must be included in the register of quality-assessed service providers that is maintained by The Thai Rice Project shall be eligible to receive loans through the CSL scheme.

* All data on the registry, including location information, shall be kept confidential.
* Climate-smart activities financed or part-financed by the CSL scheme shall include a sufficiently large, randomly selected sample of farmers who have received incentive payments to ensure that the results are reliable at the 95% confidence level.

### ThaCi Grants

- Grants issued by ONEP to ThaCi climate-smart rice projects shall comply with general ThaCi rules and modalities (including auditing arrangements), as laid out by legislation and ONEP regulations[1][2], and specific requirements of the project.
- GCF-funded grants shall be used to support projects that are directly relevant to, and supportive of, climate-smart agriculture in rice production. The list of eligible activities for ThaCi projects has been approved by the Environment Fund Committee.
- Funding will be provided to projects that involve marginalised stakeholders who are underserved by existing technical and financial support instruments: for example, women farmers, women workers, disabled people, ethnic minorities, and indigenous peoples (including those living in remote areas).
- ThaCi agricultural projects financed or part-financed by GCF resources shall be conducted on land that is classified as rice farmland.

### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan

#### G.2.1 Gender Assessment

**Background[3][4]**

- Thailand ranks 79 of 191 countries in the Gender Inequality Index (GII). In comparison, Malaysia is ranked 57 with a score of 0.68 compared to Thailand's score of 0.75.
- Women are underrepresented in all levels of government and have fewer female members of parliament (Thailand: 14%, Vietnam: 27%). Of the 75 provincial governors in Thailand, only four are women.
- The GII measures gender inequality across three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment, and economic security. Key challenges in relation to gender equality and the empowerment of women in Thailand include:
    - A general lack of access to education for girls and women.
    - Women's lower levels of participation in decision-making processes at all levels (national, local) against; and vulnerabilities of rural women and the low participation of women in decision-making positions.[5][6]

- There is a clear gender division of labour in agriculture. Men do so-called physically strenuous work, which includes ploughing, planting, and harvesting crops. Women are responsible for all other agricultural activities such as weeding, pest control, and post-harvest processing. Women's work is paid by workload, and the types of work that men do are often paid more per hour of work.
- Women's work, such as subsistence farming, is generally done in the morning or late afternoon for 4 to 5 hours per day and is low-paid. This


---
**Page 189**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 186

## Gender division of labour is partly based on the perception that women are better at "feminine" tasks (e.g., family employment in agriculture). In Thailand, as a share of total female employment declined from 51% in 2011 to 29% in 2018. The majority of women (53%) of female workers in the agricultural sector are essentially unpaid family workers.

## Women and men in rural areas generally share a sense of gender equality in their contributions to agriculture. This stems from the fact that both genders have equal rights over land, and equal rights to inheritance are guaranteed by the Civil and Commercial Code, although these laws are not always enforced. In addition, women and men enjoy the same rights to open bank accounts, obtain credit, and access formal financial services.

## Women farmers often operate on a small scale and typically produce only enough for backyard gardens. Interviews conducted during project implementation in Thailand indicate that women have a greater interest than men in growing vegetables and other crops that are not as profitable but provide food for the family. Women would be able to increase the production of bio-fertilizers.

## Single parents and older women in skipped-generation households are particularly vulnerable groups.

households; i.e., elderly women or men looking after their grandchildren. There are now over 400,000 single- generational households, accounting for 2% of total households, and the majority are female-headed.

* The Name Act was amended to allow women the right to choose a family name (2005).
* Amendments to the Civil Code to provide women and men equal grounds for divorce (2007).
* Rape was criminalised by the Penal Code of 1935, and the subsequent Criminal Code Amendment Act of 2007.
* The Labour Protection Act of 2008 and the Penal Code foresee legal protection in the workplace and the Labour Inspection Act of 2006 to prevent sexual abuse, harassment or mistreatment by superiors to cover all workers, both female and male.
* Domestic Violence Victim Protection Act B.E. 2550 (2017).
* Amendment to the Thai Penal Code to decriminalise the termination of pregnancy during the first 12 weeks (2021).

### Gender Equality Act was introduced in 2015

This is the law that explicitly promotes gender equality, which is one of Thailand’s 4 Pillars. The Strategy for Women’s Development in Thailand’s principal mechanism for promoting women’s development: it aligns with SDG 5 (Gender Equality) and other Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). It is also aligned with the National Vision and Social Development Plan. The Strategy has the vision of “fostering social equality without discrimination, for women to be able to participate in all aspects of society as equal partners.” The Office of Women’s Development, under the Ministry of Social Development and Human Security, has put in place a 3-year Gender Action Plan (GAP) to implement the Strategy. The GAP is divided into three main pillars: (1) strengthening gender equality; (2) strengthening gender empowerment; (3) promoting all organizations to have policies on gender equality. The Ministry of Social Development and Human Security, together with other relevant ministries at the ministry level are led by the Chief Gender Equality Officer (GEO) and the Gender Focal Point (GFP). The GEO is responsible for coordinating and supervising the implementation of the Strategy and the implementation of this law is not widely practiced.

### Thailand’s Voluntary National Review on the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development reports that women have made significant progress in many areas over recent decades. The country has achieved gender parity in basic education, with women doing slightly better in higher education and the proportion of women in political leadership positions has increased significantly since 1995. However, there are still significant conditions for women’s entrepreneurship in Asia and the Pacific. The Review also recognises the all-low representation of women in decision-making roles at national level, as well as in local government and in local administration.

### Thailand is developing a gender-disaggregated database a next step in its effort to achieve gender equality. As of 2021, only 46% of indicators needed to monitor the SDGs from a gender perspective were available. The country has made progress on gender parity in education and employment in many areas— including gender and poverty, physical and sexual harassment women’s access to assets, resources and services, and women’s representation in decision-making positions.

CEDAW’s 2017 Concluding Observations on the State of Gender Equality in Thailand. The Committee noted that despite progress made by the country in some areas, there are still significant challenges for women, including indigenous women and women belonging to ethnic groups, who remain disproportionately affected by poverty, violence, discrimination and lack of access to education and health services. Women in particular, are excluded from participation in the elaboration and implementation of policies and action plans on climate change and disaster risk reduction, despite the fact they are disproportionately affected by their impacts.

### Institutions

The Department of Women’s Affairs & Family Development of the Ministry of Social Development and Human Security is the national institution responsible for promoting gender equality and the empowerment of women.


---
**Page 192**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.10.0 | PAGE 189

**G**

women and social inclusion. It has the mandate to formulate policies and measures to enhance social security for women as well as setting up mechanisms for promoting coordination among government agencies and non-government organizations. The Department serves as the secretariat of the National Inter-Ministerial Committee on Gender and Strategic Plan for Gender Equality in Thailand. The position of Chief of Gender Equality Officer and Gender Focal Point is in place at 131 government departments/ministries, including the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MoAC). The gender focal points play a major role in gender mainstreaming in their respective agencies. They also serve as members of the National Human Rights Commission and the Law Reform Commission of Thailand.

### Key Findings

The Working Group for the implementation of SDG 5 (gender equality) supports the implementation of the Goal. Representatives from government agencies and civil society, including the Thailand Sustainable Agriculture Network (T-SAN), the Thai Women’s Development Institute (TWDI), the Foundation for Women, Homeland Talent, the Foundation for Labour and Employment Promotion, and Thailand Women’s Empowerment Network (T-WEN) are members of this Working Group. The group is open to exchange information, as well as discuss future direction in the implementation of SDG. Other important national organizations that have been involved with gender equality and women's empowerment include the Health and Opportunity Network (HoN), in Pattaya; Mu5 (HuWAIHUA); LGBTI, the Gender Development Institute (GDI), the Thai Women’s Empowerment Network (TWN), the Thailand (Bangkok), the Teeratarn Klang-akorn Foundation, the Profits Foundation and others.

### Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives

The Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MoAC) is responsible for food security in Thailand. Its 20-year Agricultural Development Plan (ADP) aims to increase agricultural productivity by promoting gender equality and women's empowerment in public-private partnerships as an objective under Strategy 2: Creating a conducive environment for the development of agriculture. The Gender Equality Unit of MoAC’s Department of Agricultural Extension (DeAE, 2023-2027) contains a specific target to promote and develop agricultural extension services that are gender-sensitive and inclusive. This is in line with the promotion of food supply and promote learning and agricultural value addition. There have also been considerable advances in increasing the number of women DeAE extension officers – to the extent that, in some districts, all the extension workers are women.

### Gender Assessment: Key Findings

Detailed analysis is provided in the Gender Assessment (Annex 8a). Key findings of the Gender Assessment include:

- Thailand is a matrilineal society, and women have a certain level of resources and power within the household. However, gender discrimination and inequality persist, particularly in relation to decision-making.
- The primary role of women in agriculture is as producers, but their access to land and other productive positions in an agricultural content means that their views on governance, resource allocation and investment are often not heard or considered.
- Women are heavily engaged in agriculture and there is a strong prevailing gender division of labour in the sector. However, women’s participation in decision-making processes at all levels of production and work, their workloads tend to be heavy.
- Women farmers have access to modern agricultural technology and are generally predisposed to technology adoption. However, they are sensitive about technology costs, as they also tend to be the financial manager of the household and responsible for family welfare and children’s education, which makes it difficult for them to draw the attention of women farmers.
- Women farmers are a key group in agriculture, and this aligns alongside their income-generating activities; provides them with a sense of livelihood security. However, their activities are often perceived to be small-scale and informal, and they lack access to credit facilities and other financial services.
- Women farmers are more interested in environmental and health issues, which serves as a good basis for building their capacity and skills.
- Thai women have good education and have relatively good basic ICT skills compared to men; they are active in providing information on agricultural extension. However, they need training given appropriate recognition and support. Women farmers’ attendance at training is generally high.

## Women farmers and women's groups are supported as service providers—initially for hybrid production (in addition to currently-used cow dung) that can be used in conjunction with traditional manure substrates:

- **Insurance scheme awareness raising and insurance literacy training** are provided to women's groups and individual women farmers.
- A feedback mechanism is established to support the insurance scheme, including for business-based or non-insured as inputs.

## Women's groups are able to list their premium due dates on a calendar and receive reminders through marketing and packaging support rather than through direct mail. The project will also provide training to women's groups in order to support the establishment of loan schemes that can be used to support access to additional inputs, including those based on standards such as the TAS, GRI, and standard fuel (gadulka) labels.

## The TAG women's empowerment indicators are reviewed annually. The project is committed to working with ACFIS to further enhance the TAS in terms of gender equality and other aspects but also with regard to other issues such as climate-smart rice farming practices, and standard fuel (gadulka) labels, including the standard fuel product labels, including the standard fuel product labels.

G3. Disbursement and procurement arrangements

Financial Management

The financial management of the Thai Rice Project will follow GIZ’s internal rules and regulations. GIZ has bank accounts with Deutsche Bundesbank and Commerzbank. GIZ will not open a specific bank account for GCF proceeds and other CCF funds but will ensure that all funds provided are clearly identifiable and distinguishable.

G3.1 Financial management and procurement

The Executive Entities – BAC, IRRJ, ONEP and RD – will sign subsidiary agreements with GIZ as the Accredited Entity, based on GIZ standard operating procedures for financing contracts. These agreements will establish the legal framework for disbursement of funds to the project partners and the implementation of activities under the Thai Rice Project. The Executive Entities are responsible for implementing and administering their activities in accordance with the terms of these agreements. GIZ will monitor the performance of the Executive Entities, assess the capacities of the Executive Entities and is satisfied that they are able to implement their respective tasks within the agreed time frame (Annex 20).

G3.2 Disbursement arrangements

The project will establish a disbursement system for the Thai Rice Project. The disbursement system will be based on GIZ’s standard operating procedures for financing contracts. The disbursement system will ensure that funds are disbursed in accordance with the terms of the subsidiary agreements and the approved budget.

G3.3 Procurement arrangements

The procurement process for the Thai Rice Project will follow GIZ’s internal rules and regulations. GIZ has bank accounts with Deutsche Bundesbank and Commerzbank. GIZ will not open a specific bank account for GCF proceeds and other CCF funds but will ensure that all funds provided are clearly identifiable and distinguishable.

The project will establish a procurement system for the Thai Rice Project. The procurement system will be based on GIZ’s standard operating procedures for financing contracts. The procurement system will ensure that procurements are conducted in accordance with the terms of the subsidiary agreements and the approved budget.

From the GCF, funds by setting up separate cost units exclusively for the funds disbursed by the GCF for each fund activity (design accounts). Funds received and expenditures incurred will be booked to the respective cost unit and accounted for in accordance with the German Accounting Standards (HGB) and the German Tax Code. As a general principle, GZ disburses funds to recipients in accordance with the progress of the project. The External Executing Entities have to report on the proper use of funds and defined progress as a prerequisite for any further disbursement.

### Procurement

457. In the case of procurements by GIZ, GIZ will follow its own procurement guidelines. GIZ is required to comply with the German Public Procurement Act (Bundesverwaltungsordnung - BVO) and the German Competition Act (Wettbewerbsrecht). The German Regulation on the Award of Public Contracts (VwG) and, if applicable, the Contracting Rules for the European Union (Richtlinien für Vertragsabschluss der Europäischen Gemeinschaften - RAG) have to be observed.

458. When awarding contracts for supplies and services (including consultancy services) to be financed in full or in part from the financing contract / grant agreement, the external Executing Entities will have to comply with the procurement procedures and minimum standards that recipients of GIZ financing must adhere to.

459. The Procurement Guidelines shall not contradict the applicable national procurement law and/or regulations for public procurements. In addition, the following principles are to be observed: the procurement procedures mentioned in the Guidelines are obligatory minimum requirements; the procurement procedures set out by the procuring entities should take reasonable account of economic efficiency as well as ecological and social aspects.

460. The Thai Rice Project's procurement plan is provided in Annex 1a.

### Independent external auditing

461. Independent external auditors will perform annual financial audits of the Thai Rice Project in line with International Auditing Standards (IAS) responsible for selecting and engaging the external auditors.

#### G.A. Disclosure of funding proposal

- ☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed by the GCF; no information is being provided in confidence.
- ☐ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed to third parties. In this case, the accredited entity must provide to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
  - A redacted copy for internal use by the GCF with an explanatory note regarding the redacted parts and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accreditation agreement.
  - A redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website. This funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP214

## Proposal name: Thai Rice: Strengthening Climate-Smart Rice Farming

### Accredited entity:
Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH

### Country:
Thailand

### Project/programme size:
Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| The project promotes a strategic approach to transforming the rice-based food systems of Thailand into climate-smart systems. | It will be important to monitor the progress of the design of the T-VER Rice Scheme and seek opportunities for scaling up after project closure is scaled up. |
| The project offers the potential to significantly reduce methane emissions from rice production without jeopardizing the rice yield and quality, thereby transforming all of Thailand’s rice production to a low-emission pathway. |  |
| The project aims at promoting behavioural change in smallholder farmers through a targeted incentive scheme for the adoption and actual uptake of climate-smart technologies, including laser land levelling (LLL). |  |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions outlined on the term sheet and addendum XXI titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations.”

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background


---
**Page 208**
---

1. Rice is Thailand’s most important food crop, accounting for roughly half of the country’s agricultural land and employing 18 million smallholder farmers. However, rice farming is also a significant contributor to the country's greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, mainly through methane from paddy fields.
2. Smallholder rice farmers in Thailand are vulnerable to climate change due to changing rainfall patterns, as well as increasing frequency and severity of flooding and droughts.
3. The project will focus on promoting adaptation and mitigation results from the rice farming sector in Thailand. If approved, it will be the first GEF project to focus solely on transforming the rice sector into a more sustainable one by providing smallholder farmers with access to a suite of climate-smart technologies and practices, as well as farmer-friendly financial services.
4. The project is structured around three components: (a) investment in climate-smart technologies and practices; (b) strengthening of service providers for the adoption of climate-smart technologies for rice farmers by service provider sessions; and (c) strengthening of the institutional framework to support the implementation of the project.
5. The proposed project is expected to reduce a total of 12,56 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO₂ eq) during the project lifetime of 15 years. This result will be achieved by promoting climate-smart technologies and practices in rice farming such as water management practices with alternative wetting and drying (AWD), site-specific nutrient management (SSNM), and integrated pest management (IPM). The project will also provide training to service provider staff, which will contribute to directly benefiting 253.400 smallholder rice farmers, and an additional 760.200 people through the multiplier effect.
6. The total project financing is EUR 180 million of which a request is being made for GEF grants of EUR 38.17 million. The co-financing of EUR 79.90 million has also been secured to support the project, including contributions from the private sector in the form of equity, loans, and in-kind financing.
7. The project is submitted under environmental and social safeguards. The implementation period of the project is five years.

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Outcome 1: Climate-smart farmers' income and food security are increased despite adverse climate change effects
- Output 2.1: Smallholder rice farmers will be provided with access to a suite of climate-smart technologies and practices.
- To do so, it will provide trainings for farmers on climate-smart technologies such as AWD, SSNM, IPM, and LLL. It will also promote the adoption of these technologies by smallholder farmers through high water intensity to low water managed systems, integrated pest management (IPM), and straw recycling with AWD and other techniques to better control water levels in the rice fields, while lowering the risk of flooding.
- The project will provide financial literacy trainings for climate-smart farming technologies, through providing financial literacy trainings. It will also develop an app for climate-smart rice farming that will help farmers adopt climate-smart technologies including AWD and LLL.

10. The merit of this outcome lies in the fact that the latter technologies will be transferred to farmers by means of a mobile app for climate-smart rice farming.
11. As noted in the funding proposal, the level of mobile phone penetration in Thailand is 137 per cent, implying that the majority of smallholder farmers have access to mobile phones and can use them to meet their needs. Once developed, the app will need to be updated on a regular basis to reflect


---
**Page 209**
---

The needs and situation of farmers on the ground, and it will be critical to secure adequate resources to operate and maintain them.

### Enhancing farmers' access to finance is another strong merit of this project.
As the proposed climate-smart technologies are at a nascent stage in the country, and farmers’ profits are extremely low, the need for financial support is high. The incentive payments of GCF funding through a grant award mechanism is essential to boost the uptake of climate-smart technologies. Farmers who have been identified as potential beneficiaries will be designed to incentivize farmers to meaningfully engage in the project activities. Only those farmers’ whose landholdings are less than 10 rai and who have completed basic agricultural literacy trainings to be carried out earlier in the project will be eligible for the incentive payment. This will motivate farmers to undertake the training.

### The proposal is financially feasible.
The proposal is based on contributions from the Rice Department (RD) of the Thai Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives, which has a history of successful projects. The RD’s financial analysis shows positive returns in the case of the incentive payments (subactivity 1.1.2.2). The proposal presents sufficient justification for the request of full grants, mainly due to the farmers’ low risk tolerance and the need for additional support.

### Outcome 2: The climate resilience of rice farming is enhanced and GHG emissions are substantially reduced through the adoption of climate-smart technologies (total costs: EUR 33.2 million; GCF cost: USD 17.6 million).
The outcome will foster access to finance schemes to invest in technology transfer and access to innovation to support the transition of rice production. Building on the capacity established under the previous Thai Rice NAMA (nationally appropriate mitigation action) Support Project (NSP), the proposal seeks to provide additional financial resources for service providers to invest in innovation and technology that will encourage production to move towards a low-emission pathway and significantly reduce GHG emissions.

### Outcome 3: Climate-smart technologies are adopted by farmers through services provided to rice farmers, including on-machine services, insurance and agent services. These enhanced service provisions will be provided by the Rice Department (RD) of the Thai Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives and provide climate smart land use (CSLs) by BAAC to credibly encourage farmers to offset the reduced farm cashflows in the first year of climate-smart farming, with favourable pricing compared to the market. This will ensure that farmers are not financially disadvantaged.

The proposal provides a clear and robust rationale for the need for technical capacity-building and service provision. The proposed approach is based on the assumption that the app under subactivity 1.1.3.2 will facilitate a positive role under outcome 2, with a mutual rating function to ensure a high standard of service from the service providers. The project will need to ensure that the app is available for as many farmers as possible and that the services are provided to as many farmers as possible as quickly as possible.

### Eligibility criteria.
The eligibility criteria of the eligible users of BACs, indicating which technologies and practices are eligible or not for accessing CSLs. The eligibility criteria are clear and aligned with the GCF investment criteria. The eligibility criteria for farmers have also been established in accordance with the guidelines provided by the Thai government. The terms and conditions of CSLs are deemed to be highly concessional as BAAC proceeds with debt


---
**Page 210**
---

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### Impact potential: Scale: High

The project has a high impact potential due to the innovative architecture of the three outcomes: household diversification for the most vulnerable rice farmers in the target regions reaching 253,460 direct beneficiaries and 760,200 indirect beneficiaries. In addition, the project will reduce GHG emissions by 189,000 tCO₂e/year (project implementation) and 2.4 M tCO₂e over the project lifetime. The project prevents rice straw from being burned in the field, which would otherwise release approximately 3.6 M tCO₂e/year. This is equivalent to planting 15 million trees or removing 700,000 cars from roads for a year.

The proposed outcomes and the focus on technology transfer, piloting and scaling up key technologies will ensure that farmers in the target regions will be able not only to save water but also to reduce methane emissions while maintaining high rice yields, and to manage fields in a more sustainable manner through IPM methods. Finally, rice straw from the project areas can be used as feedstock for bioenergy production or otherwise be burned after each rice harvest thus reducing CO₂ emissions and providing communities with an opportunity to exploit this economic potential by exploring rice straw into new products.

The project will further support and build capacity for farmers to gain access to TAS certification, which is one of their most key market and partnership with the private sector and end-takers. The project will further work with the TAS system to align with the Sustainable Rice Platform (SRP) standards and criteria on sustainable production practices in its framework, thereby not only incentivizing farmers to produce higher quality rice for premium markets but also ensuring that they are able to access these markets.

The project offers a unique opportunity to scale up the best practices from the Thai Rice Facility to rice-producing areas countrywide, transitioning Thailand's rice food systems to a

**Page 211**
---

3.2 Paradigm-shift potential Scale: High

The paradigm shift potential is high due to the innovative and long-term benefits associated with transitioning smallholder farmers with the technology, practices, and access to finance for transition their local food systems and secure a long-term access to finance, innovation and technology through unique partnerships between the project team, the NAMA Facility, and other strategic partners as well as the private sector. all managed and scaled up through the established Thai Rice Facility. The project further seeks to solve the challenges with rice straw burning, a significant source of greenhouse gas emissions in Thailand, by promoting alternative uses for rice straw while creating new markets and demand for such products.

The project builds on the success of the previous project, the NRP funded by the NAMA Facility, which ran from 2018 to 2023. A project that helped farmers increase rice production, private sector collaboration in the agriculture sector and policy support. The new proposed project will build upon this success and scale up its impact through a five-year implementation by anchoring them within the project's three outcomes.

The number of well-tested and pilot technologies that the project will scale up and promote include: improved rice varieties, integrated pest management (IPM), sustainable land management and PPM. Farmers will have better and longer-term access to these technologies to help them increase their productivity while reducing greenhouse gas emissions such as reducing water consumption, methane and nitrous oxide emissions, and reduced agricultural use through the adoption of more efficient practices.

The project will also provide training for farmers on how to use these technologies certified both through training and access to finance through the IRACAN scheme serving advice service providers and farmers to invest and deploy these technologies to help transition their rice production.

Burning of rice straw after each harvest is standard procedure but contributes to large amount GHG emissions. ## Sustainable development potential

The prospective sustainable development potential is considered high. It will foster innovation and progress across several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) such as SDG 17, SDG 13, SDG 12, SDG 6, SDG 5, SDG 2 and SDG 1.

The project aims to promote rice diversification, sustainable water management, and farming system practices by fostering integrated pest management solutions aiming to boost the productivity of existing farming systems while greatly boosting environmental co-benefits. The project will also address the pressing need for improving access to their rice production and its surrounding ecosystems using IPs as well as through the reduction of straw burning in the rice fields. This will contribute significantly towards enhancing food security, diversifying livelihood strategies.

Socio-economic benefits are well embedded in the project structure with the promotion of climate smart rice and new market opportunities created through the TAC, as well as the new window of the T-VER Rice Scheme and flow of future finance to further support farmers' access to credit. The project will also provide new opportunities and markets for farmers with the possibility of transforming the straw into paper, packaging or other products, thus creating new alternatives on the market and creating a circular economy.

Gender co-benefits are fully embedded within the project with a key focus on full participation of women in the transformation process, as well as focusing on strengthening women farmers' access to finance, technical capacities and for national institutions to provide gender-responsive support.


## Needs of the recipient

The needs of the recipient are considered high. The project targets the key rice-growing areas in Thailand's central region, an area which also has been vulnerable communities. Through fostering a transition to low-emission rice production, the project offers the opportunity to transform the entire rice sector of Thailand, one of the largest rice exporters worldwide.

The target beneficiaries and their productive assets are exposed to climate-induced risks several times per year in the form of flooding, droughts and heatwaves which significantly contributes to Thailand's annual GHG emissions. The project works directly to reduce the vulnerability of the target beneficiaries by boosting their access to innovation and technology, and financial services. This will also provide a strong foundation for the circular economy promoted by straw recycling and the opportunities this brings to project beneficiaries.

The project will also address the pressing need for innovative financing in the rice sector in Thailand, to change from a business-as-usual approach and transition rice production to low-emission production. The project offers an innovative approach to addressing these needs through the development of a financial innovation fund for the rice sector in Thailand is challenged by a lack of financial innovation and access for farmers to financial institutions. The project will directly deliver solutions to change this scenario and


---
**Page 213**
---

3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

The country ownership is considered high, rice production and rice food systems are a key priority for Thailand and play significant roles in its national economy. The project is fully aligned with the government’s long-term climate change adaptation plan, Long-term Low Greenhouse Gas Emission Development Strategy, national climate policy framework such as the Thai National Agriculture and Cooperative Climate change strategy plan for the rice sector (2022-2036), and will provide annual communication to the UNFCCC, fourth biannual update report and technology needs assessment.

The project is well aligned with past and ongoing projects focusing on rice and rice-based farming systems in Thailand from international partners, and provides a strategic framework for these initiatives linked with the national priorities and how they will be coordinated; and collaboration will take place during implementation. (a) GIZ has been working closely with the Thai government to develop a very strong, strategic and innovative mix of skills, and financial innovation. GIZ has a long-term presence in Thailand, has worked for decades with the country’s government in the rice sector and has extensive experience in implementing projects that have drawn on global, regional and national experience in food systems transition, rice-based farming systems and climate change adaptation. The project will be implemented by the Rice Department, BAIG and Office of Natural Resources and Environmental Policy and Planning (ONREP) with strong support from GIZ. The project will also benefit from a full scaling up of the best practices of the project to transition the rice sector to a sustainable future. Each of these institutions has a long history in implementing projects and are national centres of excellence, contributing with key skill sets and strong bases of innovation and knowledge.

The project will further support the Environmental Food Fund (EFFO) hosted by ONREP and strengthen its implementation capacities, building on an ongoing GIZ Readiness and Preparedness Project. The EFFO is a multi-donor trust fund that provides Thailand’s first direct access entity to provide technical and financial support to promote EFO to scale up climate-smart rice agriculture in the future.

The project will benefit from strong and well-organized stakeholder consultations led by the Rice Department since 2020 involving all stakeholder groups across the target areas and coordinated through a project working group. This national platform has fostered the key dialogues and consultation that resulted in the project architecture.


3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium-High

The project’s efficiency and effectiveness are considered medium-high, due to its innovative approach transforming the rice food systems to sustainable, climate-smart, and methane-reducing systems and the structure of the national rice scheme under the T-VER Scheme.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

##### Project background
The project aims to systematically shift smallholder rice agriculture in Thailand onto a low-emission, climate-resilient development trajectory by promoting the adoption of best practices from across the project area. The project will support rice farmers, upscaling and extending relevant services including financial instruments, and strengthening the institutional and market environment for climate-smart agriculture in the project areas through TAS certifications and off-takers, the T-VER Rice Scheme and the circular economy boosted by straw recycling and upcycling establishing new products and markets for rice straw.


---
**Page 215**
---

Thailand: The project will build capacity of smallholder farmers and service providers, respectively on the application and supply of climate-smart technologies and practices in rice farming. The project will support the development of a number of financial products and services, including micro-insurance to help smallholder farmers manage some or all of the costs of switching to climate-smart agriculture; piloting an index-based insurance based on the existing agricultural insurance scheme; providing access to credit for rice farmers at low cost of crop damage or can lower the damage following climate-smart measures; and developing a new climate-smart loan to creditworthy farmers switching to climate-smart agriculture. Among the environmental, social, economic, and financial risks associated with project activities are: improved air quality from less straw burning; and improvements in water quality and availability through reduced soil erosion and better land management practices. The project is also expected to contribute to the empowerment of the women and youth in the target areas.

### Environmental and social risk category: The AE assigned the project moderate E&S risk category (Level 2) based on the assessment of potential environmental, social, economic, and financial risks associated with the proposed E&S risk category considering the small-scale nature of the interventions that will be carried out. The project is expected to have a low level of impact on the environment due to the technologies and practices is expected to have potential minimal to moderate environmental and socio-economic impacts. These impacts are expected to be mitigated through careful design and the application of generally accepted mitigation measures and standards. The AE also confirms that the assigned risk category is within the accreditation level of the AE.

### Safeguards instrument: The AE provided an environmental and social assessment, environment and social impact assessments (ESIAs) and a safeguards management framework (SMF) with the funding proposal as the safeguards document. The document provides an overview of the project’s potential environmental and social risks, the potential mitigation measures for these risks, and the range of climate-smart technologies and practices targeted by the project. As the project will involve smallholder farmers in the North-Eastern and Central regions of Thailand, environment and social impact assessments and manage plans will be developed during the project’s implementation.

### Compliance with the GCF E&S standards: The paragraphs below provide a brief description of the programme's compliance with E&S standards:

#### ES 1: Environmental and Social Baseline: The ESF discussed the environmental and social baseline of North, North-East, and Central regions targeted by the project and assessed the general risks and impacts against the policies and strategies of the Thai government. The ESF also identified the anticipated positive and negative direct, indirect, and cumulative impacts from the project implementation on the environment and society in the target areas. The ESF also identified for the AE’s safeguards standards, as well as indicate mitigation measures related to the identified risks. The ESF also provided a summary of the environmental and social baseline information that will be used by the project team during the due diligence process to be undertaken for activities to be financed supported by the project including the identification of potential impacts on the environment and society through the Thai Climate Initiative (ThaiClim). The costs of implementing the ESF have been estimated and integrated into the project’s budget. Additional details on the identified risks and how they will be mitigated are provided in the ESIAs.

#### ES 2: Labour and working conditions: The AE assessed that most project interventions, including the deployment of the proposed technologies and practices, are expected to have minimal impact on labour and working conditions. However, the needs and interests of female smallholder farmers are not yet addressed systematically in Thailand’s rice


---
**Page 216**
---

sector labor shortage in rice farming, and forced child labour are pressing issues to be addressed in the project’s E&S risk management plan (ESMP). availability or delay of such services, the AII is recommended to monitor the potential technological changes and rate of penetration to avoid potential conflict and alleviate water stress.

### ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The project does not include any activities that will require land acquisition or resettlement. However, project activities could affect the land-use rights with regards to the farm-level water management which may involve conflicts between farmers and other stakeholders. Any such potential impacts would take place only when land ownership, possession and land use rights are clear. The location of each plot where these activities will be implemented as well as any new practices adopted, will be logged, and that only when the land is officially categorised as rice farming land by the government will be considered for project implementation. Prior to project commences, the owner of each plot will be identified through a land tenure verification information and that the landowner’s prior agreement will be obtained. Where a plot is rented by the landowner, the tenant’s consent must also be obtained in order to ensure that access can be obtained.

### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The project is noting the challenge of obtaining data on biodiversity in this rice field in general and the lack of detailed information on the effects of the technologies planned to be promoted. The project will not make any assumptions about the potential impacts on biodiversity, which did not raise any concern related to natural habitat, ecosystem or biodiversity in relation to the adoption of climate-smart agriculture (CSA). However, it is important to note that the adoption of climate-smart agriculture (with accompanying reductions in agro-chemical use) may lead to changes in the composition and structure of the local flora and fauna. Nonetheless, a precautionary approach should be exercised in implementing the project activities to ensure that the potential negative impacts such as reduced biodiversity or change in chemical composition of soil are not caused by the project unless adequately addressed. The AII will carry out a multi-taxa biodiversity study to monitor the impacts of project activities in rice fields and other areas where climate-smart agricultural technologies will be promoted. It will undertake appropriate actions in response to the results of the study where needed. In addition, a biodiversity impact assessment (BIA) should be conducted for each site where the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices. Specific to the straw and stubble management (SSM) technology, the AII will conduct a pilot study on the use of SSM in rice fields with a representative sample of farmers practicing SSM to ensure that their diversification of biomass residues for other purposes does not lead to any negative impacts such as reduced biodiversity or increased agro-chemical fertilizers. If this is found to be a problem, the project will take adaptive measures and put into place necessary training.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy (GIPP) and SDG 5 on Indigenous Peoples

The Project adopts no-harm measures to manage the risks involved for indigenous individuals that will be part of the project, and has proposed a two-stage approach to engage with the relevant indigenous communities that are affected by the Ethical Group. In the development of the Ethical Group Plans, consultations were undertaken with villages that self-identify as indigenous. Culturally appropriate methods were used in order to ensure that all voices are heard. Activities adhere to the key principles of the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy. The AII foresees to undertake a baseline study on the status and condition of indigenous peoples and the rights of the peoples involved in the project by engaging the traditional indigenous authorities of these communities. This will be done through consultations with the relevant indigenous groups, using traditional knowledge and livelihood systems and does not involve any changes in the access of indigenous peoples to their lands or resources nor any changes in the recognition of indigenous rights standards reference to indigenous peoples. In line with its roles and functions, the GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AII who is also encouraged to share emerging good practices and success stories with the Group.

### GCF.07.37/2/A/Add.05 Page 119

#### 6.3 Cultural heritage
The project intends to contribute to valuing the nations and traditions associated with rice cultivation, while noting that the ongoing societal change, modernization processes, including the uptake of new technologies and practices, may challenge such traditional values and practices. The project will ensure that its activities do not infringe on protected cultural heritage sites, nor develop natural resources on land subject to traditional use or customary rights. This includes sacred places with spiritual, social, and/or spiritual value, such as sacred groves, sacred bodies of water and watersheds, sacred mountains, sacred trees, sacred rocks, burial grounds and ancestral graves.

#### 6.4 Sustainable livelihoods and environmental harm (SEAH) safeguarding
The ESF identified that the project poses low SEAH risks in the context of project-supported training and capacity building activities for various target groups, including service providers targeted by the project as part of the project's financial support. To avoid and prevent the project from exacerbating SEAH risks, the AE will implement a zero-tolerance policy on all forms of harassment, discrimination, and other forms of abuse, exploitation, and grievances. The project will provide SEAF training to all beneficiaries of the project's training programme, including service providers targeted by the project as part of its financial support.

#### 6.5 Implementation arrangements
GIZ, at the AE, will oversee and monitor the management and implementation of the ESF. An independent management and monitoring system is to be developed during the project inception. Each of the three executing entities will be responsible for the implementation of the ESF's safeguard (ESAF) measures. The ESF will provide technical assistance in the development of a mechanism for coordinating with other supporting organizations in the implementation of ESF. The AE will recall its respective focal points to ensure that the project is implemented as planned, and as the implementation of the SEAF and ESF. AE will nominate their respective ESF focal points to ensure that the project is implemented according to the approved work plan and budget of the project. The ESF manager and the ESS focal points nominated by the EEs will form the ES management team.

#### 6.6 Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure
The AE provided a Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) for the project, which identified key stakeholders and mapped out their roles and responsibilities, female farmers, slipped generation households, ethnic groups, etc., whose specific needs in respect of the project were taken into consideration and informed the design of the project. The SEP described the Stakeholder engagement methods, timeframes, and the responsible entities for the project implementation process. The AE will ensure that the project is implemented with the full participation and relevant stakeholders.

#### 6.7 Grievance mechanism
The project will establish a project-level GRM and a separate GRM for SEAH-related grievances, which are elaborated in the ESF and the SEP. The project-level GRM will handle complaints both directly and indirectly related to the project implementation process. The AE will ensure that the project has an appropriate set of procedures to ensure higher levels of sensitivity. In line with their roles and functions, the Indigenous Peoples' focal points will be responsible for assisting the project's indigenous executing entities. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF Indigenous peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

### Gender Policy

#### 4.2 Gender Policy

**47.** The AIF provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and thereafter complied with the requirements of the SGR Gender Policy. The Thai Rice Strengthening and Climate Smart Rice Farming project provides an opportunity to mainstream gender equality and women’s empowerment and also achieve stand-alone gender outputs and outcomes in the project. This document is intended as a guide for the implementation of gender equality in the targeted country. It highlights how climate-related vulnerabilities are closely linked with gender inequalities, which can be addressed through the promotion of gender equality in the targeted country.

**48.** The AIF recognizes that women play prominent roles in off-farm and non-income-generating activities such as childcare, cooking, cleaning, and other unpaid work within their household work, hence there is less recognition of women’s work/activities. There is a need to recognize these contributions by women and provide them with the necessary support for this project, including activities related with bio-fertilizer production. In the agriculture sector, the project could benefit from the involvement of women in decision-making processes as they are unpaid, and the home-based care work that women traditionally engage in also makes it difficult for most women to fully engage in other productive areas; women and men have different roles and responsibilities within their households.

**49.** The AIF recognizes that there is a need to provide training on gender mainstreaming for the project to identify these differences when introducing climate-smart technologies and practices, as well as to ensure that the project’s activities are gender-sensitive. This includes the need for the project to enhance women's leadership in the sector; and introduce climate-smart agriculture technologies and practices that are appropriate for women farmers. The AIF also recognizes the need to build capacity of the project team on gender expertise within the partner organizations, including IRRI, BMAC, and ONEP. The project will facilitate a series of training sessions at various levels (national, provincial, district) with regards to gender expertise in order to enhance the gender outcomes earmarked for this project.

**50.** The gender action plan outlines several activities that will contribute towards the achievement of gender and women’s empowerment outcomes under this project. Among other interventions, these include: (1) Develop a monitoring and evaluation system that will track the number of beneficiaries and help to ensure gender-responsive project management – the project will target 253,640 direct beneficiaries (138,400 male and 115,240 female); (2) Key personnel of the Project Management Unit and relevant ES staff will be trained in gender mainstreaming; (3) Develop a series of training sessions on shared/division of labour at household level as it relates to agricultural activities, engage source persons from the community to provide information on shared division of labour; (4) Conduct trainings on climate-smart rice agriculture, labour laws, agricultural information technologies, women’s economic empowerment and other related topics in various communities; (5) Organize training sessions in secondary schools on climate-smart agricultural technologies and practices; (6) Develop financial literacy training and financial instruments (incentive payment and climate-smart financing) to provide access to credit and other financial services from formal and informal sources (In addition to currently used cow dung that can be used in conjunction with rice residues); (7) Provide information on insurance schemes and insurance literacy training for women farmers and women groups; (8) Ensure there is access to land, water resources, and inputs for women farmers as a means of increasing women’s empowerment as a foundational element, and many other activities aimed at gender mainstreaming in the rice sector.

# 4.3

## Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

70. GCF is requested to provide a grant of EUR8 million to enable Thai rice farmers to adapt to climate change while reducing GHG emissions in Thailand. The BMZ via AE is co-financing the project with a loan of EUR125 million, and the Thai government will make an in-kind contribution EUR37.6 million. Additional co-financers including private sector are expected to contribute EUR40-50 million. This would either be grant-in-kind or loan. IMAC is also financing a demand-based loan of EUR 30 million.

71. Thailand has an investment grade credit rating and this credit profile will support available funding from the World Bank. The Thai government has granted a grant-in-kind (GKI) based on the public-good nature of the activities and agriculture, especially rice farming forming part of the national food security strategy.

### Accredited entity/executing entity's capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

72. GIZ is in AE and co-EE of the project. The AE has been operating in Thailand since 1956. The AE has a total budget for this project of EUR30 million, with an operational budget of EUR170 million of which EUR 115 million accounted for climate change projects from 2008.

73. There are four additional co-EEs, namely Rice Department (RD) of the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MAC), National Agricultural and Forestry Research Organization (NAFRO), Bureau of Agricultural Administration and Cooperative (BAAC), International Rice Research Institute (IRRI) and Office of Natural Resources and Environment (ONE) of the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MoNRE). The AE will be responsible for the overall coordination of working with different international partners and implementing similar types of projects.

### Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

74. Impact risk - co-financing: the impact potential arising from that IMAC will provide the loan of EUR 30 million to Thai farmers, which may not be fully materialized depending on the status of the borrowers' own capital, availability of alternative resources, and other market conditions. In case of a reduction in the amount of co-financing provided by IMAC, the impact will be subsequently reduced. To partially mitigate this risk, the technical assistance to MoNRE will be provided free of charge for the duration of the project implementation period. Also, the loan terms and conditions will be flexible to be tailored to the market conditions.

75. Misuse of proceeds: GCF grant is targeting more vulnerable groups who are not eligible for loans from commercial banks or other financial institutions. The AE suggests that several measures be taken place: the payment will be subject to the commitment and performance of the farmers, and the disbursement will be made in several tranches to ensure more control over the utilization of funds by the farmers. The AE has also suggested that the Thai government should support projects in this regard. More detailed procedures will be finalized in the agreement between GIZ and MAC.

76. Low demand risk: The FP states that Thai farmers have an extreme degree of risk aversion and skeptical of new technologies. With this background, there could be a low demand and participation from farmers to take up the project activities. However, the AE believes that the demand is more demanding. As such, to AE, it offers some financial benefits such as TAS certification opening up access to premium rice market with higher prices, investment planning training and greater

## Fiduciary

### 4.4 Fiduciary

GIZ, the accredited entity (AE) for the Thai Rice project will also operate as one of the five executing entities (EEs) in relation to specific project activities. As the AE, GIZ will assume oversight responsibility and accountability for the management of the local implementing agency (LIA) between GCF and GIZ. As an AE, GIZ will administer project funds on behalf of GCF and will provide oversight guidance and quality assurance for the executing entities.

### Results monitoring and reporting

As a cross-cutting initiative, the project aims to generate both mitigation and adaptation results. On the mitigation side, the intervention is expected to result in overall GHG emission reductions of approximately 10 million tonnes CO2e by 2030. The GCF will use the GCF indicator for mitigation based on the GHG accounting methodology detailed under annex Z4 of the Agreement. Mitigation results are expected from all four EEs, with direct beneficiaries as well as indirect beneficiaries acting as adaptation impacts. The AIE provided a methodology and assumptions that will be used to determine both direct and indirect benefits.

The theory of change for the program adequately captures different levels of potential changes as well as logical linkages from the goal statement down to the proposed activities. Mitigation and adaptation results are expected at three levels: farmer, service provider and institution, which will together contribute to overall project-level mitigation and adaptation impacts. The AIE identified risks that may hinder the achievement of these changes and linked it to pertinent activities which will tackle them accordingly.

## Legal assessment

### 4.6 Legal assessment

The Accreditation Master Agreement (the "AMA") was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15 November 2017 and became effective on 18 January 2019.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Kingdom of Thailand, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not entitled to immunity from legal process or jurisdiction in Thailand. The AMA provides for further discussion on this matter as it relates to the implementation of the project.

The Accredited Entity has provided a legal certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals necessary for the implementation of the proposed project.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Kingdom of Thailand, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not entitled to immunity from legal process or jurisdiction in Thailand. The AMA provides for further discussion on this matter as it relates to the implementation of the project.

The latest communication took place on 13 March 2023 whereby the GCF Secretariat sent the updated draft to the Government of the Kingdom of Thailand. Negotiations on the agreement are ongoing.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have been informed that GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities in relation to its activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by GCF are made only after the completion of a legal due diligence process to ensure that no expropriation in the country or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board be subject to the following conditions:

- Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form of substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and
- Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.
