# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP214

## Proposal name: Thai Rice: Strengthening Climate-Smart Rice Farming

### Accredited entity: Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH

### Country: Thailand

### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

#### 1.1 Overview

This is a five-year project on rice farming in Thailand, targeting 21 provinces in the three most important rice-growing regions of the country: the north, the northeast and the east (see map). The project will be implemented by the Department of Agriculture for rice farming in Thailand. As such, it is a cross-cutting, medium-term project with a dual objective:

(a) To adapt rice farming in Thailand to order to reduce the negative impacts of observed and anticipated climate change conditions; and

(b) To reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions resulting from rice farming in order to help mitigate climate change.

To achieve these objectives, the intention of this project is to intervene on three main components:

(a) Increasing the income and food security of climate-smart farmers operating under changing climatic conditions (Outcome 1);

(b) Substantially reducing GHG emissions (Outcome 2); and

(c) Directing or channeling government policies and appropriate funds towards climate-smart rice farming (Outcome 3).

With these three components, for farmers, and for soil, water and air quality, the project's three fundamental axes will be realized through:

(a) Rice farmers' adoption of, and investment in, climate-smart technologies and practices (Outcome 1);

(b) Service providers' supply of, and investment in, climate-smart technologies (Outcome 2); and

(c) Strengthening of the institutional and business environment for climate-smart rice farming.

From this perspective, the project's outcomes and outputs focus on the fundamental components that will enable the transformation towards a more sustainable and resilient productivity of Thailand’s rice farming (through climate-smart practices and technologies) and its adaptive capacity (through institutional strengthening, improved market conditions and mastery of climate change knowledge). The adoption of these technologies and practices will also enable a low-GHG emission system.


---
**Page 225**
---

1. The technical capacity building activities and the financial and institutional arrangements have been designed to provide key stakeholders (farmers and service providers) in the rice value chain in Thailand with considerable support for the process of adopting the climate-smart agriculture practices.
2. A wide range of practices and technologies (10 in total) are envisaged: laser land levelling, alternate wetting and drying, site-specific nutrient management, straw and stubble management, water-saving irrigation systems, conservation tillage, crop rotation and intercropping, direct-seeded rice, farm-level water management and agro-met advisory services. These technologies provide adaptive practices and co-benefits.
3. This project is of the utmost importance, as rice production is strategic for the country from both economic and environmental perspectives.
4. From a socioeconomic standpoint, rice production makes a considerable contribution to the country’s economy. It should continue to play a pivotal role in meeting growing world demand for food and feed grains. Rice production also provides livelihoods for small-scale farmers, including women. This represents a challenge for the sector, which has been facing increasing environmental challenges such as climate change conditions, notably more intense and frequent droughts or rainfall. In environmental terms, the emissions from rice production are significant: 24.2 Mt CO₂ eq per year, which is about one-third of total GHG emissions in Thailand.
5. The project will be executed by a consortium of five entities: Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH, the Department of Agriculture (DOA), the Rice Department (RID) of the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MAC), the Bank for Agriculture and Agricultural Cooperatives (BAAC), and the National Environment Policy and Planning (NEPP) of the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (the nationally designated authority (NDA)) and the International Rice Research Institute (IRRI).
6. The total budget for this five-year project is EUR 118.0 million, including EUR 38.17 million in co-financing from GCF. The project will be managed and governed by three main structures: a steering committee; a management unit; and implementation units divided into working groups.
### Impact potential
Scale: Medium to High
The project will help to move towards a low-emission rice farming system in Thailand. Using a wide range of climate-smart agriculture techniques and practices (CSA & TIP), such as those referenced above, the project aims to reduce GHG emissions from rice production by avoiding the emission of 24.2 Mt CO₂ eq during its five-year implementation period and 12.56 Mt CO₂ eq per year thereafter.
The project’s mitigation impact was estimated using the Source-selective and Emission-adjusted GWP Calculator for CROPLAND (SECTOR) tool, which considered the emission and sequestration of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO₂eq), methane (CH₄), nitrous oxide (N₂O) and Hydrogen Sulfide Gas Inventories. More precisely, the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) 2013 guidelines were used to estimate the global warming potential (GWP) of greenhouse gases resulting from rice production in the target regions. To convert emissions of these gases into their carbon dioxide equivalent, warming potentials of 28 and 265 were used for methane and nitrous oxide respectively.
<sup>1</sup> Paragraph 32 of the funding proposal.


---
**Page 226**
---

**nitrogen respectively. In order to take into account the specification of the different production regimes in the target regions, the farms were divided into megafarms and non-megafarms, subdivided into irrigated and rain-fed systems over two or three cropping seasons as appropriate. The project proposes a new CSA TAP that will be applied equally for megafarms and 60% for non-megafarms in each region which is equivalent to 72% for all farms**.

13. **The rate of adoption of the different CSA TAP is based on experiences of relatively short duration and very specific contexts that are likely to evolve and also assumes a linear approach regarding the rate of adoption. The project proposes an innovative CSA TAP that will ensure that the monitoring and evaluation system adequately addresses this aspect, on which the mitigation potential depends**.

14. **Moreover, given the importance of the rate of adoption of the different CSA TAP as a variable in estimating the mitigation potential, it is important to ensure that the results of the project are not only disseminated through the existing communication and dissemination system but also that the knowledge-sharing plan must take this into account and be adapted accordingly. Emphasis should be placed on the need for capacity building among farmers regarding new technologies and practices, and so this must be a crucial worksheet to ensure a high degree of confidence in the mitigation potential of projects**.

15. **Moreover, the resilience of rice farming in Thailand will also contribute to enhancing the resilience of rice farming in Thailand. The number of direct beneficiaries is estimated to be 234,000 farmers and the total number of indirect beneficiaries (including family members) is expected to be 763,200, including 406,800 women. The farmland area covered by the project is approximately 718,000 ha, of which 396,000 ha are irrigated and 422,000 ha are rain-fed**.

16. **The project proposes a new CSA TAP that will not only increase the resilience of rice farming in Thailand but also reduce its GHG emissions. The project's theory of change is clear and clearly and coherently stated. If it is applied as stated, and the project is implemented with the same level of commitment and resources as has been replicated elsewhere, provided that the contexts in which they are implemented are taken into account and receive the appropriate support, the CSA TAP will be effective**.

17. **The project will also contribute to the creation of an enabling environment by strengthening institutional capacities, and encouraging the formulation and implementation of policies, and the channelling of financial resources towards climate smart rice farming**.


## 1.3 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to High

28. The project proposes a set of innovative CSA TAP that will not only increase the resilience of rice farming in Thailand but also reduce its GHG emissions.

29. The project's theory of change is clear and clearly and coherently stated. If it is applied as stated, and the project is implemented with the same level of commitment and resources as has been replicated elsewhere, provided that the contexts in which they are implemented are taken into account and receive the appropriate support, the CSA TAP will be effective.

30. The project will also contribute to the creation of an enabling environment by strengthening institutional capacities, and encouraging the formulation and implementation of policies, and the channelling of financial resources towards climate smart rice farming.


---
**Page 227**
---

20. Furthermore, the project will ensure that the capacity of service providers are strengthened and that the financial instruments available to them are adapted so that they can offer climate-smart technologies to farmers.
21. When supporting the formulation of policies focused on climate-smart rice farming, the project could also make a significant effort towards aspects relating to valuing the work of farmers, greater incentives for young farmers and improved land tenure security, to reduce the main barriers to adoption of climate-smart practices in the target regions.
22. In order to foster a paradigm shift in the sector, the knowledge management plan of the project needs to promote, among other things, better use of process-based crop models in the identification of optimal cropping systems and land-use strategies for rice farming. Data collected as part of the project can be used to evaluate the crop model in the target regions. This evaluation will be carried out by the Department of Agriculture (in collaboration with the Rice Research Institute, for example), will be used not only to build capacity, but also to make more informed decisions on land-use strategies and cropping systems.
23. The evidence provided for the evaluation of the crop model in the target regions is not sufficient.

24. When well calibrated and validated, process-based crop models such as the one used to simulate rice yields under climate change scenarios can be a powerful tool that can considerably “help” decision-makers by reducing the time and human resources required for analysing different land-use strategies and cropping systems in the context of this proposed project. This can also contribute to strengthening national technical and institutional capacities, thereby having long-term benefits.

25. The proposed project has an exit strategy encompassing technical, institutional and financial aspects, including the creation of strong links between farmers and other rice value chain stakeholders that enables the activities to be carried out over the long term.
26. Overall, the ITA considers the paradigm shift potential of the project as medium.

### 1.4 Sustainable development potential
**Scale: High**

The project offers several environmental, social and economic co-benefits. In so doing it promotes women’s empowerment. More precisely, it is aligned with the following United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): **2 – Zero hunger**, **3 – Good health and well-being**, 5 – Gender equality**, 8 – Decent work and economic growth**, 10 – Reduced inequality**, 12 – Responsible consumption and production,** 13 – Climate action** and 15 – Life on land.

The main environmental externalities resulting from the project’s interventions - notably the adoption of climate-smart practices in rice farming - include: improved soil and air quality, conservation of water resources and protection of biodiversity.

In social terms, the project is likely to contribute to improving the health of farmers and consumers through better access to nutritious food. It will also improve women’s empowerment by increasing their income, reducing input costs, obtaining better prices for sustainably produced rice and providing them with greater opportunities in decision-making on the provision of climate-smart adaptation and mitigation services.

Economically, the project will help to boost the incomes of climate-smart farmers and service providers by increasing yields, reducing input costs, obtaining better prices for sustainably produced rice and providing them with greater opportunities in decision-making. It will also contribute to improving access to financing and encouraging institutions to provide specific interventions to women.

**References:**
Janssen, J.W.F et al., 1998. Decision support system for agronomic advice transfer: DSSAT v4. In Teug, H.V., Hoogmoedt, G., Thornton, Understanding Options for Agricultural Production, 157-177. Kluwer Academic Publishers.

1. **Needs of the recipient** Scale: High

   - Thailand has been one of the countries most affected by climate hazards in recent years.
   - This demonstrates a high level of vulnerability that is likely to increase, particularly as a result of its exposure to unprecedented adverse climatic conditions, such as more prolonged and intense periods of drought and flooding.
   - The country's economy is highly dependent on the climate, and high temperatures combined with more frequent and longer dry spells are likely to hinder significantly the productivity of rice production. In particular, small-scale farmers, who will be particularly affected by their capacity to adapt is relatively low.

2. **Country ownership** Scale: High

   - The development of the project's interventions and activities is the result of a long and extensive stakeholder consultation process.
   - This included representatives from the public and private sectors, civil society organizations and international technical and financial partners.
   - This extensive stakeholder consultation process enabled the project’s various interventions to be aligned with Thailand’s national priorities for rice production and the development of rice farming set out in the country's various policy documents, in particular Thailand’s National Climate Change Policy (2015–2036), the National Adaptation Plan, the Thailand mid-long-term Low Greenhouse Gas Emission Development Strategy (LT-LGED) and the Sustainable Agriculture Strategic Plan for the agriculture sector (2022-2027).
   - The project GEF Trust Fund is co-financed by other institutions responsible for the project’s implementation have a long track record of implementing climate change projects. These include government agencies implementing more than 300 climate-change-related projects, with combined funding of over USD 1.9 billion.
   - The Rice Development operates more than 50 centers in more than 20 provinces of Thailand and is the largest rice research institution in Thailand.
   - The BAGC, a government-owned bank established in 1966, is the leading bank in terms of loans to the agricultural sector.

**Page 229**

### Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale High**

The proposed project promotes the adoption of several CSA T&F recommended by leading institutions in the agricultural sector, such as the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations, and rice farming in particular, such as IRRI. These CSA T&F are globally considered to be highly effective and have a proven track record.

The co-financing ratio is 1:2.1 and *the vast majority* of the GCF grant (EUR 287 million out of EUR 361.5 million) is dedicated to technical assistance and other public good-type activities.

The estimated GCF cost of adaptation per beneficiary is EUR 150-55 and the estimated GCF cost of mitigation per beneficiary is EUR 49, reflecting the expected economic, social and environmental benefits that may result from the actions implemented.

Overall, the IAP assesses the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project as high.


## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Based on the analysis presented in the above paragraph, since the IAP endorses, without condition, the project concept titled “Thai Rice: Strengthening Rice Climate Risk Farming,” In order to improve its project, including its impacts, the IAP recommends the following:

During the first year of the project, a detailed monitoring and evaluation plan should be developed, capturing the responsible institutions as well as the timeline for each activity.

During the first year of the project, a clear mechanism should be established for ensuring that any CSA T&F that are not directly related to climate change adaptation are converted into carbon credits, as per the terms of the accreditation master agreement between GCF and GIZ.

During the inception phase, an improved knowledge management plan should be developed. This includes the development of a database on best practices in rice farming, including the development of rice varieties that are better adapted to current and adverse climate conditions. Furthermore, it must ensure that knowledge of the key determinants of the adoption of CSA T&F is systematized and translated in a way that can be leveraged in future initiatives.

## Proposal name:
Thai Rice: Strengthening Climate-Smart Rice Farming

## Accredited entity:
Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) GmbH

### Impact potential
The AE acknowledges the ITAP’s response with thanks and appreciates the encouraging feedback, particularly in highlighting key success factors within the project design.

The AE agrees with the overall assessment of ITAP on the impact potential. The project will, overall, contribute to a significant reduction in GHG emissions from the rice sector in Thailand by 2035. In addition, the AE agrees that the project will be a model project in order to achieve the envisioned objectives, and the project will continue to develop the system of MRV that has been recommended for use in Thailand, the Office of Natural Resources and Environmental Policy and Planning (ONREP) and the Rice Research Institute (RRI). The Department of Agriculture (DOA) is responsible for management of Thailand’s national GHG inventory.

The range of experiences that have informed the development of the climate-smart technologies and practices are diverse. These include a combination of a number of baseline projects in Thailand, augmented by the extensive experience of the Rice Department and the International Rice Research Institute (IRRI).

The AE further acknowledges and agrees with the suggestion by ITAP to pay special attention to future research needs and to sharpen the final identification of agricultural interventions to be implemented.

### Paradigm shift potential
The AE acknowledges and appreciates the assessment by ITAP. The project will, indeed, strive to achieve a paradigm shift in the Thai rice sector through a combination of climate-smart technologies and practices.

The recommendation by ITAP also place an increased focus on the use of process-based crop models to identify adaptation solutions is well noted. The AE plans to take up this suggestion with the Department of Agriculture (DOA) and other relevant stakeholders for the purpose of individual climate-smart agricultural interventions during project implementation. This data can then be used as a basis for further research, development, testing, validation, and refinement of existing crop models, thereby strengthening the capacities of national partners and helping to make informed decisions about adaptation strategies.

# Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

We note and acknowledge the positive and encouraging response from ITAP.

As indicated above, the project's monitoring and evaluation system – in parallel with the project’s own internal monitoring and evaluation framework – will be based on the recommendation by ITAP. The AE has already started to identify potential areas to improve the current monitoring and evaluation framework and agree to develop a revised version within the first year of project implementation.

The AE acknowledges and agrees with the recommendation by ITAP regarding the need for establishing a dedicated credit management system in order to ensure that all project credits will be used only for rice production. The AE will also establish a mechanism to safeguard this during project implementation. While mobilizing potential sources of finance from carbon credits is an element of the post-project exit strategy, the AE agrees with ITAP’s recommendation and agrees to develop a revised version within the first year of project implementation.

The AE fully notes the feedback on the knowledge management plan. This plan will be continuously updated in line with the project’s progress and will include the evaluation of empirical adoption rates and the use of process-based crop model information for the decision-making support system, such as the Rice Department, as well as for the benefit of other (for example, regional) initiatives.

The changing demographic (aging) of rice farmers, as well as other socio-economic, gender and labour-related issues, are indeed, factors that influence the project design. While reversing these trends is not within the scope of this project implementation phase, the AE will integrate these socio-economic factors into project implementation and the selection of interventions (as part of the on-going development programming).

# Chapter 1: Introduction

This gender assessment serves as an input to the Funding Proposal for the project "Thai Rice - Strengthening low-emission smart rice farming" to the GCF that will include elements of technical cooperation as well as financial cooperation. The project aims at strengthening the resilience of Thai rice farmers and their families against climate change impacts through sustainable rice farming, through the promotion of low emission, sustainable rice production. During the Concept Note development, a preliminary identification of gender-related risks indicated that female rice farmers are often excluded from access to information on climate change adaptation and rural support services. In consequence, women are often at risk of being excluded from training programs and other activities related to the project. To address these issues and GCF gender-related requirements for Funding Proposals, this gender assessment has been developed.

This report was written based on desk review of reports as well as academic papers, and a series of key informant interviews of government officials, NGOs and GIZ field officers. It is also based on information gathered during field visits to Chiangrai province and Roiet province to meet women's groups and mega farmers. Please see appendix for the list of respondents.

# Chapter 2: Information on gender dimensions in the country or the region

## 2.1 Meta level (norms)

### Gender equality in statistics

Thailand ranks 79 out of 181 countries in the 2021 Gender Inequality Index (UNDP, 2021). In comparison with other countries in the Southeast Asian region, Malaysia was ranked 57 and Vietnam was ranked 63. The gender pay gap for women in Thailand is 49%, which means that the ratio of female to male earnings is wider - Vietnam is 69.8% for women and 79.4% for men; while in Thailand, it is 59.9%. For women's representation in parliament, Thailand has a lower rate than Malaysia (21%) but higher than Vietnam (20%). In terms of female representation in the cabinet, Thailand has a higher rate compared to both Malaysia and Vietnam (24% for Thailand; 37.6% for Malaysia; 58.9% for Vietnam). The number of women serving as parliamentarians is 12%, which is lower than that of Malaysia (22%) but higher than that of Vietnam (16%). The adolescent birth rate was considerably high in these two countries (Thailand 32.7, Vietnam 34.5 births per 1000 girls aged 15–19 years) compared to the global average of 28.5.

UN Women observe that the challenges faced by Thailand in terms of gender equality and women's empowerment include: Lack of gender-disaggregated data; violence against women; lack of access to justice for women; limited access to education, especially among those pertaining to an ethnic group and rural women; HIV/AIDS prevalence; low participation of women in political life; persistence of traditional norms and beliefs that endorse the discrimination against women (UNDP, 2021).

With the limited sex-disaggregated data available, gender gaps can be seen in Table 1. Women are less likely to be employed in certain sectors as compared with men. According to Thailand's Voluntary National Review Report, the proportion of companies that had at least one female executive increased from 74% in 2017 to 86% in 2020 (Thailand, 2021). Women spend disproportionately more time on unpaid care work and domestic work than men. On average, women spent one hour more on care work and household work per day than men (Thailand, 2021).

### Violence against women

Violence against women is still prevalent in Thailand. The number of women who experienced violence and received the support of One Stop Crisis Center (OSCC) under the Ministry of Public Health has increased from 3,754 cases in 2019 to 6,866 cases in 2021. More than half (55%) of these cases involved their intimate partners as abusers (Sumaro, 2020). The number of women who experienced violence and received support from the OSCC was 23% lower in 2020 at 31,866 compared to 2019 and 2013, respectively (Chueanchit et al., 2018). Of the 30,000 cases of violence against women reported to the OSCC, only around 5,000 cases are recorded by the police, and only about 1,700 cases are referred for legal action. The data from the OSCC in 2021 indicates a higher proportion of violence cases in the North and Central regions compared to other regions (Sumaro, 2020).

The entrenched culture of shame in Thailand makes reporting of these incidents of violence, as well as reporting of harassment cases, difficult. The Thai Public Broadcasting Service (PBS) calls sexual violence against women a "taboo topic" that is not discussed openly. Men's strong public image and authority are used to threaten the victims, and the abusers can be trusted by society because they have power over their victims. Women who report the perpetrator have more power than the victim, the latter being too scared to report since they are afraid that no one will believe her. The stigmatization of female victims of sexual harassment or abuse is a major reason for not reporting such incidents (Sumaro, 2020). The Thai justice system is not well equipped to support the victims (Buung, 2022).

In Thailand, and especially in the lowlands, most communities practice matrilineal patterns of living and inheritance. Women are considered the owners of their own bodies, their women's house, and women stay with or near their own parents and relatives. Women, or often the younger generation, manage the family property and inherit it from their mothers. Women also have more control over land and property. Matrilineal patterns of living have their advantages. Living near their own parents and relatives provides a sense of security for women in terms of protection, food, and being away from such living patterns by moving into urban areas can diminish women's position since they lose their support system that is available in their place of origin (Mills, 1997). Matrilineal patterns also provide women with more control over land inheritance.


---
**Page 239**
---

towards women to be more responsible for their elderly parents. When women and men migrate from rural to urban areas in search of work, women tend to remit more regularly because of their socially expected responsibility to look after their parents (Rungnawee et al., 2021).

Gender stereotypes and women's political participation

Although the statistics and reports point to the fact that gender inequality persists in Thailand, gender issues are manifested in a subtle way in daily life. On the surface, women in rural areas of Thailand are seen as more submissive than their urban counterparts. However, there exist strong gender stereotypes that are derogating towards women. Women are not able to become monks or join the military because they are considered too weak and fragile for such roles. Such women lack physical strength can affect women's choices of work or can be used as an excuse for gender discrimination in the workplace (Rungnawee et al., 2021). In addition, women are expected to keep their skin fair and beautiful, and are expected to be delicate and feminine (Richardson & Richardson, 2013). Women are also expected to be more patient than men. Some women still have to do most of the household work. However, even with such a clear division of labour, it is often assumed that women can do everything while men cannot. It is believed that they are able to do whatever men do. Women are often perceived to be good at business and communication and it is widely believed that women are more patient and hence better able to deal with difficult situations (Rungnawee et al., 2021).

Women face cultural barriers to political participation. For example, "parochial norms pose challenges to women who run for office at the local level – norms that expect them to stay at home" (Pantazis & Sutharat, 2013). In addition, "the gender stereotypes and expectations that are involved in politics complicate women's advancement in the political arena.

Women self-identifying as pertaining to an ethnic group

Ethnic groups self-identifying as indigenous people mainly reside in the hilly areas of Northern Thailand, although some are forced to move out of their communities because they have lost cultivated lands to outside investors (WINT, 2011). Although many hill community people have ID cards and can vote, there is a lack of political representation. Furthermore, there are fewer women who can read, write and speak the Thai language well (WINT, 2011).

Women who have no official nationality must endure a form of imprisonment in their own communities because they cannot leave or enter Thailand without permission from the police. Although security measures fail to protect these women, many of them decide into enter the sex industry as a means of survival and to earn money for themselves (WINT, 2011). It is believed that will help them obtain nationality for their families and themselves, particularly for those who are widows and have children to take care of (WINT, 2011).

Traditional belief systems limit the way women in the hill communities express themselves and their opinions. Women are seen as responsible for reproductive work. In Thailand, there are many migrant workers from neighboring countries, who in many cases stay for an extended period of time. These women are often subjected to gender-based violence while working in Thailand. Their precarious legal status is exacerbated by the fact that women migrants are more vulnerable than men because they have less access to information and protection against gender-based violence. Women are paid less than men because of gender discrimination and sex segregation (Rungnawee et al., 2021). Women who migrate with their families often leave family and children (both physically when the children are with them, and through remittances when they have left children behind) (Pearson & Kusakabe, 2012).


5

2.2 Macro level (policies and laws)

Thailand ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1985 and further ratified the optional protocol in 2006. It adopted the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (BPFA) in 1995. Recent improvements in gender equality include:

- The Name Act was amended to allow women the right to choose a family name (2005).
- Amendment to Civil Code to provide women and men equal grounds for divorce (2007).
- Rape was criminalized by the Penal Code of 1965, and the subsequent Criminal Code Amendment Act of 2008.
- The Labor Protection Act of 2008 and the Penal Code foresee legal protection in the workplace against sexual harassment. However, sexual harassment, including harassment or nuisance by superiors to cover all workers, both female and male, with penalties for those found guilty, is still a serious problem that nonetheless continues to be an issue in the workplace (UNDP, 2021).
- Anti-trafficking law (Act No. 38/2560) was introduced as a measure to prevent and suppress the trafficking of women and children.
- The Domestic Violence Victim Protection Act B.E. 2550 passed in 2007, and the amendment in 2019 aims at preventing and suppressing domestic violence and gender-based violence.
- Amendment to the Thai Penal Code to relax currently restrictive regulations on abortion by decriminalizing the termination of pregnancy during the first 12 weeks (2021).

The Gender Equality Act was introduced in Thailand in 2015. The Act establishes three committees for the promotion of gender equality: the gender equality committee, the gender equality fund executive committee. This is the first law to provide a comprehensive framework for the protection and empowerment of people from gender discrimination, including transgender people. Although the Department of Women's Affairs and Family Development recognizes challenges faced by transgender peo- ple, they have not yet been able to address these issues due to religious and national security exceptions on issues related to religious principles and national security” (UNDP 2021:15).

The 2017 Constitution prohibits discrimination on the grounds of sex and affirms the principle of equality between men and women. The government has established a Gender Equality Committee under the Ministry of Social Development and Human Security, which is responsible for formulating the National Gender Responsive Budgeting, determining that the government when allocating the budget, shall take into account gender equality in order to ensure fairness. Following this, the Budget Bureau has asked spending ministries to indicate their spending plans with regard to gender equality and to conduct a gender impact (GOI), 2021). In line with the SDG 5 (Gender equality) and aligning with the 20-Year National Gender Equality Strategy (2017-2021) was established with the aim to create social justice without discrimination. It is the main mechanism for Thailand to promote women's development (UNDP, 2021).

The three-year Action Plan to promote gender equality (2020-2022) of the Department of Women's Affairs and Family Development under the Ministry of Social Development and Hu- man Security aims at: (i) promoting gender equality; (ii) strengthening women’s rights and gender equality; (iii) strengthening gender empowerment; (iv) all organizations to have policies on


---
**Page 241**
---

gender equality; and (iv) promote research and development on gender equality. The monitoring at the ministry level is done through the Chief Gender Equality Officer (CGEO) and the Gender Focal Points (GFPs). Since the establishment of the CGEO in 2015, the Cabinet has made all permanent secretaries to serve as their ministries' CGEO.

The weakness in Thailand's policy on gender equality was summarized by UNDP [2021:16] as below:

"In CEDAW's 2017 Concluding Observations on the state of gender equality in Thailand, the Committee expressed particular concern for the situation of rural Thai women, including indigenous women and those from ethnic and religious minorities, since they are often excluded from decision-making processes and limited to traditional opportunities. CEDAW spells out that Thai women, in particular rural women, are also excluded from access to information on climate change and disaster risk reduction, despite the fact they are disproportionately affected by these phenomena. The Committee recommended action plans on climate change and disaster risk reduction, due to the fact they are disproportionately affected by these phenomena."

Besides the lack of clear policy directives on the integration of gender equality and mainstreaming in national development strategies and implementation of climate policies and plans, another key challenge in gender equality and social inclusion is lack of sex-disaggregated data.

The lack of data prohibits the government's ability to design specific interventions. Thailand considers itself a middle-income country but has not been able to achieve gender equality (UNDP, 2021). As of 2020, only 45.9% of the indicators needed to monitor progress towards SDG 5 are available in Thailand. This is also true for labor market indicators such as the gender pay gap, gender and poverty, physical and sexual harassment, and violence against women.

Data lacks comparable methodologies for regular monitoring (UN Women, n.d.). Thailand has developed a National Action Plan on Gender Equality (NAPGE) but still needs to translate it into action. To achieve this goal, strengthening the collection of sex-disaggregated data, as well as capacity building of organizations, remain an urgent task.

### 2.3 Meso level (institutions)

The Ministry of Social Development and Human Security has tasked the Department of Women's Affairs and Family Development as the national women's machinery responsible for providing policy direction on gender equality in Thailand. The Department of Women's Affairs and Family Development is responsible for formulating policies and programs to promote gender equality, including the promotion of women's rights and increasing coordination among concerned government offices and non-government organizations. It is also the focal point office for the implementation of the National Action Plan on the Improvement of the Status of Women, which is chaired by the Prime Minister. The Office implements the NAPGE through the Chief Gender Equality Officers (CGEOs) and Gender Focal Points (GFPs) in 131 government departments and agencies. CGEOs are responsible for providing policy direction and mainstreaming support in the various subnational government agencies (ADB, 2013). Other government agencies that play an important role in gender equality are the National Human Rights


---
**Page 242**
---

The Thai government has constituted a Working Group for the Implementation of SDG 5. The Working Group is chaired by the Prime Minister and includes representatives from organizations such as the Thailand Sustainable Development Foundation, the Association to Empower the Potential of Women, the National Commission on Human Rights, the Thailand Union of Industrial Relations Foundation for Labour and Employment Promotion and Welfare. The Working group exchanges information and discusses future directions on gender equality (UNDP, 2021).

Other important non-government institutions and Civil Society Organizations (CSOs) that are engaged in equal opportunities for women include the following: These include the Health and Opportunity Network (HON), in Pattaya; M+ Plus HIV/AIDS, LGBTIQ+, Gender and Development Network (M+); Thai Women’s Foundation (TWF); The Association of Transgender Bangkok (Bangkok); Teratai Karanja-Isaeng Foundation; Profounders Foundation; Sisters Foundation (Pattaya); The Transgender Alliance of Thailand (TAT); The Transgender Alliance of Thailand (TAT), Transgender People Association of Thailand, Transmen Alliance of Thailand (TMAT), Transpiration Power, and Asia Pacific Transgender Network (APTN).

The United Nations Framework Convention on Population (UNFPA) Act 2017-2021 was signed by the United Nations Country Team (UNCT) in Thailand and the Royal Thai Government (RTG) in July 2017 with a single outcome: By 2021, inclusive systems, structures and processes advance gender equality and women’s empowerment. The UN Women Gender Equality and Empowerment of Women changed the Gender Theme Group in 2017, serving as an advisory group to the UNCT on gender equality and women's empowerment. The group has been instrumental in highlighting its collective impact in advancing gender equality and women’s empowerment, and led the 2017 UNCT Country Programme.

Since July 2019, the theme group has been changed to the Gender Equality and Social Inclusion (GESI), chaired by UN Women and UNDP, with the aim not only advise UNCT on gender issues but also provide technical assistance in implementing GESI-related recommendations including facilitation of the System-wide Action Plan (SWAP) on Gender Equality and the Empowerment of Women. It also provided training as well as monitoring implementation through the SWAP. The GESI Theme Group was a key partner to UNDP in developing SDG Results Group on Peace and Governance with UNDP (since February 2018) and the COVID-19 response based discussions (UNDP, n.d.b).

### 2.4 Micro level (target groups)

#### Discrimination against women

According to the Association for the Promotion of the Status of Women, discriminatory practices against women still exist in many areas in Thailand, especially in rural areas. These include employment discrimination and lack of access to education and health services. More specifically, there is a lack of policies promoting economic empowerment and access to financial services for disadvantaged groups of women. Only 13.67% of women have borrowed money from banks or other financial institutions (Association for the Promotion of the Status of Women, n.d.).


---
**Page 243**
---

### Women's representation in decision-making bodies

Women are under-represented in both public and private decision-making bodies. There is only one female governor from 78 provinces (1.32%), women Chiefs of Provincial Administrative Organisations (PAOs) account for 45% (UNDP, 2021). These figures rank Thailand as one of the lowest in Asia and the world in terms of gender equality. Women who aim for an active role in the community are still expected to fulfill their household tasks before they go beyond their household (Homlemen- Inthavong, 2019).

### Women's access to reproductive health services

Thailand’s primary health care, including reproductive health care services, is provided through a network of sub-district health centres. (WHO, 2021:1) lists the achievements of reproductive health care services in Thailand:

- **80%** of all pregnant women used prenatal care in 1987, 92% in 2000, and 100% in 2012 when the last data was collected.
- The contraceptive prevalence rate (CPR) increased from 4.8% in 1990 to 99.7% in 2012.
- Further, the Contraceptive Prevalence Rate (CPR) increased from 14.8% in 1976 to 79.3% in 2012.
- The total fertility rate also decreased from 5.3 children per woman in 1976 to 1.8 in 2012.

However, there are some remaining challenges, such as a high level of teenage pregnancy. The adolescent birth rate increased from 42 per 1,000 women aged 15-19 years in 2007 to 52 in 2012 (WHO, 2013). In addition, the percentage of young women who have received reproductive health knowledge is higher among younger women (11.6% for women aged 15–19 years old) than for older women (8.4%). The high level of teenage pregnancy puts more stress and burden on women than men. Pregnant girls continue to be prohibited from going to school, and their education. Teenage pregnancy becomes a problem for the girls and their families, while the boys and the community benefit.

### Challenges accessing safe abortions

Women in Thailand face many challenges accessing safe abortions, though from October 2019, abortion has been allowed up to the 28th week of pregnancy. Abortions during the first 12 weeks became legal in Thailand in February 2021. Previously, abortion had been illegal and was only permitted if the life or mental health of the pregnant woman was threatened; the woman was under 15 years of age, became pregnant as the result of rape or incest, or was married to a man who was more than three years older.

It is officially estimated that about 30,000 abortions occur each year (including induced, spontaneous and therapeutic abortions), but this can be under-estimated, and other figures suggest 200,000–300,000 annually (UNDP, 2021).


---
**Page 244**
---

# Women in vulnerable situations

One of the sub-groups of women who are in a vulnerable situation is young mothers, especially young mothers (including teenage mothers) with small children. They frequently face difficulties because they have to balance their responsibilities at home and at work due to lack of day-care management because of childcare responsibilities. Furthermore, they often face stigma, although since there are more single-parent households than ever before in Thailand, people are becoming more used to seeing young mothers.

Another sub-group of women in vulnerable situations are older women in skipped-generation households (grandchildren or grandchildren whose parents are working and living elsewhere). Because of the lack of affordability, many elderly women have no choice but to live with their grandchildren under the care of their grandparents. There were 405,000 skipped-generation households (elderly women who live with their grandchildren) in Thailand as of September 2019, an increase from 378,000 households, with an increasing trend (UNDP, 2021). Among the skipped-generation house- hold, they are more likely to be single mothers or widows (with no husband or son and grandchildren), and they tend to be more vulnerable. About 20% of elders in skipped-generation households have income below the poverty line – more than twice the national poverty rate (UNDP, 2021).

A third sub-group are women elders who live alone with elderly spouses or with grandchildren. 63% of them live in rural areas and 37% in urban areas. The number of single elderly people living alone has increased from 589,000 in 2017, and it is estimated that 20% of elders will soon be living below the poverty line, the majority being women (UNDP, 2021).

A fourth sub-group of women are disabled women as well as women who take care of the sick and the disabled. Disabled people receive only 800 baht per month as monthly support from the government. They also have to pay for their own medical expenses. Many of them do not know about their disability and have difficulties finding employment. As a result, they are excluded from compensation measures that are available through the employment social security scheme of the government (UNDP, 2021). This makes it difficult for disabled women to earn an income both financially and physically, which puts a disproportionately larger responsibility on them (Protection interna- tional, 2016).

A fifth sub-group of vulnerable women are women self-identifying as pertaining to an ethnic group. Most of the ethnic groups in rural Thailand live in remote hill areas. They experience significant barriers to accessing health care and education due to their geographical location (UNDP et al., 2018). Ethnic groups face discrimination in access to health care, especially since older women from these groups are more likely to be illiterate or have limited knowledge of the language. As a result, women self-identifying as pertaining to an ethnic group are more vulnerable to preventable diseases (e.g. cervical cancer and HIV/AIDS). Ethnic groups in Thailand tend to be more isolated than other groups due to their geographical location which makes it difficult for them to access health services (UNDP, 2021).

A sixth sub-group consists of migrant women from neighboring countries. Even when they do arrive in Thailand, they are often unable to find work and tend to be paid less and have less access to training and careers. This situation persists despite the Labor Protection Act 1998 guaran- teeing equal opportunities for employment and equal treatment and remuneration between women and men in employment.

* Since indigenous people (ethnic groups) live in the hill areas, there are very few living in the inland paddle production com-


---
**Page 245**
---

Care work

In Thai society, care work is carried out almost solely by women – both childcare and elderly care. As mentioned above, much of the vulnerability of women comes from their care work responsibilities. The majority of these jobs are unpaid or underpaid, and the number of jobs they are limited in scale and scope. For example, Village Health Volunteers (VHVs) are introduced to provide basic healthcare services for rural communities, but most VHVs only look after small children. They are trained and receive a small stipend for their work. Most VHVs are women. Recently, all sub-districts have introduced a care worker scheme, with the municipality government providing training and stipends for women who want to become care workers, along with women’s group in Roi et province, 1 October (2022).

There are also some sub-districts that have introduced day care centers. Around fifty of child age groups can be accommodated at Child Development Centres or pre-primary education. However, the lack of affordable childcare services for children under three is still a problem [United Nations, 2019].

COVID-19 pandemic has led to close down of schools, which increased women’s childcare work. It has also increased women’s healthcare work at home, as well as other reproductive activities as family members spend longer time at home (UNDP, 2021).

Conclusions for the project

• Thailand is a patriarchal society, and women have a certain level of resources and power within the household. However, gender discrimination in all forms, especially periodic violence-based violence endures, teenage pregnancy dignifies girls, and women in leadership positions earn much less than men. The lack of access to education and strong gender norms expecting women to be good mothers and wives are strong.

• Because of the lack of gender-disaggregated data, it is difficult to understand the extent of the problem. However, there have been a number of laws that were introduced to improve gender equality, but the enforcement and operationalization of gender equality law remain weak.

• Thai women in general have a good education, have relatively good basic ICT skills compared to men, and are active in productive activities. There is a potential for women that women can exploit if given appropriate recognition and support.

• Some particularly vulnerable groups of women include single mothers with young children who are living in poverty, elderly people, elderly women living alone, disabled women and women with care responsibilities. These groups are often stigmatized as pertaining to an ethnic group, and women migrant workers.

• There is little support for women’s care work responsibilities. There is a lack of affordable childcare services for children under three.

# Chapter 3: Gender dimensions in the sector

## 3.1 Meta level

### Women’s employment in agriculture

According to the World Bank (2020), estimated women's employment in agriculture as a share of all female employment declined from 60.8% in 1991 to 27.8% in 2019 in Thailand. Thai women are more likely than men to be employed in agriculture, forestry, and fishing due to limited access to education and other productive resources, and have greater control over household income than men (Akar et al., 2017). Women in rural areas of Thailand are also more likely to be involved in agricultural activities than those who live in urban areas. This is partly because women's rights to land ownership are often limited by the Civil and Commercial Code, although those who take care of the parents (who tend to be daughters) get to lend out (UNDP, 2021). Historical and ethnographic studies de-scribe women as being more involved in agriculture than men. Women and men have the same economic rights including in relation to opening bank accounts according to the Civil Code (Akar et al., 2017; UNDP, 2021; World Bank, 2014). The government has put in place a number of measures, such as the National Village and Urban Community Fund, to assist women in rural and urban communities and provide vocational training to women and men alike (UNDP, 2021).

### Women as unpaid family workers

Despite such a positive trend, gender inequality in Thailand persists in a more subtle way. One of the issues is the invisibility of women's contribution. The majority of women (37%) workers in the agriculture sector are considered unpaid farm workers. Having women’s work categorized as unpaid farm work means that they do not receive any compensation for their labor spent farming together with women, but women might also be engaged in other backyard activities such as cooking and cleaning. In this case, women's contribution to agricultural production is often underestimated because it is not counted on the basis of men's farming activity since women are supposed to be "assisting" men. Such a perception can lead to gender inequality in terms of compensation for work done and support women’s farming activities. Hence, policy measures such as agricultural trade expansion might not be beneficial for women farmers (Korwananakorn, 2020). In addition, the unpaid nature of farm work is often taken into consideration; making unpaid women workers relatively more vulnerable than men in the agriculture sector (Korwananakorn, 2020).

In Thailand, 27% of women legally own agricultural land, which is higher than the average for OECD countries. However, it should be noted that Thai women make up 41% of the agricultural workforce. Women put in more work relative to their land ownership in Thailand (OECD, 2021).

### Gender division of labour in agriculture in Thailand

There is a clear gender division of labour in agriculture in Thailand. Men do the so-called phys-ically strenuous tasks such as ploughing, planting seeds, operating machinery tractors, harvesters, etc., as well as spraying chemicals. Agricultural labour is often paid by work done, and the types of work that men do are often paid more per hour. Women’s work, such as supplementary farm work, is usually unpaid or underpaid (Korwananakorn et al., 2019). This is particularly the case for migrant agriculture workers. Women migrants often do packing work, while men migrants do the heavier tasks of ploughing and planting seeds. This division of labour was discussed at a Fi-ocus Group Discussion with women in Chiangrai September 30, 2022. Such gender division of labor is based on the perception that women are better at "feminine" tasks (Richardson and


---
**Page 247**
---

Roberts, 2020). Women are regarded as secondary farm laborers rather than principal earners (Nguyen et al., 2019). Women also tend to be more concentrated in lower-value produce production and marketing of agricultural products such as processing and retail trade of agricultural goods or non-farm income-generating activities. Even when they participate in the same activity with men, their roles are often limited to labor-intensive production; women's activities are most of the time considered as supplementary income.

Despite the continued prevalence of gendered roles and gender stereotypes, both women and men in rural areas hold a sense of gender equality in agriculture. A study investigating the thought process behind this phenomenon found that there is a feeling of partnership between women and men in rural Thailand (Pimprap, 2012). Women are more likely to be involved in decision-making at the household level than in urban areas (Nguyen et al., 2019; Pimprap, 2012; Hotkates, 1984). This higher level of collectivism, which has manifested itself in the importance placed on family and community relationships rather than individualism or extended relationships (Pimprap, 2012), makes it difficult for women to separate their personal interests from those of their families. Such cultural values make gender issues more difficult to identify and to problematize. Gender issues exist but they are internalized into everyday practices in rural areas.

### 3.2 Macro level

The Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives (MOAC) is responsible for food security in Thailand. Its 30-year agriculture and cooperative plan (2017-2036) includes SDG 5 (gender equality) as one of the guiding SDGs. It recognizes gender equality and women's empowerment in public-private partnerships, with the aim of increasing the efficiency of agricultural cooperatives through their supply chains.

In other plans related to agriculture, gender equality is mentioned sporadically. The Action Plan of the Department of Agriculture (2017-2021) includes a specific action under "Development of agricultural group and community enterprises into Small Groups" (Activity 1.3, page 9). It outlines an objective to strengthen women's groups and women farmers' ties in order to increase their participation in agricultural cooperatives and other organizations. The subsequent Action Plan of the Department of Agriculture Extension (2023-2027) also refers to women's groups as a priority group for extension services.

Although MOAC does not have a separate gender policy or gender strategy, it follows the national policy on gender equality. It recognizes women's role in agriculture, but with a greater emphasis on their contribution to agricultural development and food security. It is being coordinated with the Community Development Department of the Ministry of Interior to develop and to promote "One Tambon One Product" (OTOP). Many OTOP products are produced by women's groups or cooperatives that have been established in rural areas, often incorporating into existing agricultural policy (Nguyen et al., 2019). Despite higher levels of women's

10 One Tambon One Product (OTOP) is a total entrepreneurship stimulus program which aims to support the unique locally made and marketed products of each Thai tambon (sub-district) all over Thailand. Superb products from sub-districts are selected for serious formal branding as a "Tambon OTOP product" and are promoted with promotion support locally and nationally.

# The Plan to Drive the Realization of Gender Equality 2019-2021 by the Gender Equality Pro- motion Committee (GEP) for Thailand

The GEP has set a goal to promote gender equality in agriculture. The plan noted, under its commitment to SDG 2 (zero hunger), that agricultural activities are not only about food production but also include raising small animals, collection and processing of food from the forest, mixed crop agriculture, and producing cash crops. It is important to note that when traditional ways of agriculture are destroyed, and cash income is acquired greater importance than household subsistence for sustainable development. Therefore, the Plan argues that in order to help the environment and implement sustainable agriculture, improving women's decision-making power is important.

## 3.3 Meso level

The Department of Women’s Affairs and Family Development sent a directive to all ministries to appoint a Chief Gender Equality Officer in November 2020. However, such appointments have not been made as yet.

### The Rice Department promotes and supports rice farmers to obtain the Sustainable Rice Plat- form certification. It is noted that the Sustainable Rice Platform Performance Indicators for Sustainable Rice Cultivation (SRI) are based on the following principles: increased productivity, improved soil health, reduced greenhouse gas emissions, improved maternal health, improved family health and well-being. Women’s empowerment is expected to lead to better nutrition and food security.

The measurement of women's empowerment is based upon a scorecard covering the following topics:

- Women's control over decisions regarding household agricultural production
- Women's control over decisions regarding their own labor input
- Women's access to land, credit, and inputs
- Women’s access to information and capacity building
- Women's control over long-term resources for farm activities
- Women's control over decisions regarding household income
- Women's control over decisions regarding household expenditure
- Women's participation in collective decision-making

The Department of Agricultural Extension works closely with farmers. In the past, most extension workers were men. However, with the improvement of infrastructure, travel to villages has become easier and more frequent. As a result, more women are coming in to work as government officers in ADACs. As a result, there are now more women extension workers and, in some districts, at the extension workers are women.

15


---
**Page 249**
---

The Bank for Agriculture and Agricultural Cooperatives (BAAC) is implementing several projects targeted at the rural populace, some of which promote gender mainstreaming and support national strategies on women's empowerment. These projects aim to provide financial services to women in rural areas and identify those who have difficulty in repaying loans, although such gender-disaggregated data or even a gender lens are not yet available for BAAC. The Bank has also received the Sustainable Discharge Plaque as an outstanding organization that protects rights and promotes equality between the genders on International Women's Day 2016 (BAAC, 2015).

BAAC offers both life insurance and agriculture insurance. While those holding an account in BAAC is covered by life insurance, women are less likely to have one because many of them have difficulty accessing BAAC loans. At the same time, BAAC does not currently have any loan-focused financial schemes for women. However, there are some initiatives that can be considered. The Women Empowerment Funds under the Community Development Department, Ministry of Interior, a Women's Microfinance Fund (WMF) under the National Bank of Agriculture and Cooperatives, and others can borrow money for income-generating purposes. A committee is organized with women leaders in each village to provide loans to women who are willing to pay interest rates ranging from 10% for other agriculture and non-agriculture activities of women. WMF uses 80% of its funds for loans and 20% for subsidies, with a maximum loan size of 30,000 baht for support. The experience of learning from the WMF and applying it to the BAAC might be useful in reflecting on a women-focused financial scheme of BAAC itself.

### Micro level

#### Women's active engagement in agriculture

Men used to be dominant in agricultural work, but the economic situation and the need for cash have pushed women to take up a larger role in Thai agriculture. Nowadays, women usually perform all or most of the farm work while men are more involved with livestock farming and the household while men work in the field as well as earn money outside the village. Women also participate in agricultural activities such as planting, weeding, harvesting, and off-farm. Hence, women have increasingly assumed a decision-making role alongside their husbands in agriculture. This is because of the increasing burden on women within the family and community, as well as women's lower social location in society and lack of women in leadership roles; the rise of financial management is actually more of a burden and responsibility for women and does not always increase their power and freedom (Rungnangkug et al. 2019).

#### Women's time burden and care work

As noted above, women are in charge of almost all of the household work and men only "help" with household chores. Even though women are doing ever more work in agriculture, their workload is still much higher than that of men. This is because women do not normally perceive household work as a burden. Agriculture is perceived by women as an ideal livelihood to allow them to balance their household work and care work with productive work. Women, as well as men, frequently seek employment in the class after finishing their studies (Odhogale, Kasetsart University, 2018).

Reference: The Bank for Agriculture and Agricultural Cooperatives (BAAC) Kanchanaburi Office. For info @ Aseeya Prasertsuk, BAAC Kanchanaburi Office. The Women Empowerment Funds under the Community Development Department is composed of one elected representative per district (http://www.womenfund.or.th/). According to a study by the National Bank of Agriculture and Cooperatives (2015), women's participation in agriculture has increased but without accompanying programing to raise awareness on gender equality, the loan itself has a limited impact to change gender relations (Wassakorn, 2020).

education, but when women have children or when their parents need care, women are the ones who come back to take up the care work alongside agricultural work1).

Gender division of labour and wage gap

As far as rice production is concerned, the workload has diminished since much of the work has been mechanized. Ploughing, which used to be men’s work or now done using tractors ( albeit driven by men), transplanting, which used to be women's work, has now been largely elimi- nated because it can be done with machines and is now done by both men and women, but more frequently by women. It is now done by harvesters as well. The harvesting of rice is still predominantly a male activity, although women are increasingly doing this work. Weeding is typically strenuous work (including the operation of machinery) and spraying chemicals, while women do not have access to these machines or chemicals. Women’s role in weeding has increased since they are now responsible for secondary transplanting since there are some areas where rice does not sprout. In some places, there is a tendency to cut down on weeding because it is difficult when rice is broadcast and cannot be mechanized. In Roel, farmers cut the rice to get- ting women involved in order to eliminate the weeds. Such cutting is done by men using mechani- cal grass cutters.

Although the agricultural workload has diminished for both women and men, agriculture is still physically hard work. The most commonly experienced health problem of rural women is back pain or lower body aches. This is due to the fact that they are often required to carry heavy loads in their focus group discussions mentioned these problems as being caused by their work load. Despite this, many women do not seek medical help for their aches and pains. Some use home remedies such as medicinal patches or body massages to reinvigorate the strained muscles. Others go to the health centre only when they are seriously ill. They have regular medical checkups and blood tests to check for pesticide contamination (Aitker et al., 2014).

Youths tend to leave rural villages for education and for non-farming employment. This is the case for both women and men. However, when women have children and/or when their par- ents become old, it is often women who come back home to take care of children and the elderly, especially if they are not able to hire help. Women’s role in farming has increased as women have to manage the household and agriculture themselves. Women's rice farming workload has increased because men are leaving for non-farming employment. The fact that pre- production work is mechanized and women will hire labour for land preparation, spraying chemicals, transplanting and harvesting means that they need to be able to do this work. The reason why young men are not able to contribute as much as their fathers did in the past is because young men are not able to commit - that is, there is no one to help in their task of farm management. Women have to make decisions about land preparation and crop selection, and farm management decisions in addition to daily household management (FAO, 2010). Still, there are some women who are currently farming alone because they have household labour and agriculture. For example, young women with children often have to manage their time by juggling different activities.

There is a severe labour shortage in agriculture, and farmers often hire workers. Sometimes the workers are from other villages but often they hire Burmese migrant workers. Especially in the North, they prefer to hire Thai Yai Burmese workers since they speak the Thai language. Women’s role as agricultural workers has increased because men have left for non-farming employment. In 2014, women earned 500 kyat while men earn 300 kyat. This discrepancy is explained as being due to the fact that women are not able to do heavy work and that they are paid less in the past farming season.

These points have been repeatedly mentioned during group discussions with women in Chiangrai and Roel, but especially Roel (where there are no young people who migrate out to work).

# Technology adoption

During trainings organized by the Rice Department as well as NGOs, it is often observed that it is the women who best learn new agricultural technologies. Not only are the majority of train- ing participants women, but they also tend to do better in classroom learning sessions. Although the decision whether to adopt a technology is usually made by men, women's decisions can be given higher importance, since most often women are the ones who will be implementing the technologies. The detailed tasks that are associated with new agricultural practices are often assigned to women, and they are also the ones who have more influence in guiding household members to adopt different ways of doing things, as was expressed during the Focus Group Discussion (FGD) with women in Changrai, during the discussion on agriculture technologies:

> "Many male farmers have been told that they need to use chemicals to do their farming. They are starting to realize that they can use fewer chemicals and go organic. Women are working hard to use less chemicals and advocate for organic farming. Men are not very cooperative, but they are starting to understand. Men are more for chemicals than women, since men do not want to work on details and just want to use phytosanitary products. They are not interested in the chemical details to manage the farm. Women are sacrificing for the family and community. They are the ones who are really working hard."

Women - as the financial managers of the household - are sensitive to the cost of new technologies. Before even thinking about the potential yield increase, their first reaction is technological costs such as seed prices or fertilizer prices. The "organic farming" is popular among women farmers, not only because it is advocated by the former king, but also because they can use less chemicals and fertilizers. This makes organic farming more in line with women farmers' preferences, and it is easier for them to adopt and embrace". When introducing new technologies, it is important that the initial cost is minimized in order to catch the attention of interest of the women.

Women are often keen to embrace new technologies, but there are some areas over which they do not have control. For example, tractors are operated by men. These male operators do not like to share their knowledge with women and will not allow them to operate the tractor. So, they will burn the stubble, sometimes without even discussing this action with the landowner or the farmer.

Both women and men use social media to obtain information on new technologies. As seen in the earlier section, there are fewer women in computer program development, but women are slightly more active than men when it comes to using social media. They help the elderly by downloading apps and registering for various support programs[1]. They google when they have questions, and they watch YouTube to learn how to implement new agricultural technology practices[2]. Such internet-based learning services act as supplementary training.

[1] According to the women's group in Chiangrai Province interviewed on 30 September 2022

[2] According to the women's group in Roet Province, interviewed on 1 October 2022


---
**Page 252**
---

acquisition to support what they hear during training and increases their understanding of the technology.

Both women and men are selected and trained as smart farmers and soil doctors by the sub- district agriculture officers. But, since they do not receive any support other than the initial training, farmers bring their soil samples to the center themselves (with a recommendation from the sub-district agriculture officer) to check the pH level. Rather than go to the soil doc- tors, if the farmer is unable to pay for the soil testing and analysis service, the government will provide farmers with an ideal mix of fertilizer. Contacting government offices is often done by women.

## Certification

The respondents during the field visits all noted that even when the farmers produce better quality rice, it is often not reflected in the market price. There is a small market for organic rice as a niche product for certain groups of buyers in the urban area. Some rice mills buy Sustain- able Rice Initiative (SRI) certified organic rice at a premium price. However, the respondents noted that even those farmers who were able to produce organic rice are selling their rice as SRI rice for the small bonus that the project provides for SRI rice instead of selling it as an organic rice unless they have certification from the Department of Agriculture Extension’s Planter’s Manual developed.

There are efforts by women’s group to develop such a premium rice market. Some women’s groups use the Good Agricultural Practices (GAP) certificate as a story for their products to appeal to environmentally conscious consumers[1].

## Income-generating activities of women

Since agriculture is a viable occupation, farmers prefer to diversify. This is especially the case for women farmers, who often serve both as the farm manager and the household’s financial manager. As is seen in the Department of Agriculture Extension’s Planter’s Manual, there are women’s groups that engage in income-generating activities such as poultry rearing, fish farming, and beekeeping[2]. There are active women’s groups that are engaged in joint income-generating activities, and more cohesive women’s groups that have established their own businesses. Some of these groups also make compost and bio-fertilizers using earthworms. They tend to operate on a small scale, so they produce only enough for backyard gardens. Women’s groups in Chiang Rai Province are engaged in the production of bio-fertilizer using earthworms[3]. If the worms, they would be able to increase the production of bio-fertilizer[4]. Currently, these activi- ties are not supported by any government or non-government organizations and is an extension of household work. Women are not generally considered entrepreneurs; however, they tend to be neglected for support to grow or expand. If these women producers were to be supported in their activities, it would be possible that they could have considerable potential for them to increase their incomes and be recognized as entrepreneurs.

[1] According to the women’s group in Roi-Prue District mentioned on October 2020

[2] According to the women’s group in Chiang Rai Province mentioned on October 2020.

[3] Good Agricultural Practices for Rice is a voluntary standard that certifies rice that has no bias in consumption with good qual- ity and safety. The Department of Agriculture Extension (2019) p. 54.

[4] The Action Plan of the Department of Agriculture Extension (2020/2021 and 2020/2027). See Section 3.2 of this report.


The women’s group in Chiang Rai Province is engaged in the production of bio-fertilizer using earthworms[5]. According to one of the women leaders interviewed on October (September) 2020, they buy 15 kg as of cow dung with 20-30 kg of earthworms. After one month, they obtain 80 kg of organic fertilizer which they can sell with 20 baht per kg.


---
**Page 253**
---

It is important to note that lower cost of production and diversity of income sources create a sense of security among women farmers, as noted by Chikwanda. She emphasized the importance of diversification in income generation.

Most women’s groups focus on income-generating activities. It is only when NGOs are present that they start to engage with other issues such as communal lands and basic rights (Shongwe et al., 2019 September 2020). Thus, none of the issues related to the environment and health, as well as new technologies, there is an untapped opportunity to involve women more strongly in climate smart agriculture.

### Women in leadership

The numbers of female heads of village or heads of farmers’ groups are rising. This also coincides with the fact that men are frequently busy working outside the village and with the fact that with stronger Sub-district Administrative Offices (that is in control of budgets), the heads of villages are slowly losing their political influence and resources. Regardless of the reasons, with more women being visible in official leadership positions, they serve as good role models.

Being in leadership positions is more difficult for women than men since women working for the community are often not recognized by the village headmen or other male leaders; otherwise, they will be exposed to criticism from both family and community members[1].

It is noteworthy that many of the strong women leaders in the villages were women who had gathered together as a group. They have been able to gain more influence within their village after some time. They display a more confident demeanor and are often more knowledgeable and capable than other women in the village, which makes them more influential[2].

These women also run for local elections and win seats. For example, in Swazimphung District in Nkhotakota, there is a woman who has been elected as a member of the district council. This has created changes in the way the sub-district utilizes the budget. These women were able to highlight the importance of women’s group activities and were able to allocate more budget for their projects[3]. It should be noted that this was not because they were women’s groups, but the fact that women were in leadership positions did make a difference.

### Aging farmers and young farmers

Youths tend to leave the village for education and work and the remaining smallholder farmer population is, on average, aging as a consequence. Because of mechanization, it is still possible for older farmers to farm their land but they are often not able to do so because of lack of help from young people. In some cases, young people are more interested in working with other young people asking family members, mostly parents and/or siblings, to look after their children while they seek employment elsewhere (Rungamire et al., 2021). This leads to an increase in female-headed households[4]. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) (2013) has reported that women-headed households commonly encounter difficulties both financially and in terms of labour availability.

There are some people who come back from the cities and start farming. These older returnee farmers tend to be more open to new technologies and can serve as good role models or
