# Conclusions for the project

- **Women play a prominent role in agriculture**, but they are perceived as unpaid family labour. It is important that gender-disaggregated data is properly collected and used so that women's contributions are correctly reflected in policies and plans.
- **Women play a prominent role in agriculture, but at the same time, they take almost the entire responsibility for care work at home**. It is important that this is recognized as an unpaid contribution to the household and community through sharing of household work as well as by promoting social services such as childcare services.

**Page 255**

- The female share of participants in agricultural training is high. It is helpful if there are follow-up supplementary learning materials available online. Noting that often women farmers like using online applications even more than men, such online materials will be useful for their learning.
- Women are interested in agricultural technologies but might have different priorities or interests than men. For example, they might be more interested in livestock and poultry production. It is therefore important to be sensitive to gender differences in the needs and interests of farmers and to address these through appropriate training programs. Encouraging and providing support to these returnee women to apply climate-smart technologies.
- Young women and men leave farming but when women have children or when their parents are old/ill, women tend to return to the rural villages. Such women who have been exposed to outside experience and knowledge can be a great asset for the village development. Encouraging and providing support to these returnee women to apply climate-smart technologies.
- Women play a prominent role in off-farm and non-farm income-generating activities. However, these activities are often seen as an extension of their household responsibilities rather than as a source of income. This recognition of women's activities leads to a lower position of women in society. It is therefore important that women who engage in such activities are treated as serious business, are given proper support and are provided with business opportunities. For example, women producers and women's groups should be involved in the project, notably in the context of bio-fertilizer production.
- Some women's groups sell their produce not on small markets but directly to processors or supermarkets. The premium rice market is still underdeveloped. There is a need to support the development of premium rice markets and link women's groups to access such markets.
- Female farmers are underrepresented in leadership roles, with men holding more positions than women. Women can make a difference in re-sourcing their farms by taking on leadership roles and assuming management positions. To ensure that gender equality efforts can be sustained in the project, it is important to provide training for women leaders.
- Women's groups tend to focus on income-generating activities. With external support and encouragement, they have the potential to play a key role as environmental and climate-smart technology advocates.
- Migrant workers are essential in agricultural production but it is noted that wages and benefits are paid late or not at all. It is important to ensure gender equality in wages among migrant workers.
- Young people frequently leave the community for education and then do not come back. It is important to introduce opportunities for young


---
**Page 256**
---

women and men to be inspired by climate-smart agricultural technologies so that they might be interested to take them up in the future. Supporting young people who have migrated out of their villages or return to their villages to start up agriculture could serve as a complementary approach to supporting climate-smart rice farming adoption.

# Chapter 4: Gender responsiveness and expertise, as well as gender equality in the partner organization(s)

## Rice Department (RD)

The RD of the Ministry of Agriculture and Cooperatives applies the national policies/strategies to promote gender equality, e.g., the Gender Equality Act, B.E. 2558 (2015), the 20-Year Na- tional Strategy, the National Economic and Social Development Plan and the Gender Equality Promotion Plan. However, these strategies are not always applied in practice for its own purposes, and staff members do not seem to be aware of who the Chief Gender Equal- ity Officer is or what his/her responsibilities are. The RD has not yet developed a clear awareness of gender equality in the rice sector nor is this commonly shared among staff mem- bers.

The Department of Women’s Affairs and Family Development (DWAFF) of the Ministry of So- cial Development and Human Security (MSDH) has been working on several national gender-specific points, including strengthening the mechanism of women’s development, ensuring that government policies are gender-sensitive, promoting women’s participation in decision-making processes at all levels, as well as allocating funds to research into gender needs and differences. With sup- port from DWAFF, the Rice Department can identify gender issues to focus on their programs, make sure that they are included in the project design, and develop a monitoring and evaluation framework for their monitoring and evaluation positions.

Since the involvement of women in rice production at the village level is high, the Rice Depart- ment should be encouraged to promote the role of women’s engagement in successful climate-smart agriculture, for learning and sharing with communities. It would also like to identify oppor- tunities for women to participate more actively in decision-making processes at all levels, especially at higher hierarchical levels above the village (sub-district, district, etc.).

It is also noted that the Rice Department is currently supporting the development of a Thai Agricultural Standard (TAS) on Sustainable Rice Production based on the global SGP standard. It is strongly recommended that the TAS standard adopts the SGP approach of in- cluding a gender equality indicator as an evaluation indicator in its monitoring and performance framework (see Section 3.3 of this report).

## Back for Agriculture and Agricultural Cooperatives (BAAC)

In its Sustainability Report 2020, BAAC has included the following orientation with regard to SDG 5 (gender equality):

While operating its business, BAAC has paid respect to human rights by upholding and prac- ticing the relevant principle of laws. The company’s policy in recruiting bank personnel makes sure that there is an equal chance of being recruited for both genders involved, and career paths are clearly developed together with human resource maintenance and develop- ment. BAAC also ensures that its business strategy is aligned to the SDGs, especially in order to deliver financial services and create sustainable value for all stakeholders. With respect to financial inclusion, BAAC has been providing financial services as clients; BAAC is also equally open to both men and women being accepted (BAAC, 2020).

In its Business Strategy for the fiscal year 2020, BAAC notes the importance of equipping its personnel with higher capabilities to deal with different generations, especially young farmers.


---
**Page 258**
---

It also emphasizes compliance and provides a grievance procedure for complaints and misconduct of employees, although there is no specific mention of sexual harassment. The committee and finance sub-committee have equal numbers of women and men (3 women out of 6 members).

While BAAC has a policy for inclusiveness and respect for human rights, there is no concrete gender balance in the board and management are male-dominated. According to its Annual Report 2021, the gender composition of management members is as below:

- Of the 15 Board members, 3 are women
- Of the 21 Management Committee members, all are men
- All of the 49 employees (including both employee and employee representatives) are male
- All of the 7 board members are men (of which 6 are board members)
- Of the Sub-Committee on the Development of Grassroots Economy and Social Envi-
   ronmental Responsibility members (8 in total, 7 men and one woman), the only woman is on the sub-committee.

Although the lack of women in management does not necessarily mean that BAAC's operation is gender-blind, there is room for more effort to be made to increase the number of women in higher management positions.

BAAC collects sex-disaggregated data on clients and service operation recipients, but such data is not made available to the public. The gender balance among clients seems to differ from one project to another. For example, in the Microfinance Project (MFP) for microfinance clients were male (as they were the household heads and the land was registered under their names), while women were more likely to be involved as borrowers or guarantors, and hence more women are contacting BAAC Preterminals® Women clients seem to be aware of their legal entitle-
ment that they are entitled to from the government through BAAC.

A number of BAAC projects support gender equality. For example, the Microfinance Linkage Project (MLP) aims at increasing access for rural women to credit and providing access to formal credit and supporting alternative income-generating activities such as small-scale vegetable produc-
tion. BAAC also provides training on financial literacy and business management skills to women on their own land, helping members earn income to improve their financial security while increas-
ing the economic empowerment of women in rural communities and contributing to rural communi-
ties. Half of the participants are women, and 90% of the committee members are women (The Government of Thailand Annual Review, 2014). However, other BAAC projects – such as the Land Ownership Project® - distribute land to men, largely because they are seen as more
therefore, not be responsive to the needs of rural women.

In order to make sure that BAAC is able to meet the needs of female clients, as well as en-
courage more women to participate in its projects and encourage members to engage with women and youths and reflect their needs in its operations. As seen in Section 3.3 of this report, BAAC
is one of the committee members of Thailand Women's Empowerment Fund. The experience in operating the Fund can be used to further develop its own women-focused programs.

Office of Natural Resources and Environmental Policy and Planning (ONEP)

ONEP is a public entity under the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MoNRE) and is responsible for the Environmental Fund Division (EFD) and serves as the focal point for international cooperation in environmental matters. According to ONEP’s 2019 Annual Report, 75% of its government officials, 72% of whom are women. More than 70% of those at the executive level are female. However, the report notes that “there is no gender-neutral approach in its recruitment and ca- reer growth policy with all levels,” (GIZ, 2021).

ONEP serves as the government’s focal point for climate change together with the Department of Women's Affairs and Family Development (DWAFD). Although ONEP has stated that it will adopt a gender equality approach in its work, according to the information coming out to DWAFD, its mission statement does not explicitly refer to gender. ONEP administers the national budget for the environment, which is part of the General Appropriation Bill (GAB) that is administered by ONEP does not apply gender criteria when making funding decisions (GIZ, 2021).

Since ONEP is an entity under MoNRE, it falls under the government’s national plans on gen- der equality in Thailand. ONEP is supposed to have its own gender focal point, which the DWAFD has been working with to develop a Gender Action Plan (GAP). However, the General Appropriation Bill (GAB) that governs the budget for the environment does not include provisions related to gender equality. But since the Thai government itself has a gender equality policy, ONEP is subject to its provisions.

International Rice Research Institute (IRRI)

IRRI, as a member of the Consultative Group on International Agricultural Research (CGIAR), has a strong commitment to gender equality and empowerment of women, as it believes that when women have equal access to resources they can contribute significantly to the sustainability of food systems. IRRI’s Gender Equality Strategy 2018-2023 is aligned with CGIAR’s Gender Equality Strategy 2018-2027.

IRRI regularly publishes research on gender topics in rice production and has become a re- search leader in gender and livelihoods. IRRI is an equally opportunity employer that values diversity and inclusion, and it is committed to promoting gender equality at all levels. Gender equality at IRRI overall is generally under the guidance of the executive team, which is com- posed of research, human resources, and communications.

IRRI implements CGIAR’s Gender Equality Strategy 2018-2027. Further, IRRI’s Code of Con- duct explicitly states the non-acception of discrimination on the basis of race, ethnicity, reli- gious belief, sex and gender. Currently, IRRI being part of the One CGIAR*, is bound by the One CGIAR Gender Equality Strategy 2018-2027, which is a key component of the overall em- phasis in CGIAR’s Workplan**. As at 30 September 2022, the data shows that IRRI employs

# Conclusions from the project

## Recommendations (RD)

- The Chief Gender Equality Officer (CGEO) does not seem to be widely known to staff members. Better awareness raising on the roles of the CGEO and his/her visibility is needed.
- There is no Gender Focal Point (GFP) in RD. It would help the functioning of the CGEO by having GFPs at the department, office and project levels.
- There is no RD Gender Action Plan. Having an action plan would help facilitate the gender equality efforts of the project.
- The RD follows the standard protocols and regulations of the Thai government on gender equality, discrimination and sexual harassment policy for RD which would highlight the importance of gender equality in the rice sector.
- Although women play a large role in agriculture, gender issues in Thailand are subtle and not easily identifiable. Training and awareness raising to identify gender issues is needed. The Gender Focal Points (GFPs) and the Gender Focal Officer of RD can help RD to monitor – and, ultimately, steer – gender equality issues in the rice sector.
- It is noted that RD includes women's empowerment indicators that are not yet fully operational to foster the status of women’s empowerment in the rice sector. Such good practices need to be continued in the Thai Agricultural Standard for Sustainable Rice (TAS) as well.

27


---
**Page 261**
---

BAAC:
- BAAC has more women clients than in the past. BAAC can strengthen its services to contribute to gender mainstreaming efforts by designating a GPF focal person. The GPF focal person can help with GPG management through developing a Gender Action Plan (GAP). GPG management can receive training to effectively guide the process and give clear direction on how to integrate gender equality into its services.
- As was discussed in the earlier section, women farmers tend to have different needs and different drivers of a sense of security than male farmers. Therefore, it is important that any GAP can fully capture female farmers' needs – in terms of both financial support (lending) as well as for insurance products.

IRRI:
- IRRI as an organization has declared a strong commitment to gender equality and operates a code of conduct for non-discrimination. Designing a Gender Action Plan (GAP) is one way that IRRI can effectively translate such organizational commitment to country operations.
- Although there is a clear commitment to the topic, there is currently no (IRRI) Thailand Country Office being managed for Thailand. An action plan would help guide in-country operations.

ONGP:
- ONEP has recognized the importance of gender perspectives and is one of the government's two GPFs for climate change in Thailand. However, it has not yet fully integrated gender equality into its organization, such as including gender equality in its mission, gender guidelines in EIA guidelines, or gender criteria in Environmental Fund disbursements.

# Chapter 5: Gender responsiveness and expertise in the project/among project staff in the country

## 5.1 Equal opportunities in the team

The project is still at the proposal stage, so the personnel selection of the team members is not yet concluded. According to GIZ’s Gender Strategy, the Human Resources Department monitors the gender balance within the project team and ensures that it corresponds with GIZ's Gender Strategy, on sexual harassment policy and the functioning of the Gender Working Group in GIZ Thailand.

Currently, the GIZ Thailand office is finalizing its own sexual harassment policy.

## 5.2 Gender expertise of the team members

GIZ Thailand office currently has a part-time GFP (10% of the total time allocated to work as GFP). In principle, all GIZ projects need to designate GFPs, but not all have yet done so. The country GFP and the project GFPS constitute the GIZ Gender Working Group and meet once a month. They are responsible for training as well as for monitoring and evaluating gender mainstreaming activities in their respective projects. There has been an effective forum for peer-to-peer learning.

GIZ Thailand ensures that all proposals include gender considerations and promote gender equality objectives. This is done through the need to be assisted by the country GFP against the GIZ Safeguards + Gender checklist. The country GFP provides recommendations on whether or not a project should be supported, as well as advice on how to integrate pro-jects developed by GIZ Thailand to date have been designated the level of G-0 for the gender marker (no gender equality objectives) and G-1 for projects with gender equality objectives). Some projects already have successful achievements in terms of gender equality, and this was attributed to including a gender-related indicator in the design of these pro-jects.

GIZ Thailand central office staff receive gender training from an external resource person. However, not all field officers receive such opportunities. Integrating gender in projects is still a new concept for many field officers who are currently introduced to the “reach-benefit-empower” framework of gender integration.

GIZ Thailand project staff need to work with their partners, and sometimes they face difficulty advocating for gender equality or integrating gender considerations into their partner’s quality policies or action plans. The first step that they are taking is to introduce GIZ's gender strategy and code of conduct to partners.

It seems that there is a certain level of gender awareness among GIZ Thailand staff, but there still is a lack of capacity in terms of operationalizing their gender knowledge into practice in project implementation and management. How to influence and guide partners to take up gender equality transformative approaches in project implementation and monitor-ing is an additional challenge.

4 According to the Gender Focus Point of GIZ, 3 November 2002
5 Such as the Sustainable and Climate Friendly Palm Oil Production and Procurement in Thailand project (see http://www.fair-governance-cooperation.org/en/).

**Page 263**
---

# Conclusions for the project

* During staff recruitment, gender balance among staff in general and in relation to specific positions needs to be kept in mind. Staff TORs need to include gender equality as a criterion when defining work tasks.
* A GFFP needs to be recruited for the Thai Rice Project. If more and more GIZ projects are to adopt gender integration seriously, a part-time overall GFFP would not be able to handle the workload and a full-time employee would be of benefit.
* Assign GFFPs for all projects. By having all projects participating in the Gender Working Group, peer-to-peer learning will be even more effective.
* Gender training for all levels of staff needs to be conducted. Regular gender training for all levels – from management to field staff – is needed so that all staff members are aware and able to exercise leadership in a gender-transformative approach. Project staff need specific training on how to integrate gender equality into their work, while communication training is needed by all staff to ensure the message of gender equality to national partners and to final beneficiaries.
* Develop gender monitoring indicators for all projects. This will facilitate project management to keep a constant focus on gender equality issues.
* Sharing gender experiences with other agencies and with other GIZ countries is also recommended so that the Gender Team can further develop gender-related practices and to promote the development of projects with strong gender dimensions.

# Introduction

A Gender Action Plan (GAP) forms the basis for gender mainstreaming throughout the project cycle. It is thus an operational planning as well as a Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) instrument that includes integration of the respective indicators and baseline information into the system. The GAP will also be discussed with project partners who are involved in the project's implementation.

The gender assessment of the Thai Rice Project has identified the following gender issues/points of importance to be addressed by the project:

- Monitoring of the gender equality situation is important to ensure that the project will not expand gender disparities and can contribute to gender equality and women’s empowerment.
- Gender-disaggregated data is lacking and needs to be collected whenever there is an opportunity.
- Gender equality perspectives need to be shared by all actors involved in the project.
- Gender equality largely stems from an unequal gender division of labour that places greater responsibilities for household and care work on women while also granting them fewer leadership roles.
- Certain groups of women are especially vulnerable and need special attention, including women migrant workers, female-headed households, female-headed single-parent families with children under 18 years old, elderly women living alone, women living with people with care needs, women with disabilities, and women self-identifying as belonging to an ethnic group.
- Women farmers have different knowledge and skills in different types of technologies. Technology promotion and dissemination strategies need to consider such gender differences.
- There are groups of women whose high potential for supporting climate-smart rice farming is not fully appreciated, including returning female farmers as well as young women.
- Financial and insurance services need to take into consideration women's needs.
- Women farmers need to be acknowledged as entrepreneurs and service providers and need to be provided with business support.
- Women farmers need access to market support for sustainable rice.

Some of the Project’s Executing Entities (EEs), namely the Rice Department (RD), the Bank for Agriculture and Agricultural Cooperatives (BAAC), the Office of Natural Resources and Environment (ONE), the National Institute of Horticultural and Herbal Science (NIHHS), and the International Rice Research Institute (IRRI), need to review – and, in some cases, improve or develop – their policies and practices to be gender-inclusive.

The project will directly support and facilitate women’s leadership in the community, nationally and regionally.
